Epistolae
ad Ethicam pertinentes

Letters
on the Ethics

Introduction to the Letters

Spinoza published nothing of the Ethics in his lifetime, but parts of it circulated among his friends, first as drafts and later as a nearly finished manuscript, and some of those who read it wrote to him with questions. His answers are the nearest thing we have to his own commentary on the book. The twenty-seven letters printed here are those in which he explains doctrines of the Ethics, or the views on which it rests, to readers who had found them difficult, together with the letters of his correspondents to which he replies point by point. They are numbered, as in the editions of Van Vloten and Land and of Gebhardt, in the order of their dates; the Opera Posthuma of 1677, which first printed them, used a different order and numbering, given in the notes.

The text

The Latin is that of Carl Gebhardt’s edition (Spinoza Opera, vol. IV, Heidelberg, 1925), whose page numbers are given in the Latin in the form [G IV 42]. Where Gebhardt prints both the text of the 1677 edition and a surviving original or copy, the 1677 text is followed, as for the Ethics, and the textual notes report what the manuscript adds; the one exception is Letter 8, whose original survives and of which Gebhardt prints only that. Passages that are found only in the Dutch version of 1677 (De Nagelate Schriften), which Gebhardt gives in Dutch, are left out of the Latin and translated in the notes. The orthography has been brought into line with that of the Latin of the Ethics in this edition: accents are dropped, j is printed as i, and capitals within a sentence are reduced, except in names. A few slips of the digital text used here have been corrected from the edition of Van Vloten and Land and from the 1677 edition, Greek words which it transliterates are restored from the 1677 pages, and two small drawings in Letter 9 and the figure of the two circles in Letter 12 are not reproduced.

As in the Ethics, numbered notes belong to the English and lettered notes to the Latin, and references to the Ethics are linked to the passages they cite.

Nine of the letters were written in Dutch: the exchange with Blyenbergh (Letters 18–24 and 27) and the letter to Jelles (Letter 50). The 1677 edition printed them in a Latin version, headed Versio, and the Dutch in the Dutch edition of the same year; Gebhardt prints both. Here, as for the other letters, the Latin of 1677 is the text translated, so that the English renders a translation; the notes give the Dutch where the two differ in sense, and translate the passages, such as the closing paragraphs of Letters 19 and 23, which only the Dutch has.

The translation follows the conventions of the translation of the Ethics, and renders Spinoza’s technical vocabulary in the same way. The letters of De Vries, Blyenbergh and Tschirnhaus are not always polished Latin; where they are awkward or incomplete, the English is too.

Simon de Vries, 1663 (Letters 8–10)

Simon Joosten de Vries, born around 1634 into an Amsterdam merchant family of Mennonite background, was one of the circle of friends whom Spinoza had known since the 1650s (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., pp. 166, 200). In 1663, while Spinoza was living at Rijnsburg, the circle met to read the drafts he sent them, taking turns to explain and demonstrate the propositions; Letter 8, of February 1663, describes these meetings and reports their difficulties with the definitions. What they were reading was an early version of Part I: De Vries quotes its third definition, which then defined substance and attribute together, and the scholium that became the scholium to Prop. 10. Spinoza’s reply, Letter 9, distinguishes a definition that must be true of its object from one that need only be conceivable, restates the definition of substance and attribute, and explains, with the examples of Israel and Jacob, how one thing can bear two names. Letter 10 answers two further questions, about the place of experience in knowing the attributes and about eternal truths.

Lodewijk Meyer, 1663 (Letter 12)

Letter 12, the ’letter on the infinite’, was written to Lodewijk Meyer, the Amsterdam physician who wrote the preface to Spinoza’s Principles of Descartes’s Philosophy in the same year and who did more than anyone to bring Spinoza’s writings into print (Nadler, pp. 202–203). It distinguishes three pairs of infinites that the philosophers had confused, explains the difference between eternity and duration, and argues that measure, time and number are aids of the imagination. Much of it reappears, sometimes word for word, in the scholium to Part I, Prop. 15, and its closing remarks on an argument of Hasdai Crescas bear on Part I, Prop. 28. Thirteen years later Tschirnhaus asked Spinoza to explain one of its examples (Letters 80 and 81).

Willem van Blyenbergh, 1664–1665 (Letters 18–24, 27)

Willem van Blyenbergh was a grain merchant and broker of Dordrecht with a taste for theology and philosophy, the author of a book of 1663 defending the knowledge of God and religion against the atheists (Nadler, pp. 251–252). In December 1664 he wrote to Spinoza, whom he did not know, after reading the Principles of Descartes’s Philosophy and its appendix, the Metaphysical Thoughts; it was all he knew of Spinoza’s work, and he could not always tell where Spinoza gave his own views and where Descartes’s. His question (Letter 18) was how, if God continually creates every thing and every motion of the will, there can be evil that God does not himself bring about. Spinoza’s reply (Letter 19), written in January 1665 at a farm near Schiedam where he was staying during the plague (Nadler, p. 251), sets out his account of evil: everything, considered in itself, has as much perfection as it has reality; imperfection appears only when things are compared, and the privation we ascribe to them belongs to our way of thinking, not to God’s intellect, the account given in the Preface to Part IV.

Blyenbergh’s answer (Letter 20) made clear that he held two rules of philosophy, the clear and distinct conceptions of the intellect and the revealed word of God, and that the second must prevail; Spinoza replied that they disagreed about first principles themselves, and explained the difference between privation and negation (Letter 21; Nadler, pp. 253–254). In Letter 23 he gives the example of Nero, whose matricide was outwardly the same act as that of Orestes, refuses to speak philosophically of God in the language of theology, and refers to his Ethics, ’not yet published’. After a visit, Blyenbergh asked for the definitions, postulates and axioms of the Ethics (Letter 24), and Spinoza declined (Letter 27). Nine years later Blyenbergh published a refutation of the Theological-Political Treatise (Nadler, p. 255).

Henry Oldenburg, 1665 (Letter 32)

Henry Oldenburg, born in Bremen about 1620, visited Spinoza at Rijnsburg in the summer of 1661 and began a correspondence that, after a long interruption, was renewed in the last years of Spinoza’s life; he was secretary of the Royal Society in London, and passed on to Spinoza the work of Robert Boyle (Nadler, pp. 213, 217, 225, 382–383). In Letter 32, of November 1665, Spinoza answers Oldenburg’s question how each part of nature agrees with its whole and coheres with the rest. He imagines a little worm living in the blood, which would take each particle for a whole, as we take the bodies around us, and could not know how the parts of the blood are governed by its nature as a whole; and he concludes that every body is a part of the whole of nature, and the human mind a part of an infinite intellect. The letter is the fullest statement outside the Ethics of the physics of Part II and of the doctrine of the corollary to Part II, Prop. 11.

Jarig Jelles, 1674 (Letter 50)

Jarig Jelles, an Amsterdam grocer in spices and dried fruit of Mennonite family, who had given up business for a life of religious reflection, was a close and lifelong friend of Spinoza’s (Nadler, pp. 198–199). Letter 50, dated 1674 in its Latin version, answers three of his questions: how Spinoza’s politics differs from Hobbes’s (he keeps natural right intact, so that the sovereign has right over its subjects only in proportion to its power); why God can be called one or unique only improperly, since we number things only after bringing them under a common kind, and God’s essence is not a kind; and why figure is a negation, since it is nothing but determination, and ’determination is negation’. It ends with Spinoza’s opinion of a refutation of the Theological-Political Treatise he had seen in a bookseller’s window.

Henry Oldenburg, 1675 (Letters 73–75)

After an interruption of several years, which followed his first, hostile reaction to the Theological-Political Treatise, Oldenburg wrote to Spinoza again in June 1675 (Nadler, pp. 382–383). In the autumn he told him which passages of the Treatise troubled its readers: those that seemed to confuse God with nature, to take away the authority of miracles, and to keep silent about Christ. Spinoza answered (Letter 73) that he held God to be the immanent, not the transitive, cause of all things (Part I, Prop. 18), but did not take God to be a mass of corporeal matter; that the certainty of revelation rests on the wisdom of its teaching, not on miracles, which are ignorance; and that it is not necessary for salvation to know Christ according to the flesh, but only the eternal wisdom of God, which showed itself most of all in Christ. Oldenburg replied (Letter 74) that readers thought Spinoza asserted a fatal necessity of all things, and asked about the raising of Lazarus and the Word made flesh; Spinoza’s answer (Letter 75) explains the necessity of all things by the way in which God understands himself necessarily and yet freely, and reads the resurrection as spiritual. Appendix D describes how Don Garrett’s case for reading Spinoza as a necessitarian starts from this exchange.

Tschirnhaus and Schuller, 1674–1676 (Letters 57–58, 63–66, 80–83)

Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus, born in 1651 into a noble German family, had studied law at Leiden, where he lodged with a medical student, Georg Hermann Schuller; by 1674 both were in Amsterdam, Schuller now a physician who knew Spinoza, and Tschirnhaus was allowed to take a manuscript of the Ethics with him when he left for London and Paris, on condition that he show it to no one (Nadler, pp. 260, 349). Schuller served as the go-between, and some of the letters pass through his hands. They raise some of the hardest questions the Ethics poses. Letter 57, on free will, prompted Spinoza’s Letter 58, with its account of freedom as free necessity and its image of a stone that, set in motion and conscious of its striving, would think itself free. In 1675 Schuller, writing for Tschirnhaus, asked whether we can know attributes other than thought and extension, and for examples of the infinite modes (Letter 63); Spinoza’s answer (Letter 64) names examples of them, which the Ethics does not give. Tschirnhaus then put the question why the mind, if each thing is expressed under infinitely many attributes, perceives only the body (Letter 65), and Spinoza’s short reply (Letter 66) is the starting point of the discussion of this question in Appendix C. The last exchange, of 1676, concerns whether the variety of bodies can be deduced from extension as Descartes conceived it, and whether more than one property can be deduced from a definition (Letters 80–83); Spinoza answers that matter is wrongly defined by extension, and promises to explain more clearly ’if life lasts’. He died seven months later.

Epistola VIII

Letter 8

Simon de Vries to Spinoza

Simon de Vries.
Simon de Vries
Amice Integerrime,
Most upright friend,
[G IV 38] Iamdudum exoptavi tibi semel adesse,a sed tempus, magnaque hyems non satis mihi propitia fuerunt. Aliquando de sorte mea queror, quod nobis intercedit spatium, quod nos ab invicem tam longe dividit.
For a long time I have wished to be with you once, but the season and the hard winter have not been kind enough to me. Sometimes I complain of my lot, that there lies between us a distance which separates us so far from one another.
  1. a The original of this letter survives, and Gebhardt prints its text (G IV 38–41), which is followed here. The version printed in the Opera Posthuma (OP pp. 459–461, Letter 26 of that edition) is shortened and revised: it leaves out De Vries’s complaint about the distance between them and the passage on Casearius, the remark that the group had begun to meet again, and the account of how it worked (Ep8.2, sentences 1–2), which Van Vloten and Land print in square brackets (VVL³ III 27).
Felix, imo Felicissimus tuus socius Casuarius sub eodem tecto remorans, qui inter prandendum, coenandum, ambulandumque tecum optimis de rebus sermones habere potest.
Happy, indeed most happy, is your companion Casearius,1 who lives under the same roof and can talk with you of the best things at dinner, at supper and on your walks.
  1. 1 Johannes Casearius, born in Amsterdam in 1642, a student of theology at Leiden, who moved into Spinoza’s house at Rijnsburg to be taught the philosophy of Descartes (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., pp. 231–233). Spinoza’s reply, in a passage the 1677 editors left out, warns De Vries not to envy him: see the note at the start of Letter 9.
Quamvis autem corpora ab invicem tam longe divisa sint, animo tamen saepissime praesens adfuisti meo, praesertim tuis in scriptis cum versor, manibusque tracto. Sed cum nobis collegis non omnia satis clara appareant, (ideoque iterum colegii initium fecimus) neque me tui immemorem esse putes, animum ad hasce litteras scribendas appuli.
But although our bodies are separated so far from one another, you have very often been present to my mind, especially when I am occupied with your writings and have them in my hands. But since not everything appears clear enough to us members of the group2 (and for this reason we have begun our meetings again), and so that you should not think me forgetful of you, I have set myself to write this letter.
  1. 2 collegis, collegium: the circle of Spinoza’s friends in Amsterdam who met to read and discuss the drafts of his philosophy that he sent them (Nadler, p. 237). What they were reading was an early version of Part I of the Ethics, whose numbering differs from the final text: see the notes on Ep8.3.
[G IV 39] Collegium quod attinet, eo instituitur modo: Unus (sed suae cuique vices) perlegit, pro suo conceptu explicat, porroque omnia demonstrat, secundum tuarum propositionum seriem, ac ordinem; tum si accidat, ut alter alteri satisfacere non possit, operae pretium esse duximus, illud annotare, atque ad te scribere, ut, si possibile, nobis clarius reddatur, et duce te contra superstitiose religiosos, Christianosque veritatem defendere, tum totius impetum mundi stare possimus. Quare, cum prius perlegentibus, explicantibusque, definitiones, non omnes nobis clarae videbantur, non idem de natura definitionis iudicium tulimus.
As for the group, it is arranged in this way. One of us (each in his turn) reads the text through, explains it as he understands it, and then demonstrates everything, following the sequence and order of your propositions. Then, if it happens that one cannot satisfy another, we have thought it worth while to note the point and write to you, so that, if possible, it may be made clearer to us, and so that under your guidance we may defend the truth against those who are superstitiously religious and Christian, and withstand the onslaught of the whole world. So, since not all the definitions seemed clear to us when we first read them through and explained them, we did not all form the same judgment about the nature of definition.
Interim te absente authorem quendam, mathematicum scil., qui Borellus vocatur, consuluimus; ille dum definitionis naturae, axiomatis, et postulati mentionem facit, atque aliorum de ea re sententias adfert.
Meanwhile, in your absence, we consulted an author, namely a mathematician called Borelli;1 he, while he mentions the nature of definition, of axiom and of postulate, and brings forward the opinions of others on the subject.
  1. 1 Giovanni Alfonso Borelli, author of Euclides restitutus (1658), as Van Vloten and Land note (VVL³ III 28).
Ipsius vero sententia sic sonat: Definitiones adhibentur in demonstratione, ut praemissae. Quare necesse est, ut sint evidenter cognitae, alias cognitio scientifica, seu evidentissima ex eis acquiri non potest. alio loco: Non temere, sed maxima cautione eligi debet ratio structurae, aut essentialis passio prima, et notissima alicuius subiecti. Nam si constructio, et passio nominata sit impossibilis, tunc non efficietur definitio scientifica, ut si quis diceret: Duae rectae lineae spatium comprehendentes, vocentur figurales, essent definitiones non entium, et impossibiles; et propterea potius ignorantia, quam scientia ex iis deduceretur. Deinde si constructio, aut passio nominata sit quidem possibilis et vera, sed nobis ignota, aut dubia; tunc bona definitio non erit: Nam conclusiones ab ignoto, et dubio ortae, incertae quoque, et dubiae erunt; et ideo suspisionem, aut opinionem; non autem scientiam certam afferent.
His own opinion runs thus: ‘Definitions are used in demonstration as premises. So it is necessary that they should be evidently known; otherwise scientific, or most evident, knowledge cannot be acquired from them.’ And in another place: ‘The principle of the construction, or the first and best-known essential property of a subject,2 must be chosen not rashly but with the greatest caution. For if the construction and the property named are impossible, then no scientific definition will be produced; as if someone were to say, Let two straight lines enclosing a space be called figurals: these would be definitions of non-beings, and impossible, and so ignorance rather than knowledge would be deduced from them. Next, if the construction or the property named is indeed possible and true, but unknown or doubtful to us, then the definition will not be a good one; for conclusions which arise from what is unknown and doubtful will also be uncertain and doubtful, and so will produce suspicion or opinion, but not certain knowledge.’
  1. 2 passio: in the scholastic sense of a property that belongs to a subject, not Spinoza’s ‘passion’.
A cuius opinione dissentire videtur Tacquet, qui autumat ex falso pronuntiato directe ad veram conclusionem procedi posse, uti tibi notum.
Tacquet seems to dissent from this opinion, who maintains, as you know, that one can proceed directly from a false statement to a true conclusion.3
  1. 3 André Tacquet’s Elementa geometriae planae ac solidae (Antwerp 1654) has an appendix demonstrating that a truth can be deduced directly from a falsehood (VVL³ III 28).
Clavius vero, cuius etiam sententiam introducit, sic sentit: Definitiones sunt artis vocabula, neque opus est, ut ratio afferatur, cur res aliqua hoc aut illo definiatur modo; sed satis esse, ut nunquam res definita asseratur alicui convenire, nisi prius definitionem traditam eidem convenire demonstretur. Ita ut Borellus velit, quod definitio alicuius subiecti debeat constare ex passione sive structura prima, essentiali, nobis notissima, et vera; Clavius non ita, sive prima, sive notissima, sive vera nec ne, nihil refert, modo ne definitionem, quam tradidimus, alicui convenire asseratur, nisi prius definitionem traditam eidem convenire demonstretur.
Clavius, however, whose opinion he also introduces, thinks thus: ‘Definitions are terms of art, and there is no need to give a reason why a thing is defined in this way or in that; it is enough that the thing defined should never be asserted to agree with anything unless it is first demonstrated that the definition given agrees with it.’ So Borelli holds that the definition of a subject must consist of a property, or construction, that is first, essential, best known to us, and true; Clavius not so: whether it is first, or best known, or true or not, makes no difference, provided that the definition we have given is not asserted to agree with anything unless it is first demonstrated that the definition given agrees with it.
Nos potius pedibus in sententiam Borelli iremus; utri dominus, an neutri horum concinnat, non bene scimus: Igitur, cum tales de definitionis natura, quae inter principia demonstrationis numeratur, disceptationes variae moventur, et, si animus non vindicatus est ab eius difficultatibus, etiam non de iis, quae de ea deducuntur; magnum nobis esset desiderium, ut dominatio vestra ad nos, multum nimis negotium tibi si non facessamus, perque otium tuum liceat, scriberet, quid ipsa de ea re sentiat, etiam quae est distinctio inter axiomata et definitiones; Borellus vero nullam veram admittit, nisi quoad nomen, sed te aliam ponere credo.
We would rather side with Borelli’s opinion; but which of the two you, Sir, agree with,4 or whether with neither, we do not well know. Since, therefore, such various disputes are raised about the nature of definition, which is counted among the principles of demonstration, and since, if the mind is not freed from its difficulties, it is not freed from those either which concern what is deduced from it, we should very much like you, Sir, to write to us, if we are not giving you too much trouble and your leisure allows, what you think of the matter, and also what the distinction is between axioms and definitions. Borelli admits no true distinction except as to the name; but I believe that you posit another.
  1. 4 dominus, dominatio vestra: De Vries uses the respectful third person, as in Dutch (U.E.), alongside the familiar ‘you’.
[G IV 40] Porro definitio 3tia nobis non satis constat, in exemplum attuli, quod dominus mihi dixit Hagaecomitis, scil.: quod res duobus modis potest considerari, vel prout in se est, vel prout respectum habet ad aliud, uti intellectus, vel enim potest considerari sub cogitatione, vel ut constans ideis. Sed, quaenam hic esset distinctio, non bene videmus; nam existimamus, quod nos, si cogitationem recte concipiamus, oporteat comprehendere sub ideis, quia remotis ab ea omnibus ideis cogitationem destrueremus. Qua de re, cum exemplum nobis satis clarum non est, manet adhuc res ipsa aliquo modo obscura, et ulteriore explicatione indigemus.
Further, the third definition is not clear enough to us.1 As an example I brought forward what you, Sir, told me at The Hague, namely that a thing can be considered in two ways, either as it is in itself, or as it has a relation to something else; as the intellect, for it can be considered either under thought or as consisting of ideas. But we do not well see what the distinction would be here; for we think that if we conceive thought rightly, we must comprehend it under ideas, since if all ideas were removed from it we would destroy thought. On this account, since the example is not clear enough to us, the matter itself still remains somewhat obscure, and we need a further explanation.
  1. 1 The draft’s third definition, which Spinoza quotes in his reply (Letter 9), defined substance and attribute together; the final text separates them as Part I, Defs. 3 and 4.
Novissime, in scholio 3tio prop. 8. habetur ab initio, sic scl: Ex his apparet, quod quamvis duo attributa, realiter distincta concipiantur (hoc est unum sine alterius ope) neque ideo ipsa duo entia sive duas diversas substantias constituunt; ratio est, quia de natura substantiae est, ut omnia eius attributa, unumquodque inquam, per se concipiatur, quandoquidem simul in ipsa fuerunt.
Lastly, at the beginning of the third scholium to Prop. 8 there is this: ‘From these things it is apparent that although two attributes are conceived as really distinct (that is, one without the aid of the other), they do not on that account constitute two beings, or two different substances; the reason is that it is of the nature of substance that all its attributes, each one, I say, should be conceived through itself, since they have been in it together.’2
  1. 2 In the final text this is the scholium to Part I, Prop. 10, with small changes of wording.
Sic dominus videtur supponere, substantiae naturam ita esse constitutam, ut plura possit habere attributa, quod nondum demonstravit, nisi quintam definitionem substantiae absolute infinitae, sive Dei respiceret; alias, si dicam, unamquamque substantiam tantum habere unum attributum, et mihi esset idea duorum attributorum, recte concludere possem, quod ubi duo diversa attributa, ibi duae diversae sint substantiae: in quod etiam clariorem explicationem te rogemus.
So you seem to suppose that the nature of substance is so constituted that it can have several attributes, which you have not yet demonstrated, unless you are referring to the fifth definition,3 of absolutely infinite substance, or God. Otherwise, if I were to say that each substance has only one attribute, and I had the idea of two attributes, I could rightly conclude that where there are two different attributes, there are two different substances. On this too we ask you for a clearer explanation.
  1. 3 In the final text the definition of God is Part I, Def. 6.
[G IV 41] Porro gratias ago maximas pro tuis scriptis, mihi a P. Balling communicatis; quae magno me gaudio affecerunt. Sed potissimum scholium prop. 19.; si hic et tibi, in aliquo, quod in mea potestate sit, servire possim, ibi tibi sum, tantum opus, ut me scientem facias. Ego collegium annotomiae inivi, medium fere absolvi, absoluto, incipiam chimicum, sicque suasore te percurram totam medicinam.
Further, I thank you very much for your writings, which P. Balling1 passed on to me, and which delighted me greatly. But above all the scholium to Prop. 19;2 if here too I can serve you in anything that is in my power, I am at your service; you have only to let me know. I have begun a course in anatomy and am nearly halfway through it; when it is finished I shall begin chemistry, and so, on your advice, I shall go through the whole of medicine.
  1. 1 Pieter Balling, one of Spinoza’s close friends among the Amsterdam Collegiants (Nadler, p. 166).
  2. 2 Again the numbering of the draft; which scholium of the final text it became cannot be told from this mention.
Desino, et exspecto responsionem, et a me sis salutatus, qui sum
I stop, and await your reply; and receive my greetings, as from one who is
Tibi addictissimus S. I. de Vries.
Yours most devotedly, S. J. de Vries.
Datum Amstelodami die 24 februarii 1663.
Given at Amsterdam, 24 February 1663.

Epistola IX

Letter 9

Spinoza to Simon de Vries

Doctissimo Iuveni
To the most learned young man
Simoni de Vries
Simon de Vries
Amice colende,
Esteemed friend,
[G IV 42] Quod ad quaestiones a te propositas attinet.a Video te iis haerere, propterea quod non distinguis inter genera definitionum. Nempe inter definitionem, quae inservit ad rem, cuius essentia tantum quaeritur, et de qua sola dubitatur, explicandam, et inter definitionem, quae ut solum examinetur, proponitur. Illa enim, quia determinatum habet obiectum, vera debet esse; haec vero id non requirit.
As for the questions you have put to me.1 I see that you are held up by them because you do not distinguish between the kinds of definition: namely between a definition which serves to explain a thing whose essence alone is sought, and about which alone there is doubt, and a definition which is proposed only in order to be examined. For the former, because it has a determinate object, must be true; the latter does not require this.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP, Letter 27), as Gebhardt prints it first (G IV 42–46). The original, which he prints after it, addresses the whole group in the plural where the printed text has the singular (dicitis, noluistis, adfertis, proni estis for dicis, noluisti, adfers, pronius es), and has the opening passage translated in the note to the English.
  1. 1 The original letter survives (G IV 42–46, second text) and opens with a passage the 1677 editors left out: ‘I have received your long-awaited letter, for which, and for your affection towards me, I thank you very much. Your long absence has been no less irksome to me than to you; meanwhile I am glad that my little night-labours are of use to you and our friends, for in this way I speak to you while you are away. There is no reason for you to envy Casearius. For no one is more tiresome to me, nor is there anyone of whom I have taken more care to beware; so I should like you and all our acquaintances to be warned not to communicate my opinions to him until he has reached a more mature age. He is still too much a boy, too little constant to himself, and more eager for novelty than for truth. But I hope that he will correct these childish faults in a few years; indeed, as far as I can judge from his character, I am almost certain of it. So his nature bids me love him’ (VVL³ III 31). On Casearius see Letter 8.
Ex. gr. si quis templi Solomonis descriptionem me roget, ipsi veram templi descriptionem tradere debeo, nisi cum ipso cupiam garrire. Sed si ego templum aliquod in mente concinnavi, quod aedificare cupio: ex cuius descriptione concludo me talem fundum, totque millia lapidum, aliorumque materialium emere debere, aliquisne sanae mentis mihi dicet, me male conclusisse, ex eo quod forte falsam adhibui definitionem? vel an aliquis a me exiget, ut meam definitionem probem? Is sane nihil aliud mihi dicit, quam quod id, quod conceperam, non conceperim, vel a me exiget, ut id, quod conceperam, probem, me concepisse, quod sane est nugari.
For example, if someone asks me for a description of Solomon’s temple, I must give him a true description of the temple, unless I want to talk nonsense with him. But if I have devised in my mind some temple which I want to build, and conclude from its description that I must buy such a piece of ground and so many thousand stones and other materials, will anyone of sound mind tell me that I have concluded badly because I have perhaps used a false definition? Or will anyone require me to prove my definition? He would certainly be telling me nothing other than that I have not conceived what I had conceived, or requiring me to prove that I have conceived what I had conceived; which is certainly to trifle.
Quare definitio vel explicat rem, prout est extra intellectum, et tum vera debet esse, et a propositione, vel axiomate non differre, nisi quod illa tantum circa rerum, rerumve affectionum essentias versatur; hoc vero latius, nempe ad aeternas veritates etiam se extendit: Vel explicat rem, prout a nobis concipitur, vel concipi potest, tumque in eo etiam differt ab axiomate et propositione, quod non exigit, nisi ut concipiatur absolute, non ut axioma sub ratione veri. Quare mala definitio illa est, quae non concipitur.
So a definition either explains a thing as it is outside the intellect, and then it must be true, and must not differ from a proposition, or axiom, except in that the former is concerned only with the essences of things, or of the affections of things, whereas the latter extends more widely, namely to eternal truths as well; or it explains a thing as it is conceived by us, or can be conceived, and then it differs from an axiom and a proposition in this too, that it requires only that it be conceived absolutely, not, like an axiom, under the aspect of the true. So a bad definition is one which is not conceived.
Quod ut intelligatur, Borelli exemplum capiam. Nempe si quis diceret, duae rectae lineae, spatium comprehendentes, vocentur figurales. Si is per lineam rectam, intelligit id, quod omnes per lineam curvam, tum bona est definitio, (per illam enim definitionem intelligeretur figura, ut [figurae], aut similesb) modo quadrata, et alias postea non intelligat figuras. Verum si per lineam rectam intelligat id, quod communiter intelligimus, res est plane inconceptibilis, ideoque nulla est definitio. Quae omnia a Borello, in cuius sententia amplectenda pronius es, plane confunduntur.
To make this understood, I shall take Borelli’s example: namely, if someone were to say, Let two straight lines enclosing a space be called figurals. If by a straight line he understands what everyone understands by a curved line, then the definition is good (for by that definition would be understood figures such as [figures], or similar ones), provided that he does not afterwards understand by it squares and other figures. But if by a straight line he understands what we commonly understand, the thing is entirely inconceivable, and so it is no definition. All these things are completely confused by Borelli, to whose opinion you are more inclined.
  1. b Spinoza draws two small figures here (G IV 44), which are not reproduced.
Aliud exemplum addo, id nempe, quod in fine adfers. Si dicam unamquamque substantiam unum tantum habere attributum, mera est propositio, et eget demonstratione. Si vero dicam per substantiam intelligo id, quod uno tantum attributo constat, bona erit definitio, modo postea entia pluribus attributis constantia alio nomine a substantia diverso insigniantur.
I add another example, namely the one you bring forward at the end. If I say that each substance has only one attribute, it is a mere proposition, and needs demonstration. But if I say, By substance I understand that which consists of only one attribute, it will be a good definition, provided that afterwards beings consisting of several attributes are designated by another name, different from substance.
Quod autem dicis, me non demonstrare substantiam (sive ens) plura habere posse attributa, forte ad demonstrationes noluisti attendere. Duas enim adhibui, prima quod nihil nobis evidentius, quam quod unumquodque ens sub aliquo attributo a nobis concipiatur, et quo plus realitatis, aut esse aliquod ens habet, eo plura attributa ei sunt tribuenda. Unde ens absolute infinitum definiendum, etc. Secunda, et quam ego palmariam iudico, est, quod quo plura attributa alicui enti tribuo, eo magis cogor, ipsi existentiam tribuere, hoc est, eo magis sub ratione veri ipsum concipio, quod plane contrarium esset, si ego chymaeram, aut quid simile finxissem.
As for your saying that I do not demonstrate that substance (or being) can have several attributes, perhaps you did not want to attend to the demonstrations. For I used two. The first: that nothing is more evident to us than that each being is conceived by us under some attribute, and that the more reality, or being, a being has, the more attributes must be attributed to it.2 Hence a being absolutely infinite must be defined, etc. The second, which I judge the chief one,3 is that the more attributes I attribute to a being, the more I am compelled to attribute existence to it, that is, the more I conceive it under the aspect of the true; which would be quite the contrary if I had feigned a chimera or something similar.
  1. 2 In the final text: Part I, Prop. 9 and the scholium to Prop. 10, ‘nothing in nature is clearer than that each being must be conceived under some attribute’.
  2. 3 Compare the scholium to Part I, Prop. 11: ‘the more reality belongs to the nature of a thing, the more powers it has, of itself, to exist’.
Quod autem dicis, te non concipere cogitationem, nisi sub ideis, quia remotis ideis cogitationem destruis, credo id tibi contingere propterea, quod dum tu, res scilicet cogitans, id facis, omnes tuas cogitationes et conceptus seponis. Quare non mirum est, quod ubi omnes tuas cogitationes seposuisti, nihil postea tibi cogitandum supersit.
As for your saying that you do not conceive thought except under ideas, because when the ideas are removed you destroy thought, I believe this happens to you because, while you, a thinking thing, do this, you set aside all your thoughts and concepts. So it is no wonder that when you have set aside all your thoughts, nothing afterwards remains for you to think.
Quod autem ad rem attinet, puto me satis clare, et evidenter demonstrasse, intellectum, quamvis infinitum, ad naturam naturatam, non vero ad naturantem pertinere. Porro quid hoc ad tertiam definitionem intelligendum faciat, nondum video, nec etiam cur ea moram iniiciat.
As for the matter itself, I think I have demonstrated clearly and evidently enough that the intellect, even though infinite, pertains to Natura naturata, not to Natura naturans.4 Further, I do not yet see what this has to do with understanding the third definition, nor why it should hold you up.
  1. 4 Part I, Prop. 31, with the distinction drawn in the scholium to Prop. 29.
[G IV 46] Ipsa enim definitio, ut ipsam, ni fallor, tibi tradidi, sic sonat: Per substantiam intelligo id, quod in se est, et per se concipitur, hoc est, cuius conceptus non involvit conceptum alterius rei. Idem per attributum intelligo, nisi quod attributum dicatur respectu intellectus, substantiae certam talem naturam tribuentis. Haec, inquam, definitio satis clare, quid per substantiam, sive attributum intelligere volo, explicat.
For the definition itself, as I gave it to you, if I am not mistaken, runs thus: ‘By substance I understand that which is in itself and is conceived through itself, that is, whose concept does not involve the concept of another thing. By attribute I understand the same, except that it is called attribute in relation to the intellect, which attributes such a definite nature to substance.’1 This definition, I say, explains clearly enough what I want to understand by substance, or attribute.
  1. 1 The draft’s third definition. The final text gives substance and attribute separate definitions (Part I, Defs. 3 and 4), and defines attribute as ‘that which the intellect perceives of a substance as constituting its essence’; the relation to the intellect remains. Appendix B discusses how this relation has been read.
Vis tamen, quod minime opus est, ut exemplo explicem, quomodo una, eademque res duobus nominibus insigniri possit. Sed ne parcus videar duo adhibebo.
You want me nevertheless, though there is no need, to explain by an example how one and the same thing can be designated by two names. But so as not to seem stingy, I shall give two.
Primo dico per Israelem intelligi tertium patriarcham, idem per Iacobum intelligo, quod nomen Iacobi ipsi imponebatur propterea, quod calcem fratris apprehenderat. Secundo per planum intelligo id, quod omnes radios lucis sine ulla mutatione reflectit, idem per album intelligo, nisi quod album dicatur respectu hominis planum intuentis. etc.a
First, I say that by Israel is understood the third patriarch; I understand the same by Jacob, the name Jacob having been given to him because he had seized his brother’s heel.2 Second, by plane I understand that which reflects all rays of light without any change; I understand the same by white, except that it is called white in relation to a man looking at the plane, etc.
  1. a The Dutch version of 1677 ends the letter with a sentence the Latin lacks (G IV 46): ‘I think that with this I have answered your questions. Meanwhile I shall wait to hear your judgment; and if there is anything that you think is not demonstrated well or clearly enough, do not hesitate to point it out to me, etc.’
  1. 2 Genesis 25:26: וְיָדוֹ אֹחֶזֶת בַּעֲקֵב עֵשָׂו וַיִּקְרָא שְׁמוֹ יַעֲקֹב (‘and his hand took hold of Esau’s heel, and his name was called Jacob’), the name יַעֲקֹב (‘Jacob’) being heard in עָקֵב (‘heel’). The second name is given in Genesis 32:29. (Verses as numbered in the Hebrew; the Vulgate numbers them 25:25 and 32:28.)

Epistola X

Letter 10

Spinoza to Simon de Vries

Doctissimo Iuveni,
To the most learned young man
Simoni de Vries
Simon de Vries
Amice colende,
Esteemed friend,
[G IV 47] Petis a me, an egeamus experientia ad sciendum, utrum definitio alicuius attributi sit vera? Ad hoc respondeo, nos nunquam egere experientia, nisi ad illa, quae ex rei definitione non possunt concludi, ut, ex. gr. existentia modorum: haec enim a rei definitione non potest concludi. Non vero ad illa, quorum existentia ab eorundem essentia non distinguitur, ac proinde ab eorum definitione concluditur.
You ask me whether we need experience to know whether the definition of some attribute is true. To this I answer that we never need experience except for those things which cannot be inferred from the definition of the thing, as, for example, the existence of modes;1 for this cannot be inferred from the definition of the thing. But not for those things whose existence is not distinguished from their essence, and is therefore inferred from their definition.
  1. 1 Compare Part I, Prop. 24: the essence of things produced by God does not involve existence.
Imo nulla experientia id unquam nos edocere poterit: nam experientia nullas rerum essentias docet; sed summum, quod efficere potest, est mentem nostram determinare, ut circa certas tantum rerum essentias cogitet. Quare, cum existentia attributorum ab eorum essentia non differat, eam nulla experientia poterimus assequi.
Indeed no experience will ever be able to teach us this; for experience teaches no essences of things, but the most it can do is to determine our mind to think only about certain essences of things. So, since the existence of the attributes does not differ from their essence,2 we shall not be able to attain it by any experience.
  1. 2 Part I, Prop. 20 and its demonstration: the same attributes of God which explain his eternal essence explain at the same time his eternal existence.
Quod porro petis, anne res etiam, rerumve affectiones sint aeternae veritates? Dico omnino.
As for your further question, whether things too, or the affections of things, are eternal truths: I say, certainly.
Si regeris, cur eas aeternas veritates non voco? respondeo, ut eas distinguam, uti omnes solent, ab iis, quae nullam rem, reive affectionem explicant, ut, ex. gr. a nihilo nihil fit; haec, inquam, similesque propositiones vocantur absolute aeternae veritates, sub quo nihil aliud significare volunt, quam quod talia nullam sedem habent extra mentem etc.a
If you reply, why then do I not call them eternal truths? I answer: to distinguish them, as everyone usually does, from those which explain no thing, or affection of a thing, as, for example, nothing comes from nothing; these and similar propositions, I say, are called eternal truths absolutely, by which they mean nothing else than that such things have no seat outside the mind, etc.1
  1. a The letter survives only in the 1677 edition (OP p. 464, Letter 28), without a date. The questions it answers are not those of Letter 8; De Vries’s letter that put them is lost.
  1. 1 Descartes’s example in the Principles of Philosophy I.49: the proposition ex nihilo nihil fit is considered ‘not as some existing thing, nor as a mode of a thing, but as an eternal truth which has its seat in our mind’, and is called a common notion, or axiom (AT VIII-1 23). Compare the eternal truth of the existence of substance in Part I, Prop. 8, Schol. 2.

Epistola XII

Letter 12

Spinoza to Lodewijk Meyer

Viro Doctissimo, atque Expertissimo,
To the most learned and expert man
Ludovico Meyer P. M. Q. D.
Lodewijk Meyer, Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine
Amice singularis,
My special friend,
[G IV 52] Duas abs te accepi epistolas,a unam die 11 Ianuarii datam, et ab amico N.N. mihi traditam; alteram vero 26 die Martii, et ab amico, nescio quo, Leida missam. Pergratae mihi ambae fuerunt; praecipue, ubi ex iis, omnia tua optime se habere, teque mei saepe memorem intellexi. Porro pro tua erga me humanitate, et honore, quo semper me dignatus es afficere, maximas, quas debeo, ago gratias; simulque precor, ut me tibi non minus addictum credas, quod semper data occasione, quantum mea ferre poterit tenuitas, ostendere conabor. Atque hoc ut incipiam, ad id, quod in tuis epistolis ex me quaeris, respondere curabo.
I have received two letters from you,1 one dated 11 January and handed to me by our friend N.N., the other dated 26 March and sent from Leiden by some friend, I do not know whom. Both were very welcome to me, especially since I learned from them that all is well with you and that you often think of me. Further, I give you the greatest thanks I owe for the kindness and honour which you have always deigned to show me; and at the same time I beg you to believe that I am no less devoted to you, which, whenever the opportunity is given, I shall try to show, as far as my slender abilities allow. And to make a beginning, I shall see to answering what you ask of me in your letters.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP, Letter 29), as Gebhardt prints it first (G IV 52–62); he also prints a manuscript copy, which begins only at ‘Quaestio de infinito’ and differs in a number of readings. The date of the letter is given only in the Dutch version: ‘Rijnsburg, 20 April 1663’.
  1. 1 Lodewijk Meyer, born in Amsterdam in 1629, who took doctorates in philosophy and in medicine at Leiden in 1660 (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., pp. 202–203); hence the superscription’s P. M. Q. D., Philosophiae Medicinaeque Doctori. He wrote the preface to Spinoza’s Principles of Descartes’s Philosophy (1663), and was, more than anyone, responsible for bringing Spinoza’s writings to publication, in his lifetime and after his death (Nadler, pp. 202, 233).
Petis autem, ut, quae de infinito excogitata habeam, tibi communicem, quod libentissime faciam. Quaestio de infinito omnibus semper difficillima, imo inextricabilis visa fuit, propterea quod non distinxerunt inter id, quod sua natura, sive vi suae definitionis sequitur esse infinitum; et id, quod nullos fines habet, non quidem vi suae essentiae; sed vi suae causae. Ac etiam, quia non distinxerunt inter id, quod infinitum dicitur, quia nullos habet fines; et id, cuius partes, quamvis eius maximum et minimum habeamus; nullo tamen numero adaequare, et explicare possumus. Denique quia non distinxerunt inter id, quod solummodo intelligere, non vero imaginari; et inter id, quod etiam imaginari possumus.
You ask, then, that I communicate to you what I have thought out concerning the infinite, which I shall most gladly do. The question of the infinite has always seemed to everyone most difficult, indeed inextricable, because they have not distinguished between that which follows to be infinite by its own nature, or by the force of its definition, and that which has no limits, not indeed by the force of its essence, but by the force of its cause.2 And also because they have not distinguished between that which is called infinite because it has no limits, and that whose parts, though we have its maximum and minimum, we cannot equal or explain by any number. And finally because they have not distinguished between that which we can only understand, but not imagine, and that which we can also imagine.
  1. 2 The first kind is substance (Part I, Prop. 8); the second, the infinite modes, which follow from the absolute nature of an attribute and are infinite by the force of their cause (Part I, Props. 21–23). The three distinctions are taken up again at the end of the letter (Ep12.7).
Ad haec, inquam, si attendissent, nunquam tam ingenti difficultatum turba obruti fuissent. Clare enim tum intellexissent, quale infinitum in nullas partes dividi, seu nullas partes habere potest; quale vero contra, idque sine contradictione. Porro etiam intellexissent, quale infinitum maius alio infinito sine ulla implicantia; quale vero non item concipi potest; quod ex mox dicendis clare apparebit.
If they had attended to these things, I say, they would never have been overwhelmed by so great a crowd of difficulties. For they would then have understood clearly what kind of infinite cannot be divided into any parts, or can have no parts, and what kind, on the contrary, can, and that without contradiction. Further, they would also have understood what kind of infinite can be conceived greater than another infinite without any contradiction, and what kind cannot; as will appear clearly from what I am about to say.
Verum prius haec quatuor paucis exponam, videlicet substantiam, modum, aeternitatem, et durationem. Quae circa substantiam considerari velim, sunt.
But first I shall briefly explain these four: namely substance, mode, eternity and duration. What I should like to be considered concerning substance is this.
Primo, quod ad eius essentiam pertinet existentia, hoc est, quod ex sola eius essentia, et definitione sequatur eam existere; quod, nisi me mea fallit memoria, antehac tibi viva voce absque ope aliarum propositionum demonstravi. Secundum, et quod ex hoc primo sequitur, est, quod substantia non multiplex; sed unica duntaxat eiusdem naturae existat. Tertium denique, quod omnis substantia non nisi infinita possit intelligi.
First, that existence pertains to its essence, that is, that it follows from its essence and definition alone that it exists; which, if my memory does not deceive me, I demonstrated to you earlier by word of mouth, without the help of other propositions. Second, and following from the first, that substance does not exist as many, but only as one of the same nature. Third, finally, that every substance can only be understood as infinite.3
  1. 3 In the Ethics: Part I, Prop. 7, that existence pertains to the nature of substance; Prop. 5 and the second scholium to Prop. 8, that there is only one substance of the same nature; Prop. 8, that every substance is necessarily infinite.
Substantiae vero affectiones modos voco, quorum definitio, quatenus non est ipsa substantiae definitio, nullam existentiam involvere potest. Quapropter, quamvis existant, eos ut non existentes concipere possumus: ex quo porro sequitur, nos, ubi ad solam modorum essentiam; non vero ad ordinem totius naturae attendimus, non posse concludere ex eo, quod iam existant, ipsos postea exstituros, aut non exstituros, vel antea exstitisse, aut non exstitisse. Unde clare apparet, nos existentiam substantiae toto genere a modorum existentia diversam concipere.
But the affections of substance I call modes, whose definition, insofar as it is not the very definition of substance, can involve no existence.4 So, even though they exist, we can conceive them as not existing; from which it further follows that, when we attend only to the essence of modes and not to the order of the whole of nature, we cannot conclude from their existing now that they will exist afterwards or will not exist, or that they existed before or did not exist. From this it is clearly apparent that we conceive the existence of substance as entirely different in kind from the existence of modes.
  1. 4 Part I, Def. 5 and Prop. 24; compare Part I, Ax. 7.
Ex quo oritur differentia inter aeternitatem, et durationem; per durationem enim modorum tantum existentiam explicare possumus; substantiae vero per aeternitatem, hoc est, infinitam existendi, sive, invita latinitate, essendi fruitionem.
From this arises the difference between eternity and duration; for by duration we can explain only the existence of modes, but that of substance by eternity, that is, the infinite enjoyment of existing, or, in defiance of Latinity, of being.5
  1. 5 essendi, the gerund of esse, which classical Latin does not use, hence the apology. On eternity and duration in the Ethics, see Part I, Def. 8 with its explanation and Part II, Def. 5.
[G IV 55] Ex quibus omnibus clare constat, nos modorum existentiam et durationem, ubi, ut saepissime fit, ad solam eorum essentiam; non vero ad ordinem naturae attendimus, ad libitum, et quidem propterea nullatenus, quem eorum habemus conceptum, destruendo, determinare, maiorem minoremque concipere, atque in partes dividere posse: Aeternitatem vero, et substantiam, quandoquidem non nisi infinitae concipi possunt, nihil eorum pati posse; nisi simul eorum conceptum destruamus.
From all this it is clearly established that when, as very often happens, we attend only to the essence of modes and not to the order of nature, we can determine at will the existence and duration of modes, conceive it as greater or less, and divide it into parts, without thereby in any way destroying the concept we have of them; but that eternity and substance, since they can only be conceived as infinite, can undergo none of these things unless we destroy their concept at the same time.
Quare ii prorsus garriunt, ne dicam insaniunt, qui substantiam extensam ex partibus, sive corporibus ab invicem realiter distinctis conflatam esse putant. Perinde enim est, ac si quis ex sola additione, et coacervatione multorum circulorum quadratum, aut triangulum, aut quid aliud, tota essentia diversum, conflare studeat.
So those who think that extended substance is put together from parts, or bodies really distinct from one another, are talking utter nonsense, not to say raving. For it is just as if someone were to try, by the mere addition and heaping up of many circles, to put together a square, or a triangle, or something else entirely different in its whole essence.
Quare omnis illa farrago argumentorum, quibus substantiam extensam finitam esse ostendere philosophi vulgo moliuntur, sua sponte ruit: Omnia enim illa substantiam corpoream ex partibus conflatam supponunt. Ad eundem etiam modum alii, qui postquam sibi persuaserunt, lineam ex punctis componi, multa invenire potuerunt argumenta, quibus ostenderent lineam non esse in infinitum divisibilem.
So that whole jumble of arguments by which philosophers commonly strive to show that extended substance is finite collapses of its own accord;1 for all of them suppose that corporeal substance is put together from parts. In the same way others too, having persuaded themselves that a line is composed of points, have been able to find many arguments to show that a line is not divisible to infinity.
  1. 1 The arguments, and this reply to them, are set out in the scholium to Part I, Prop. 15, which also uses the comparison with a line composed of points.
[G IV 56] Si tamen quaeras, cur naturae impulsu adeo propensi simus ad dividendam substantiam extensam: ad id respondeo, quod quantitas duobus modis a nobis concipiatur; abstracte scilicet, sive superficialiter, prout ope sensuum eam in imaginatione habemus; vel ut substantia, quod non nisi a solo intellectu fit. Itaque si ad quantitatem, prout est in imaginatione, attendimus, quod saepissime, et facilius fit, ea divisibilis, finita, ex partibus composita, et multiplex reperietur. Sin ad eandem, prout est in intellectu, attendamus, et res, ut in se est, percipiatur, quod difficillime fit, tum, ut satis antehac tibi demonstravi, infinita, indivisibilis, et unica reperietur.
If, however, you ask why we are so inclined by the impulse of nature to divide extended substance, I reply that quantity is conceived by us in two ways: namely abstractly, or superficially, as we have it in the imagination with the help of the senses; or as substance, which is done only by the intellect. So if we attend to quantity as it is in the imagination, which is done very often and more easily, it will be found divisible, finite, composed of parts, and many. But if we attend to it as it is in the intellect, and the thing is perceived as it is in itself, which is done with great difficulty, then, as I have sufficiently demonstrated to you before, it will be found infinite, indivisible, and unique.1
  1. 1 Almost word for word in the scholium to Part I, Prop. 15.
Porro ex eo, quod durationem, et quantitatem pro libitu determinare possumus, ubi scilicet hanc a substantia abstractam concipimus, et illam a modo, quo a rebus aeternis fluit, separamus, oritur tempus, et mensura; tempus nempe ad durationem; mensura ad quantitatem tali modo determinandam, ut, quoad fieri potest, eas facile imaginemur.
Further, from the fact that we can determine duration and quantity at our pleasure, namely when we conceive the latter abstracted from substance and separate the former from the way in which it flows from eternal things, there arise time and measure: time, namely, for determining duration, and measure for determining quantity, in such a way that we may imagine them easily, as far as can be done.
Deinde ex eo, quod affectiones substantiae ab ipsa substantia separamus, et ad classes, ut eas quoad fieri potest, facile imaginemur, redigimus, oritur numerus, quo ipsas determinamus. Ex quibus clare videre est, mensuram, tempus, et numerum nihil esse praeter cogitandi, seu potius imaginandi modos.
Next, from the fact that we separate the affections of substance from substance itself and reduce them to classes, so that we may imagine them easily, as far as can be done, there arises number, by which we determine them. From these things it can be clearly seen that measure, time and number are nothing but modes of thinking, or rather of imagining.1
  1. 1 Compare the account of how we imagine time in the scholium to Part II, Prop. 44, and of universals and transcendental terms in the first scholium to Part II, Prop. 40.
Quare non mirum est, quod omnes, qui similibus notionibus, et quidem praeterea male intellectis, progressum naturae intelligere conati sunt, adeo mirifice se intricarint, ut tandem se extricare nequiverint, nisi omnia perrumpendo, et absurda etiam absurdissima admittendo.
So it is no wonder that all who have tried to understand the progress of nature by such notions, and moreover badly understood ones, have entangled themselves so marvellously that in the end they could not extricate themselves except by breaking through everything and admitting even the most absurd absurdities.
Nam cum multa sint, quae nequaquam imaginatione; sed solo intellectu assequi possumus, qualia sunt substantia, aeternitas, et alia. Si quis talia eiusmodi notionibus, quae duntaxat auxilia imaginationis sunt, explicare conatur: nihilo plus agit, quam si det operam, ut sua imaginatione insaniat.
For since there are many things which we can attain not by the imagination at all, but only by the intellect, such as substance, eternity and others. If anyone tries to explain such things by notions of this kind, which are merely aids of the imagination, he does nothing more than if he were to take pains to go mad with his imagination.
Neque etiam ipsi substantiae modi, si cum eiusmodi entibus rationis, seu imaginationis auxiliis confundantur, unquam recte intelligi poterunt. Nam cum id facimus, eos a substantia, et modo, quo ab aeternitate fluunt, separamus; sine quibus tamen recte intelligi nequeunt.
Nor will the modes of substance themselves ever be rightly understood if they are confused with beings of reason of this kind, or aids of the imagination. For when we do this, we separate them from substance and from the way in which they flow from eternity, without which, however, they cannot be rightly understood.
Quod ut adhuc clarius videas, cape hoc exemplum: nempe, ubi quis durationem abstracte conceperit, eamque cum tempore confundendo in partes dividere inceperit, nunquam poterit intelligere,a qua ratione hora ex. grat. transire possit. Nam ut hora transeat, necesse erit, eius dimidium prius transire, et postea dimidium reliqui, et deinde dimidium, quod huius reliqui superest; et si sic porro infinite dimidium a reliquo subtrahas, nunquam ad finem horae pervenire poteris.
To see this still more clearly, take this example: when someone has conceived duration abstractly and, confusing it with time, has begun to divide it into parts, he will never be able to understand how an hour, for example, can pass. For for an hour to pass, it will be necessary that half of it pass first, and then half of the remainder, and then half of what is left of this remainder; and if you go on in this way, subtracting half from the remainder to infinity, you will never be able to reach the end of the hour.
  1. a So Van Vloten and Land (VVL³ III 41); the digital text of Gebhardt used here reads unquam.
Quare multi, qui entia rationis a realibus distinguere assueti non sunt, durationem ex momentis componi, ausi sunt asseverare, et sic in Scyllam inciderunt cupientes vitare Charybdim. Idem enim est durationem ex momentis componere, quam numerum ex sola nullitatum additione.
So many who are not accustomed to distinguish beings of reason from real ones have dared to assert that duration is composed of moments, and so have fallen into Scylla in their desire to avoid Charybdis. For it is the same to compose duration of moments as to compose number by the mere addition of nothings.
[G IV 58] Porro cum ex modo dictis satis pateat, nec numerum, nec mensuram, nec tempus, quandoquidem non nisi auxilia imaginationis sunt, posse esse infinitos: Nam alias numerus non esset numerus, nec mensura mensura, nec tempus tempus. Hinc clare videre est, cur multi, qui haec tria cum rebus ipsis confundebant, propterea quod veram rerum naturam ignorabant, infinitum actu negarunt.
Further, since it is sufficiently evident from what has just been said that neither number nor measure nor time can be infinite, since they are only aids of the imagination: for otherwise number would not be number, nor measure measure, nor time time. Hence it can be clearly seen why many who confused these three with the things themselves, because they were ignorant of the true nature of things, denied the actual infinite.
Sed quam misere ratiocinati sint, iudicent mathematici, quibus huius farinae argumenta nullam moram iniicere potuerunt in rebus, ab ipsis clare, distincteque perceptis. Nam praeterquam quod multa invenerunt, quae nullo numero explicari possunt; quod satis numerorum defectum ad omnia determinandum patefacit: multa etiam habent, quae nullo numero adaequari possunt; sed omnem, qui dari potest, numerum superant.
But how wretchedly they have reasoned, let the mathematicians judge, whom arguments of this sort have been unable to hold up at all in matters clearly and distinctly perceived by them. For besides having found many things which cannot be explained by any number, which sufficiently reveals the inadequacy of numbers for determining everything, they also have many things which cannot be equalled by any number, but surpass every number that can be given.
Nec tamen concludunt, talia omnem numerum superare ex partium multitudine: sed ex eo, quod rei natura non sine manifesta contradictione numerum pati potest, ut ex. grat. omnes inaequalitates spatii duobus circulis AB et CD interpositi,a omnesque variationes, quas materia, in eo mota, pati debeat, omnem numerum superant.
Yet they do not conclude that such things surpass every number from the multitude of their parts, but from the fact that the nature of the thing cannot admit number without manifest contradiction; as, for example, all the inequalities of the space interposed between the two circles AB and CD, and all the variations which matter moved in it must undergo, surpass every number.1
  1. a Spinoza gives a figure here (G IV 59), which is not reproduced: two circles with different centres, the space between them being widest at AB and narrowest at CD, as the text that follows explains.
  1. 1 Thirteen years later Tschirnhaus asked how this was to be understood, since mathematicians seem always to argue from the multitude of parts (Letter 80); Spinoza’s reply is Letter 81.
[G IV 59] Idque non concluditur, ex nimia spatii interpositi magnitudine: Nam quantumvis parvam eius portionem capiamus, huius tamen parvae portionis inaequalitates omnem numerum superabunt.
And this is not concluded from the excessive size of the interposed space. For however small a portion of it we take, the inequalities of this small portion will still surpass every number.
Neque etiam idcirco concluditur, ut in aliis contingit, quod eius maximum, et minimum non habeamus: utrumque enim in hoc nostro exemplo habemus, maximum nempe AB, minimum vero CD: Sed ex eo tantum concluditur, quod natura spatii inter duos circulos, diversa centra habentes, interpositi nihil tale pati possit. Ideoque siquis omnes illas inaequalitates certo aliquo numero determinare velit, simul efficere debebit, ut circulus non sit circulus.
Nor, again, is it concluded, as happens in other cases, from our not having its maximum and minimum; for we have both in this example of ours, the maximum, namely AB, and the minimum, CD. It is concluded only from the fact that the nature of the space interposed between two circles having different centres can admit nothing of the kind. And so, if anyone wanted to determine all those inequalities by some definite number, he would at the same time have to bring it about that a circle is not a circle.
[G IV 60] Sic etiam, ut ad nostrum propositum revertar, si quis omnes materiae motus, qui hucusque fuerunt, determinare volet, eos scilicet, eorumque durationem ad certum numerum, et tempus redigendo; is certe nihil aliud conabitur, quam substantiam corpoream, quam non nisi existentem concipere possumus, suis affectionibus privare, et, ut naturam, quam habet, non habeat, efficere.
So too, to return to our purpose, if anyone wants to determine all the motions of matter that there have been up to now, namely by reducing them and their duration to a definite number and time, he will certainly be attempting nothing else than to deprive corporeal substance, which we can conceive only as existing, of its affections, and to bring it about that it does not have the nature it has.
Quae clare demonstrare hic possem, ut et alia multa, quae in hac epistola attigi, nisi id superfluum iudicarem. Ex omnibus iam dictis clare videre est, quaedam sua natura esse infinita, nec ullo modo finita concipi posse; quaedam vero vi causae, cui inhaerent, quae tamen, ubi abstracte concipiuntur, in partes possunt dividi, et ut finita spectari; quaedam denique infinita, vel, si mavis, indefinita dici, propterea quod nullo numero adaequari queant, quae tamen maiora, et minora possunt concipi; quia non sequitur, illa necessario debere esse aequalia, quae numero adaequari nequeunt, ut ex allato exemplo, et aliis multis satis est manifestum.
I could demonstrate these things clearly here, as well as many others which I have touched on in this letter, if I did not judge it superfluous. From all that has now been said it can be clearly seen that some things are infinite by their nature and cannot in any way be conceived as finite; some by the force of the cause in which they inhere, which however, when they are conceived abstractly, can be divided into parts and regarded as finite; and finally some which are called infinite, or, if you prefer, indefinite, because they cannot be equalled by any number, which can nevertheless be conceived as greater and less; since it does not follow that things which cannot be equalled by number must necessarily be equal, as is sufficiently manifest from the example given and from many others.
[G IV 61] Denique causas errorum, et confusionum, quae circa quaestionem de infinito ortae sunt, breviter ob oculos posui, easque, ni fallor, ita explicui omnes, ut non putem ullam superesse circa infinitum quaestionem, quam hic non attigi, aut quae ex dictis facillime solvi non queat. Quare in his te detinere diutius, operae pretium esse non iudico.
I have now briefly set before your eyes the causes of the errors and confusions that have arisen concerning the question of the infinite, and, unless I am mistaken, I have explained them all in such a way that I do not think any question concerning the infinite remains which I have not touched on here, or which cannot very easily be solved from what has been said. So I do not judge it worth while to detain you longer on these matters.
Verum hic obiter adhuc notari velim, quod Peripatetici recentiores, ut quidem puto, male intellexerunt demonstrationem veterum, qua ostendere nitebantur Dei existentiam. Nam, ut ipsam apud Iudaeum quendam Rab Ghasdai vocatum, reperio, sic sonat.
But I should like it to be noted here in passing that the more recent Peripatetics, as I at least think, have misunderstood the demonstration of the ancients by which they strove to show God’s existence. For as I find it in a certain Jew called Rab Ghasdai,1 it runs thus.
  1. 1 Hasdai Crescas, Or Adonai (אוֹר ה׳, ‘Light of the Lord’), I.3.2, the third of the ‘roots’ of the first treatise: אם שיהיו עלות ועלולים ב״ת או בב״ת אין המלט מהיות עלה אחת לכללם (‘whether causes and effects be finite or infinite, there is no escaping that there is one cause of them all’), since if all were caused, all would be possible in themselves and would need something to determine their existence rather than their non-existence; and that is God.
Si datur progressus causarum in infinitum, erunt omnia, quae sunt, etiam causata: Atqui nulli, quod causatum est, competit, vi suae naturae necessario existere: Ergo nihil est in natura ad cuius essentiam pertinet necessario existere. Sed hoc est absurdum: ergo et illud.
If there is a progression of causes to infinity, all things that are will also be caused; but it belongs to nothing that is caused to exist necessarily by the force of its own nature; therefore there is nothing in nature to whose essence it pertains to exist necessarily. But this is absurd; therefore that is too.
Quare vis argumenti non in eo sita est, quod impossibile sit, dari actu infinitum, aut progressus causarum in infinitum: Sed tantum in eo, quod supponatur, res, quae sua natura non necessario existunt, non determinari ad existendum a re sua natura necessario existenti.a
So the force of the argument does not lie in the impossibility of there being an actual infinite, or a progression of causes to infinity, but only in the supposition that things which do not exist necessarily by their own nature are not determined to exist by a thing which exists necessarily by its own nature.2
  1. a The Dutch version adds: ‘and which is a cause, not a thing caused’ (G IV 62).
  1. 2 Compare Part I, Prop. 28, on the infinite series of finite causes, with its scholium on God’s causality of finite things.
[G IV 62] Transirem iam, quia tempus me festinare cogit, ad secundam tuam epistolam: sed ad ea, quae isthac continentur, commodius, cum dignatus fueris me invisere, respondere potero. Quaeso itaque, si fieri potest, ut quamprimum venias; nam tempus migrandi festinanter accedit. Tantum est. Vale, meique memor vive, qui sum, etc.
I would now pass on, since time compels me to hurry, to your second letter; but I shall be able to reply more conveniently to what it contains when you deign to visit me. So I beg you to come as soon as possible, if it can be done; for the time of my moving is fast approaching.1 That is all. Farewell, and remember me, who am, etc.
  1. 1 Spinoza moved from Rijnsburg to Voorburg in the spring of 1663 (Nadler, p. 239).

Epistola XVIII

Letter 18

Willem van Blyenbergh to Spinoza

Clarissimo viro
To the most distinguished man
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Guilielmus de Blyenbergh.
from Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine, et amice ignote,
Sir, and unknown friend,
[G IV 79] Iam saepiuscule tuum tractatum,a una cum eius appendice, nuper in lucem editum, attente evolvi. Magis me decebit, ut aliis, quam tibi narrem summam, quam in eo reperi, soliditatem, et quam inde percepi voluptatem: id tamen reticere nequeo, quod, quo frequentius cum attentione eum percurro, eo magis mihi placeat, et continuo aliquid observem, quod antea non animadverteram.
I have now quite often read attentively your treatise, together with its appendix, recently published.1 It would become me better to tell others than you of the very great solidity I found in it and the pleasure I took from it; yet I cannot keep silent about this, that the more often I go through it attentively, the more it pleases me, and I continually notice something I had not observed before.
  1. a Blyenbergh and Spinoza wrote to each other in Dutch. The text here is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 473–477, Letter 31), headed Versio; Gebhardt prints the Dutch first (G IV 79–85). The translation follows the Latin; notes give the Dutch where the two differ in sense.
  1. 1 The Principles of Descartes’s Philosophy with the Metaphysical Thoughts as its appendix, published in 1663 (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., pp. 242–243). Blyenbergh, a grain merchant and broker of Dordrecht, had read only this book, and had trouble telling where Spinoza gave his own views and where Descartes’s (Nadler pp. 251–252).
Verumenimvero (ne hac in epistola adulator videar) nolo eius auctorem nimium admirari: Novi, deos omnia laboribus vendere.
But truly (lest I seem a flatterer in this letter), I do not wish to admire its author too much: I know that the gods sell everything for labour.
Ne autem te in admiratione nimis diu detineam, quis sit, et qui fiat, ut tibi ignotus tanta ad te scribendi libertate utatur, dicam tibi, eum esse, qui desiderio purae, et sincerae veritatis impulsus, in brevi hac et fragili vita pedes suos in scientia, quantum humanum nostrum patitur ingenium, figere studet: quique nullum alium, ad indagandum veritatem, scopum sibi praefixit, quam ipsam veritatem: quique scientia nec honores, nec divitias; sed meram veritatem, tranquillitatemque, tanquam veritatis effectum, sibi acquirere conatur: et qui ex omnibus veritatibus, ac scientiis, nullis magis, quam metaphysicis, si non omnibus, saltem aliqua earum parte se oblectat: et qui omnem suam vitae delectationem in eo collocat, ut horas otiosas, et subcisivas in iis transigat.
But not to keep you too long in wonder as to who he is, and how it comes about that someone unknown to you takes such liberty in writing to you, I shall tell you that he is one who, driven by the desire for pure and sincere truth, strives in this short and fragile life to set his feet firmly in knowledge, as far as our human understanding allows; who has set himself no other goal in seeking out truth than truth itself; who tries to gain by knowledge neither honours nor riches, but truth alone, and tranquillity as the effect of truth; who, of all truths and sciences, delights in none more than in metaphysics, if not in all of it, at least in some part of it; and who places all the delight of his life in spending his idle and spare hours on these things.
Verum nec quivis adeo beatus est, nec tantum adhibet studium, quantum mihi persuadeo, te adhibuisse, et propterea quivis eo perfectionis non pervenit, quo te iam pervenisse, ex opere tuo perspicio. Verbo ut absolvam, is ipse est, quem familiarius cognoscere tibi licebit, si eum tibi tantopere devincire placeat, ut aperias, et quasi perfores haerentes eius cogitationes.
But not everyone is so blessed, nor does everyone apply as much study as I am persuaded you have applied; and therefore not everyone arrives at that degree of perfection which I perceive from your work that you have already reached. To finish in a word: he is the man whom you will be able to know more closely, if it please you to oblige him so far as to open up, and as it were bore through, his halting thoughts.
[G IV 80] Sed ad tractatum tuum redeo. Quemadmodum inibi multa inveni, quae summopere palato arridebant, ita etiam quaedam obtulerunt sese, concoctu difficilia, quaeque mihi tibi ignoto ita obiicere haud aequum foret, eoque magis, quod grata, nec ne haec sint futura, me latet: et haec est causa, cur haec praemittam, et rogem, an liceat, si his vesperis Hiemalibus tempus superfuerit, tibique placuerit, ad meas difficultates, quae mihi in libro tuo adhuc restant, respondere, nonnullas earum transmittere; hac tamen lege, et obtestatione, ne ego rei magis necessariae, tibique magis acceptae impedimento sim: quoniam nihil intensius, iuxta promissa in libro tuo facta, quam latiorem opinionum tuarum explicationem, ac editionem desidero.
But I return to your treatise. Just as I found many things in it which were very much to my taste, so also some offered themselves that were hard to digest, which it would hardly be fair for me, as one unknown to you, to put to you thus, the more so since I do not know whether they will be welcome or not; and this is the reason why I send this ahead, and ask whether, if time remains on these winter evenings and it pleases you to answer the difficulties which still remain for me in your book, I may send you some of them; on this condition, however, and with this entreaty, that I should not keep you from anything more necessary or more agreeable to you; since there is nothing I desire more intensely than the fuller explanation and publication of your opinions, in accordance with the promises made in your book.
Ipse id, quod tandem calamo, et chartae credo, coram salute impertita, praestitissem; quia vero primo tuum hospitium mihi ignotum erat, dein morbus contagiosus, et denique munus meum impediebant, hoc ipsum in aliud, ac aliud tempus dilatum est.
What I now at last entrust to pen and paper I should myself have done in person, with a greeting; but because, first, your lodging was unknown to me, then the contagious disease,1 and finally my office prevented me, it has been put off from one time to another.
  1. 1 The plague, which in the summer of 1664 was ‘scattered generally over the whole country’, as an English diplomat reported (Nadler p. 250).
[G IV 81] Ne vero haec epistola plane sit vacua, et quia simul spe ducor, non tibi fore ingratum, unicum tantum tibi proponam: nempe, te passim tam in principiis, quam in cogitatis metaphysicis statuere, sive ut propriam sententiam, sive ut Cartesium, cuius philosophiam docebas, explicares, creare, et conservare unum et idem esse, (quod per se adeo clarum est iis, qui eo suas cogitationes direxerunt, ut etiam prima sit notio) et Deum non modo substantias; sed etiam motum in substantiis creasse, hoc est, Deum non solum substantias in statu suo continua creatione; verum etiam earundem motum, ac conatum conservare.
But so that this letter may not be altogether empty, and since I am led at the same time by the hope that it will not be unwelcome to you, I shall put forward just one thing: namely, that in various places, both in the Principles and in the Metaphysical Thoughts, whether as your own opinion or in explaining Descartes, whose philosophy you were teaching, you maintain that to create and to preserve are one and the same1 (which is so clear in itself to those who have turned their thoughts to it that it is even a first notion), and that God has created not only substances but also motion in substances; that is, that God preserves by continual creation not only substances in their state, but also their motion and striving.
  1. 1 Principles, Part I, Ax. 10 (G I 157): ‘No less a cause is required to preserve a thing than to produce it in the first place’; Metaphysical Thoughts II.10 (G I 269): ‘the operation of God in creating the world is the same as in preserving it’; and II.11 (G I 273): God ‘continually creates a thing as if anew at each moment’.
Deus, verbi causa, non tantum efficit, ut anima, immediata Dei voluntate, atque operatione (perinde est, quomodo id nomines) diutius existat, inque suo perseveret statu; verum etiam causa est, ut tali modo sese ad animae motum habeat:a hoc est, quemadmodum continua Dei creatio efficit, ut res diutius existant, ita quoque conatus, vel rerum motus per eandem causam fit in rebus; quandoquidem extra Deum nulla motus datur causa.
God, for example, not only brings it about, by his immediate will and operation (it makes no difference what you call it), that the soul continues to exist and perseveres in its state, but is also the cause that he stands in such a relation to the motion of the soul; that is, just as God’s continual creation brings it about that things continue to exist, so also the striving or motion of things comes about in things through the same cause; since outside God there is no cause of motion.
