  1. a The Dutch (G IV 82) says only that God ‘also stands in such a way towards the motion of the Soul’; the Latin version makes him the cause of it.
Sequitur igitur, Deum non tantum causam esse substantiae mentis, sed etiam cuiuscunque conatus, vel motus mentis, quem voluntatem nuncupamus, veluti passim statuis: ex qua assertione etiam necessario sequi videtur, vel nil mali in motu, sive mentis voluntate esse, vel Deum ipsum illud malum immediate operari. Nam ea quoque, quae mala appellamus, per animam, et consequenter per eiusmodi immediatum influxum, et Dei concursum fiunt.
It follows, therefore, that God is the cause not only of the substance of the mind, but also of every striving or motion of the mind, which we call will, as you maintain throughout; and from this assertion it also seems necessarily to follow either that there is no evil in the motion or will of the mind, or that God himself works that evil immediately. For the things which we call evil also come about through the soul, and consequently through such an immediate influence and concurrence of God.
Exempli gratia, anima Adami vult edere de fructu prohibito, efficitur ergo, iuxta superius tradita; non tantum ut illa Adami voluntas per Dei influxum velit; sed etiam, quemadmodum statim ostendetur, ut tali modo velit; adeo ut ille Adami prohibitus actus, quatenus Deus non modo voluntatem eius movebat, sed et quatenus eam tali modo movebat, vel in se non sit malus, vel ut Deus ipse illud operari videatur, quod nos malum vocamus.
For example, Adam’s soul wills to eat of the forbidden fruit; it is brought about, therefore, according to what was said above, not only that this will of Adam wills through God’s influence, but also, as will be shown presently, that it wills in such a way; so that this forbidden act of Adam, insofar as God not only moved his will but also moved it in such a way, is either not evil in itself, or God himself seems to work what we call evil.
Nec aut tu aut D. Cartesius hunc nodum solvere videris, dicendo malum esse non ens, cum quo Deus non concurrit: unde enim voluntas edendi, aut: voluntas Diabolorum ad superbiam procedebat?
Nor do you or Mr Descartes seem to untie this knot by saying that evil is a non-being, with which God does not concur:2 for where, then, did the will to eat, or the will of the devils to pride, come from?
  1. 2 Compare the Principles, Part I, Prop. 15 (G I 172): ‘Error is not something positive’, with its scholium.
Nam quoniam voluntas (ut recte animadvertis) non sit diversum quid ab ipsa mente; sed sit hic, aut ille motus, vel mentis conatus, tam ad hanc, quam ad illam motionem concursu Dei ei opus erit; iam vero Dei concursus, ut ex scriptis tuis intelligo, nihil aliud est, quam rem per suam voluntatem hoc, vel illo pacto determinare: sequitur, ergo Deum aeque cum mala voluntate, quatenus mala est, ac cum bona, quatenus bona est, concurrere, hoc est, eam determinare. Nam voluntas Dei, quae causa absoluta est omnium, quae existunt tam in substantia, quam in conatu, videtur etiam prima causa esse malae voluntatis, quatenus mala est.
For since the will (as you rightly observe) is not something different from the mind itself,3 but is this or that motion or striving of the mind, it will need God’s concurrence as much for the one motion as for the other; and God’s concurrence, as I understand from your writings, is nothing other than to determine a thing by his will in this or that way: it follows, therefore, that God concurs with the evil will, insofar as it is evil, just as much as with the good will, insofar as it is good, that is, determines it. For the will of God, which is the absolute cause of everything that exists, both in substance and in striving, seems also to be the first cause of the evil will, insofar as it is evil.
  1. 3 Metaphysical Thoughts II.12 (G I 279): ‘we have shown clearly that the will is nothing but the mind itself, which we call a thinking thing, that is, an affirming and denying thing’.
Deinde nulla in nobis fit voluntatis determinatio, quin Deus eam ab aeterno sciverit; alioquin, si nesciverit, in Deo imperfectionem statuimus; sed qui Deus illam aliter sciverit, quam ex suis decretis? Sunt igitur eius decreta nostrarum determinationum causa; et ita rursum sequi videtur, malam voluntatem vel non esse malum quid, vel Deum illius mali causam esse immediatam, et id operari.
Next, no determination of the will occurs in us without God’s having known it from eternity; otherwise, if he did not know it, we ascribe an imperfection to God; but how could God have known it otherwise than from his decrees? His decrees, then, are the cause of our determinations; and so again it seems to follow either that the evil will is not something evil, or that God is the immediate cause of that evil and works it.
Neque hic distinctio Theologorum de differentia inter actum, et malum actui adhaerens locum habere potest: nam Deus cum actum, tum modum actus decrevit, hoc est, Deus non solummodo decrevit, ut Adamus ederet, verum etiam ut necessario contra mandatum ederet. Adeo ut iterum sequi videatur, vel τὸ edere Adamib adversus praeceptum non esse malum, vel Deum ipsum illud operari.
Nor can the theologians’ distinction between the act and the evil adhering to the act have any place here: for God decreed both the act and the manner of the act; that is, God decreed not only that Adam should eat, but also that he should necessarily eat against the command. So that again it seems to follow either that Adam’s eating against the command is not evil, or that God himself works it.
  1. b τὸ (‘the’): the Greek article, which makes the infinitive a noun, ‘the eating of Adam’, as printed in 1677 (OP p. 476). The usage recurs in Letters 19–22; the digital text of Gebhardt transliterates it.
[G IV 84] Haec sunt, vir clarissime, quae in praesentia in tractatu tuo percipere nequeo: extrema namque utrinque statuere durum est. Tale autem responsum a perspicaci tuo iudicio et industria, quod mihi faciat satis, exspecto, et spero me sequentibus meis ostensurum, quantum tibi propterea debeam.
These, most distinguished Sir, are the things which at present I cannot understand in your treatise: for it is hard to maintain either extreme. But I expect from your perspicacious judgment and diligence such an answer as will satisfy me, and I hope to show in my following letters how much I shall owe you for it.
Certo tibi, vir Clar. persuadeas, me haec nulla alia de causa, quam studio veritatis quaerere: liber sum, nulli adstrictus professioni, honestis mercaturis me alo, et tempus, quod mihi reliquum est, hisce rebus impendo. Submisse quoque rogo, ut meae difficultates tibi non sint ingratae; si ad hasce respondere animus est, id quod ardentissime cupio, scribe ad, etc.a
Be assured, most distinguished Sir, that I ask these things for no other reason than the pursuit of truth: I am free, bound to no profession; I support myself by honest trade, and spend the time that remains to me on these matters. I humbly ask, too, that my difficulties may not be unwelcome to you; if you have a mind to answer them, which I most ardently desire, write to, etc.
  1. a The Dutch gives the address as the initials ‘W. v. B.’, and signs with them (G IV 85).
[G IV 85] Guil. de Blyenbergh.
Willem van Blyenbergh.
Dordraci 12. Decemb. 1664.
Dordrecht, 12 December 1664.

Epistola XIX

Letter 19

Spinoza to Willem van Blyenbergh

Viro Doctissimo, ac Prudentissimo
To the most learned and most prudent man
Guilielmo de Blyenbergh
Willem van Blyenbergh
Amice Ignote,
Unknown friend,
[G IV 86] Epistolam tuam 12. Decembris datam,a et in alia inclusam, scripta 21. eiusdem mensis, 26. Schiedami demum accepi; unde intensum tuum veritatis amorem, eamque solummodo omnium studiorum tuorum scopum esse, intelligebam: quod me, qui etiam in nihil aliud animum intendo, ut concluderem coegit, non tantum tuam petitionem, nempe ut ad tuas, quas iam mittis, et imposterum mittes quaestiones, pro viribus mei intellectus responderem, plene concedere; verum omnia quoque pro mea parte conferre, quae ulteriori notitiae, sinceraeque amicitiae inservire queunt.
Your letter dated 12 December, enclosed in another written on the 21st of the same month, I received at last at Schiedam1 on the 26th; and from it I understood your intense love of truth, and that truth alone is the aim of all your studies: which compelled me, who also set my mind on nothing else, to resolve not only to grant your request fully, namely to answer, as far as my intellect allows, the questions which you send now and will send in future, but also to contribute everything on my part that can serve further acquaintance and sincere friendship.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 477–481, Letter 32), headed Versio. Gebhardt prints Spinoza’s Dutch after it (G IV 86–95); it is dated ‘Op de Lange bogart den 5 Januarii 1665’, and has a closing paragraph and a postscript which the Latin lacks (see the note at the end of the letter).
  1. 1 During the plague Spinoza spent the winter of 1664–1665 at De Lange Boogert, a farm near Schiedam owned by Jacob Simons Gijsen, the father-in-law of Simon de Vries’s sister (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 251).
Quantum enim ad me attinet: Nullas ex omnibus rebus, quae in potestate mea non sunt, pluris facio, quam cum Viris veritatem sincere amantibus foedus inire amicitiae; quia credo, nos nihil omnino in mundo, quod nostrae potestatis non est, tranquillius, quam istiusmodi homines, posse amare; quoniam tam impossibile est amorem dissolvere, quem ii erga invicem habent, eo quod amore, quem unusquisque erga veritatis cognitionem habet, fundatus est, quam ipsam veritatem semel perceptam non amplecti. Est insuper summus, ac gratissimus, quae in rebus, quae nostri arbitrii non sunt, dari potest; quandoquidem nulla res, praeter veritatem, diversos sensus, et animos penitus unire valet.
For as far as I am concerned, of all the things which are not in my power I value none more highly than to enter into a bond of friendship with men who sincerely love truth; because I believe that of the things in the world which are not in our power we can love nothing at all with more tranquillity than such men; since it is as impossible to dissolve the love which they have for one another, founded as it is on the love which each has for the knowledge of truth, as not to embrace truth itself once it has been perceived. This love is, moreover, the highest and most pleasing that can be had among things not within our choice; since nothing but truth can unite different opinions and minds completely.2
  1. 2 Compare Part IV, Prop. 35: ‘Only insofar as men live from the guidance of reason do they always necessarily agree in nature’.
Taceo maximas utilitates, quae inde fluunt, ut rebus, quas procul dubio ipse nosti, diutius te non detineam, quod tamen huc usque feci, quo eo melius ostenderem, quam gratum mihi, et imposterum futurum sit, occasionem nancisci ad meum tibi officium praestandum.
I pass over the very great advantages which flow from it, so as not to keep you longer with things which you doubtless know yourself; which I have nevertheless done so far, the better to show how welcome it is to me, and will be in future, to have the opportunity of doing you a service.
Ut autem praesentem captem, propius accedam, et ad quaestionem tuam respondebo, quae in eo vertitur cardine, nimirum; quod clare videatur sequi, tam ex Dei providentia, quae ab eius voluntate non differt, quam ex Dei concursu, rerumque creatione continua, vel nulla dari peccata, nullumve malum, vel Deum ea peccata, atque illud malum efficere. Verum non explicas, quid per malum intelligas, et quantum ex exemplo de determinata voluntate Adami perspicere licet, videris per malum intelligere ipsam voluntatem, quatenus tali modo determinata conciperetur, vel quatenus Dei mandato repugnaret, et propterea ais, magnam esse absurditatem (ut et ego, si se ita res haberet) alterutrum horum statuere, nempe, Deum ipsum res, quae contra suam voluntatem sunt, operari, vel eas bonas fore, licet voluntati Dei repugnarent.
But to seize the present one, I shall come closer and answer your question, which turns on this hinge: that it seems clearly to follow, both from God’s providence, which does not differ from his will, and from God’s concurrence and continual creation of things, either that there are no sins and no evil, or that God brings about those sins and that evil. But you do not explain what you understand by evil, and as far as one can see from the example of Adam’s determinate will, you seem to understand by evil the will itself, insofar as it is conceived as determined in such a way, or insofar as it conflicted with God’s command; and therefore you say that it is a great absurdity (as I too would, if the matter stood so) to maintain either of these: namely, that God himself works things which are against his will, or that they would be good although they conflicted with God’s will.
Quoad me, non possum concedere peccata, et malum quid positivum esse, multo minus, aliquid esse, aut fieri contra Dei voluntatem. Econtra dico, non solum peccata non esse quid positivum; verum etiam affirmo, nos non nisi improprie, vel humano more loquendo dicere posse, nos erga Deum peccare, ut cum dicimus, homines Deum offendere.
For my part, I cannot grant that sins and evil are something positive, much less that anything is or happens against God’s will. On the contrary, I say not only that sins are not something positive; I also affirm that we can say only improperly, or speaking in human fashion, that we sin against God,3 as when we say that men offend God.
  1. 3 In the Ethics sin can be conceived only in a state, where what is good and evil is decided by common consent (Part IV, Prop. 37, Schol. 2).
Nam, primum quod spectat, novimus quicquid est, in se consideratum sine respectu ad aliud quid, perfectionem includere, quae sese eo usque in quacunque re extendit, quo se extendit ipsa rei essentia: nam essentia etiam nihil aliud est.
For as regards the first point, we know that whatever is, considered in itself without relation to anything else, includes perfection, which in each thing extends as far as the very essence of the thing extends: for essence too is nothing else.4
  1. 4 Compare Part II, Def. 6: ‘By reality and perfection I understand the same thing’.
Sumo, exempli gratia, consilium, sive determinatam Adami voluntatem ad edendum de fructu prohibito: hoc consilium, sive illa determinata voluntas in se sola considerata tantum perfectionis includit, quantum realitatis exprimit; et hoc inde intelligi potest, nempe, quod in rebus nullam imperfectionem possimus concipere, nisi ad alias res attendamus, quae plus habent realitatis; et propterea in Adami decreto, quando illud in se consideramus, nec cum aliis perfectioribus, perfectioremve statum ostendentibus, comparamus, nullam poterimus invenire imperfectionem; imo cum infinitis aliis comparare respectu illius longe imperfectioribus, uti lapidibus, truncis, etc. licet.
Take, for example, the resolve, or determinate will, of Adam to eat of the forbidden fruit: this resolve, or that determinate will, considered in itself alone, includes as much perfection as it expresses reality; and this can be understood from the fact that we can conceive no imperfection in things unless we attend to other things which have more reality;5 and therefore in Adam’s decree, when we consider it in itself and do not compare it with other things more perfect or showing a more perfect state, we shall be able to find no imperfection; indeed, we may compare it with countless other things, far less perfect in relation to it, such as stones, tree-trunks, etc.
  1. 5 The account of perfection and imperfection as comparisons in the Preface to Part IV.
Et hoc quivis revera quoque concedit: nam quilibet res, quas in hominibus detestatur, cumque aversione contemplatur, in animalibus cum admiratione intuetur, uti apum bella, atque columbarum zelotypiam, etc. quae in hominibus spernuntur, et nihilominus ob ea animalia perfectiora iudicamus. Haec cum ita sint, clare sequitur, peccata, quandoquidem ea nil nisi imperfectionem indicant, non posse in aliquo consistere, quod realitatem exprimit, sicuti Adami decreto, eiusque executione.
And this, in truth, everyone also concedes: for everyone looks with wonder, in animals, on things which he detests in men and contemplates with aversion, such as the wars of bees and the jealousy of doves, etc., which are despised in men, and yet on account of them we judge the animals more perfect. Since this is so, it clearly follows that sins, since they indicate nothing but imperfection, cannot consist in something which expresses reality, such as Adam’s decree and its execution.
[G IV 90] Praeterea, neque dicere valemus, Adami voluntatem cum lege Dei pugnare, ac ideo illam esse malam, quia Deo displiceret: nam, praeterquam quod magnam in Deo poneret imperfectionem, si quicquam contra eius voluntatem fieret, sique quid desideraret, cuius compos non fieret, eiusque natura tali modo determinata esset, ut, quemadmodum creaturae, cum his sympathiam, cum illis antipathiam haberet; etiam omnino cum voluntatis divinae natura pugnaret, quia enim illa ab eius intellectu non discrepat, impossibile aeque est, aliquid fieri contra eius voluntatem, ac contra eius intellectum, hoc est, id, quod contra eius voluntatem fieret, talis deberet esse naturae, ut eius etiam intellectui repugnaret, ut quadratum Rotundum.
Further, neither can we say that Adam’s will conflicts with God’s law, and that it is evil because it displeases God: for besides the fact that it would posit a great imperfection in God if anything happened against his will, and if he desired something which he did not obtain, and if his nature were so determined that, like creatures, he had sympathy with some things and antipathy towards others; it would also conflict altogether with the nature of the divine will; for since that does not differ from his intellect, it is as impossible for anything to happen against his will as against his intellect; that is, what happened against his will would have to be of such a nature as to conflict with his intellect too, like a round square.
Quandoquidem ergo voluntas, decretumve Adami, in se spectatum, nec malum, nec quoque proprie loquendo contra Dei voluntatem erat; sequitur Deum eius causam posse, immo, iuxta eam rationem, quam animadvertis, debere esse; non vero quatenus malum erat: nam malum, quod in eo erat, non erat aliud, quam privationis perfectioris status, quem propter illud opus Adamus amittere debebat, et certum est, privationem non esse quid positivum, eamque respectu nostri, non vero Dei intellectus ita nominari.
Since, then, Adam’s will or decree, regarded in itself, was neither evil nor, properly speaking, against God’s will, it follows that God can be its cause, indeed, by the reasoning which you point out, must be; but not insofar as it was evil: for the evil that was in it was nothing other than the privation of a more perfect state which Adam had to lose because of that deed; and it is certain that a privation is not something positive, and that it is so called in relation to our intellect, not God’s.
Hoc autem hinc oritur, quia cuncta eiusdem generis singularia, ea omnia, verbi gratia, quae externam hominum figuram habent, una eademque definitione exprimimus, et idcirco iudicamus, ea omnia aeque apta esse ad summam perfectionem, quam ex eiusmodi definitione deducere possumus; quando autem unum invenimus, cuius opera cum ista pugnant perfectione, tunc id ea privatum esse iudicamus, et a sua natura aberrare, quod haud faceremus, si id ad eiusmodi definitionem non retulissemus, talemque ei naturam affinxissemus. Quoniam vero Deus res nec abstracte novit, nec id genus generales format definitiones, nec plus realitatis rebus competit, quam iis divinus intellectus, et potentia immisit, et revera tribuit, manifesto sequitur, privationem istam solummodo respectu nostri intellectus, non vero respectu Dei dici posse.
This arises from the fact that we express all singulars of the same kind, for example all those which have the external figure of men, by one and the same definition,1 and therefore judge that they are all equally fit for the highest perfection which we can deduce from a definition of that kind; but when we find one whose works conflict with that perfection, we judge that it is deprived of it and departs from its nature; which we would not do if we had not referred it to such a definition and ascribed such a nature to it. But since God neither knows things abstractly nor forms general definitions of that kind, and no more reality belongs to things than the divine intellect and power has put into them and really given them, it plainly follows that this privation can be spoken of only in relation to our intellect, not in relation to God.
  1. 1 The same explanation, from universal ideas of a kind, in the Preface to Part IV.
[G IV 92] Hisce, mihi ut videtur, quaestio penitus soluta est.
With this, it seems to me, the question is entirely solved.
Ut vero viam magis planam reddam, omnemque scrupulum evellam, necesse habeo, ad has duas sequentes quaestiones respondere, nempe primo, quare S. Scriptura dicat Deum flagitare, ut se improbi convertant, et etiam quare Adamo prohibuerit ex arbore non edere, cum tamen contrarium conclusisset. Secundo, quod ex dictis meis sequi videatur, improbos superbia, avaritia, desperatione. etc. aeque Deum colere, ac probos generositate, patientia, amore, etc. propterea quod Dei voluntatem exsequuntur.
But to make the way smoother and remove every scruple, I must answer the two following questions: first, why Holy Scripture says that God demands that the wicked be converted,1 and also why he forbade Adam to eat from the tree2 when he had nevertheless decreed the contrary. Second, that it seems to follow from what I have said that the wicked worship God by their pride, greed, despair, etc., just as much as the good by their nobility, patience, love, etc., because they carry out God’s will.
  1. 1 For example Ezekiel 33:11: nolo mortem impii, sed ut convertatur impius a via sua, et vivat (‘I desire not the death of the wicked, but that the wicked turn from his way, and live’).
  2. 2 Genesis 2:17.
Ad primum ut respondeam, dico Scripturam, quia plebi praecipue convenit, et inservit, continuo humano more loqui: plebs etenim rebus sublimibus percipiendis inepta est; et haec est ratio, cur mihi persuadeam ea omnia, quae Deus prophetis revelavit ad salutem esse necessaria, legum modo scribi; et hoc pacto integras Parabolas prophetae finxerunt, nempe primo Deum, quia salutis, et perditionis media revelarat, quorumque erat causa, instar Regis, ac Legislatoris adumbrarunt; media, quae nil nisi causae sunt, leges appellarunt, et ad modum Legum conscripserunt; salutem, perditionemque, quae nil nisi effectus sunt, qui necessario ex illis mediis fluunt, tanquam praemium, et poenam proposuerunt, atque iuxta hanc Parabolam magis, quam iuxta veritatem omnia sua verba ordinarunt, Deumque ad instar hominis passim expresserunt, modo iratum, modo misericordem, iam futura desiderantem, iam zelo, et suspicione captum, quin ab ipso Diabolo deceptum; adeo ut philosophi, et simul omnes, qui sunt supra legem, hoc est, qui virtutem, non ut legem; sed ex amore, quia praestantissima est, sequuntur, eiusmodi verbis non debeant offendi.
To answer the first, I say that Scripture, because it is suited chiefly to the common people and serves them, continually speaks in human fashion: for the common people are incapable of perceiving sublime things; and this is the reason why I am persuaded that everything which God revealed to the prophets as necessary for salvation is written in the manner of laws; and in this way the prophets devised whole parables: namely, first, because God had revealed the means of salvation and perdition, and was their cause, they represented him as a king and lawgiver; the means, which are nothing but causes, they called laws and wrote down in the manner of laws;1 salvation and perdition, which are nothing but effects that follow necessarily from those means, they set forth as reward and punishment; and they ordered all their words more according to this parable than according to the truth, and everywhere depicted God after the likeness of a man, now angry, now merciful, now desiring things to come, now seized by jealousy and suspicion, indeed deceived by the Devil himself; so that philosophers, and with them all who are above the law, that is, who follow virtue not as a law but from love, because it is the most excellent thing, ought not to be offended by words of this kind.
  1. 1 Developed in the Theological-Political Treatise, ch. 4 (G III 63), where Spinoza says that God revealed to Adam only the evil that would necessarily follow if he ate of the tree, and Adam perceived the revelation as a law.
[G IV 93] Edictum ergo Adamo factum in hoc solummodo consistebat, nempe quod Deus Adamo revelavit, τὸ edere ex illa arbore mortem operari, quemadmodum et nobis per naturalem intellectum revelat, venenum esse mortiferum. Si vero roges, in quem finem hoc ei revelaret? responsum do, ut eum tanto scientia perfectiorem redderet.
The command given to Adam, then, consisted solely in this: that God revealed to Adam that eating of that tree brought about death,1 just as he also reveals to us through the natural intellect that poison is deadly. If you ask to what end he revealed this to him, I reply: to make him so much the more perfect in knowledge.
  1. 1 Compare the reading of the story of Adam in Part IV, Prop. 68, Schol..
Deum ergo interrogare, quamobrem ipsi perfectiorem non dederit voluntatem, est aeque absurdum, ac interrogare, cur circulo omnes globi proprietates non fuerit largitus, uti ex supra dictis perspicue sequitur, et ego scholio prop. 15. Partis primae Princip. Cartes. more geometrico demonstrat. demonstravi.
To ask God, therefore, why he did not give him a more perfect will is as absurd as to ask why he did not bestow on the circle all the properties of the sphere, as follows clearly from what has been said above, and as I have demonstrated in the Scholium to Prop. 15 of Part I of Descartes’s Principles Demonstrated in the Geometrical Manner.2
  1. 2 Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, Schol. (G I 175): ‘God has no more deprived us of a greater intellect, or a more perfect faculty of understanding, than he has deprived the circle of the properties of the globe, and the circumference of those of the sphere’.
[G IV 94] Secundam difficultatem quod attinet, verum quidem est, impios Dei voluntatem suo modo exprimere; ideo tamen cum probis nullatenus sunt comparandi: quo enim res aliqua plus perfectionis habet, eo etiam magis de Deitate participat, Deique perfectionem exprimit magis.
As for the second difficulty, it is true indeed that the impious express God’s will in their own way; but they are not on that account in any way to be compared with the good: for the more perfection a thing has, the more it also participates in the Deity, and the more it expresses God’s perfection.
Quum ergo probi inaestimabiliter plus perfectionis, quam improbi habeant, virtus eorum cum improborum virtute comparari nequit, eo quod improbi amore divino, qui ex Dei profluit cognitione, et quo solo nos pro humano nostro intellectu Dei servi dicimur, carent. Imo, quia Deum non cognoscunt, non sunt nisi instrumentum in manu artificis, quod inscium servit, et serviendo consumitur; probi contra conscii serviunt, et serviendo perfectiores evadunt.a
Since, then, the good have incomparably more perfection than the wicked, their virtue cannot be compared with the virtue of the wicked, because the wicked lack the divine love which flows from the knowledge of God, and by which alone, according to our human intellect, we are said to be servants of God. Indeed, because they do not know God, they are nothing but a tool in the hand of the craftsman, which serves without knowing it and is used up in serving; the good, on the contrary, serve knowingly, and become more perfect by serving.
  1. a Spinoza’s Dutch continues (G IV 95): ‘This, Sir, is all I can now bring forward to answer your question. I wish for nothing more than that it may satisfy you. But if you still find difficulty, I ask that I may know it too, to see whether I could remove it. You need not hesitate on your part; as long as you think yourself unsatisfied, I would like nothing better than to know the reason, so that the truth may at last appear. I could wish that I might write in the language in which I was brought up; I could perhaps express my thoughts better; but please take it in good part, and correct the mistakes yourself, and hold me for your devoted friend and servant, B. de Spinoza. At the Lange Bogart, 5 January 1665.’ A postscript says that he will stay at the farm three or four weeks more and then return to Voorburg, and asks for letters to be sent there, ‘to be delivered in the Kerklaan, at the house of Master Daniel Tydeman, the painter’. The language in which he was brought up was Portuguese (Nadler p. 56).

Epistola XX

Letter 20

Willem van Blyenbergh to Spinoza

Clarissimo viro
To the most distinguished man
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Guilielmus de Blyenbergh.
from Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine, et Charissime amice,
Sir, and dearest friend,
[G IV 96] Ubi tua mihi primum tradebatur epistola,a eaque a me cursim evolvebatur, non tantum statim respondere, sed et multa refutare animus erat. Verum quo eam magis volvebam, eo minorem obiectionum materiam inveniebam, et quantum desiderium legendi me ceperat, tantam legendo percepi voluptatem.
When your letter was first handed to me and I ran through it hastily, I had a mind not only to answer at once but also to refute many things. But the more I turned it over, the less matter for objections I found, and as great as the desire to read it that had seized me, so great was the pleasure I took in reading it.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 482–497, Letter 33), headed Versio; Gebhardt prints the Dutch first (G IV 96–125). The 1677 edition prints in italics the words that Blyenbergh takes from the earlier letters and from Spinoza’s books; quotation marks mark the verbatim quotations here.
Antequam vero ad postulatum accedam, ut nimirum quasdam difficultates solveres, imprimis sciendum est; me duas generales habere regulas, iuxta quas semper studeo philosophari, prior regula est clarus, et distinctus mei intellectus conceptus, posterior est verbum Dei revelatum, vel Dei voluntas. Iuxta priorem amator veritatis, iuxta utramque vero Christianus ut sim philosophus conor; et siquando post longum accideret examen, ut naturalis mea cognitio vel videretur cum hoc verbo pugnare, vel minus bene cum eo convenire, tantum hoc verbum apud me habet auctoritatis, ut conceptus, quos claros esse mihi imaginor, mihi sint potius suspecti, quam ut eos supra et contra illam veritatem, quam in illo libro mihi praescriptam puto, collocarem.
But before I come to my request, namely that you should resolve certain difficulties, you must know first of all that I have two general rules according to which I always try to philosophize:1 the first rule is the clear and distinct conception of my intellect, the second is the revealed word of God, or the will of God. According to the first I try to be a lover of truth, but according to both a Christian philosopher; and if it should ever happen, after long examination, that my natural knowledge seemed either to conflict with this word or to agree with it less well, this word has so much authority with me that the concepts which I imagine to be clear are rather suspect to me than that I should place them above and against the truth which I think is prescribed to me in that book.
  1. 1 Spinoza answers this at the start of his reply (Letter 21): with the second rule, he says, they disagree about first principles themselves (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 253).
Et quid mirum? nam constanter credere volo, illud verbum esse Dei verbum, hoc est, id provenisse a summo, et perfectissimo Deo, qui plures perfectiones includit, quam ego capere possum; et forte de se ipso, suisque operibus plures voluit perfectiones praedicare, quam ego finito meo intellectu hodie, hodie inquam, percipere queo: fieri enim potest, ut memet operibus meis maioribus perfectionibus privarim; et ideo, si forte ea praeditus essem perfectione, qua actionibus propriis privatus sum, percipere possem omne id, quod in eo verbo nobis proponitur, et docetur, cum sanissimis meae mentis conceptibus congruere; verum quia mihi ipsi suspectus sum, annon continuo errore memet meliore privaverim conditione, et, ut statuis, Princ. Part. 1. Prop. 15. nostra cognitio, licet clarissima, adhuc imperfectionem includat; potius, etiam sine ratione, ad illud verbum inclino, hoc nisus fundamento, quod a perfectissimo prodiit, (hoc quippe iam praesuppono, quia eius probatio hic non haberet locum, vel nimis longa foret) et propterea a me credi debet.
And what wonder? For I wish to believe constantly that this word is the word of God, that is, that it has come from the highest and most perfect God, who includes more perfections than I can grasp; and perhaps he willed to declare more perfections of himself and his works than I can perceive today, today, I say, with my finite intellect: for it may be that I have deprived myself by my works of greater perfections; and therefore, if I were perhaps endowed with the perfection of which I have been deprived by my own actions, I could perceive that everything which is set before us and taught in that word agrees with the soundest concepts of my mind; but because I am suspicious of myself, whether I have not deprived myself of a better condition by continual error, and since, as you maintain in the Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, our knowledge, however clear, still includes imperfection, I incline rather to that word, even without reason, relying on this foundation: that it has come from the most perfect being (this indeed I now presuppose, because its proof would have no place here, or would be too long), and therefore must be believed by me.
Si iam solo ductu regulae meae primae, exclusa secunda, quasi eam non haberem, aut ea non extaret, de tua epistola iudicarem, multa mihi essent concedenda, ut et concedo, subtilesque conceptus tui essent suspiciendi; secunda vero regula longius a te me dissentire cogit. Verum pro epistolae modo ductu et huius et illius regulae aliquanto latius eos examinabo.
If I were now to judge your letter by the guidance of my first rule alone, the second excluded, as if I did not have it or it did not exist, I should have to grant many things, as I do grant them, and your subtle concepts would have to be admired; but the second rule compels me to disagree with you more widely. But as far as a letter allows I shall examine them somewhat more fully under the guidance of both the one rule and the other.
[G IV 98] Primo, iuxta primo positam regulam, rogaveram, quia, secundum tuam positionem, creare, et conservare unum, idemque erant, et quia Deus non tantum res; sed motus quoque, et rerum modos in statu suo perseverare faciebat, hoc est, cum iis concurrebat; annon videretur sequi, nullum esse malum, aut, ipsum Deum malum operari; hac nixus regula, quod nihil contra Dei voluntatem potest fieri, alioquin involveret imperfectionem, aut res, quas Deus operatur, (quibus etiam res, quas malas dicimus, comprehensae videntur) etiam deberent malae esse. Verum quia et hoc contradictionem includit, et, quocunque eam verterem modo, me a contradictione statuenda liberare non possem, propterea me ad te recipiebam, utpote optimum tuorum conceptuum interpretem.
First, according to the first rule laid down, I had asked1 whether, since according to your position to create and to preserve were one and the same, and since God made not only things but also the motions and modes of things persevere in their state, that is, concurred with them, it did not seem to follow that there is no evil, or that God himself works evil; relying on this rule, that nothing can happen against God’s will, since otherwise it would involve imperfection, or the things which God works (among which the things we call evil also seem to be included) would also have to be evil. But because this too includes a contradiction, and in whatever way I turned it I could not free myself from asserting a contradiction, I had recourse to you, as the best interpreter of your own concepts.
  1. 1 In Letter 18.
In responsione dicis, te persistere in prima sententia, nempe nihil contra Dei voluntatem vel fieri, vel posse fieri: Verum quum huic difficultati respondendum esset, annon igitur Deus nullum faciat malum, negas peccatum quid positivum esse, addisque, non nisi valde improprie dici posse nos in Deum, peccare; et appendicis parte I. Cap. 6. dicis, malum absolutum nullum dari, ut per se est manifestum: Nam quicquid existit, in se consideratum, sine respectu ad aliud quid, perfectionem includit, quae sese semper eo usque in quacunque re extendit, quo se extendit ipsa rei essentia, et propterea evidenter sequitur, peccata, quia ea nihil nisi imperfectionem denotant, non posse in aliquo, quod essentiam exprimit, consistere.
In your reply you say that you persist in your first opinion,2 namely that nothing either happens or can happen against God’s will. But when the difficulty had to be answered whether God then does no evil, you deny that sin is something positive, and add that only very improperly can we be said to sin against God; and in the Appendix, Part I, ch. 6, you say that ‘there is no absolute evil, as is self-evident’:3 ‘For whatever exists, considered in itself without relation to anything else, includes perfection, which always extends in each thing as far as the very essence of the thing extends; and therefore it evidently follows that sins, because they denote nothing but imperfection, cannot consist in something which expresses essence.’
  1. 2 Letter 19.
  2. 3 Metaphysical Thoughts I.6 (G I 247). What follows is pieced together from Letter 19.
Si peccatum, malum, error, vel quocunque id appelles nomine, nihil aliud est, quam perfectiorem statum amittere, aut eo privari, utique sequi videtur, τὸ existere non quidem malum, aut imperfectionem esse; sed aliquod malum in re existente posse oriri. Nam perfectum per aeque perfectam actionem perfectiore statu non privabitur; sed quidem per id, quod ad aliquod imperfectum, quia concessis nobis viribus abutimur, inclinamus.
If sin, evil, error, or whatever name you call it by, is nothing other than to lose a more perfect state, or to be deprived of it, it certainly seems to follow that to exist is not indeed evil or imperfection, but that some evil can arise in a thing that exists. For what is perfect will not be deprived of a more perfect state by an equally perfect action, but by our inclining to something imperfect, because we misuse the powers granted to us.
Hoc videris non malum; sed bonum minus appellare, quia res in se consideratae perfectionem includunt: deinde, quia rebus, ut ais, non plus essentiae competit, quam divinus intellectus, et potentia eis tribuit, et revera confert; et propterea non plus etiam existentiae in actionibus suis ostendere, quam essentiae accepere, possunt.
This you seem to call not evil but a lesser good, because things considered in themselves include perfection; and next because, as you say, no more essence belongs to things than the divine intellect and power attributes to them and really confers; and therefore they can show no more existence in their actions than the essence they have received.
Nam si nec plures, nec pauciores possum edere operationes, quam quantum essentiae accepi, nulla perfectioris status privatio fingi potest: si enim nihil contra Dei voluntatem fit, et si solummodo tantum fit, quantum essentiae collatum est, qua via malum, quod melioris conditionis privationem nuncupas, excogitari potest? Quomodo quis per statutum adeo, et dependens opus perfectiorem statum amittere potis est?
For if I can bring forth neither more nor fewer operations than the essence I have received, no privation of a more perfect state can be feigned: for if nothing happens against God’s will, and if only as much happens as there is essence conferred, by what way can evil, which you call the privation of a better condition, be thought up? How can anyone lose a more perfect state through a work so fixed and dependent?
Adeo ut mihi persuadeam, Virum clarissimum alterutrum debere statuere, vel aliquod esse malum, vel si minus, nullam esse posse melioris status privationem: Nam nullum esse malum et meliori conditione orbari contradictorium mihi videtur. At dices, per perfectioris status privationem ad minus quidem bonum; sed non ad absolutum malum recidimus; sed (Append. Part. 1. Cap. 3.) me docuisti, non esse de verbis litigandum.
So that I am persuaded that you, most distinguished Sir, must maintain one of two things: either that there is some evil, or, if not, that there can be no privation of a better state: for that there is no evil and that one is bereft of a better condition seem to me contradictory. But you will say that through the privation of a more perfect state we fall back into a lesser good, but not into absolute evil; but you have taught me (Appendix, Part I, ch. 3) that one should not quarrel about words.4
  1. 4 Metaphysical Thoughts I.3 (G I 242): ‘for I am not accustomed to dispute about names’.
Quare, an id absolutum, nec ne dicendum sit, iam non disputo; sed solum, annon a meliori in peiorem statum delabi apud nos iure peior status, aut status malus dicatur, et dici debeat. At regeres, malus ille status multum adhuc boni continet; ego vero interrogo, annon ille homo, qui per imprudentiam suam in causa fuit, quod perfectiore statu orbatus est, et per consequens nunc minor est, quam antea fuit, malus vocari queat.
Therefore I do not now dispute whether it should be called absolute or not; but only whether falling from a better into a worse state is not rightly called among us, and ought to be called, a worse state, or an evil state. But you will reply that this evil state still contains much good; I, however, ask whether the man who through his imprudence was the cause of his being bereft of a more perfect state, and is consequently now less than he was before, cannot be called evil.
[G IV 101] Ut autem antecedens ratiocinium, quia nonnullae circa id supersunt tibi difficultates, declines, affirmas malum quidem esse, et in Adamo fuisse, verum id non esse positivum quid, et respectu nostri, non autem Dei intellectus tale dici, nostrique respectu privationem, (sed tantum quatenus eo optima libertate, quae ad nostram naturam spectat, et in nostra potestate est, nosmet ipsos privamus) Dei vero respectu negationem esse. Hic autem examinemus, an illud, quod malum vocas, siquidem duntaxat nostri respectu malum foret, malum non esset; deinde an malum, ut tu vis, acceptum, Dei respectu tantum negatio dici debeat.
But to evade the preceding reasoning, since some difficulties about it remain for you, you affirm that there is indeed evil, and that it was in Adam, but that it is not something positive, and is so called in relation to our intellect, not God’s; and that in relation to us it is privation (‘but only insofar as we thereby deprive ourselves of the best freedom, which belongs to our nature and is in our power’1), but in relation to God negation. Here, however, let us examine whether what you call evil, if it were evil only in relation to us, would not be evil; and next whether evil, taken as you wish, should be called only negation in relation to God.
  1. 1 Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, Schol. (G I 175), which ends by calling the imperfection of error a privation in relation to man, but in relation to God a negation.
[G IV 102] Ad primum supra aliquo modo videor respondisse: et quamvis concederem, minus alio ente perfectum esse in me nullum ponere posse malum, eo quod meliorem statum a creatore flagitare nequeo, et tantum efficere, ut meus status gradibus differat; non tamen ideo potero concedere, et fateri, si iam imperfectior sum, quam ante fui, eamque imperfectionem mea culpa mihi creavi, me eatenus non tanto peiorem esse;a si, inquam, meipsum, antequam in imperfectionem unquam incideram, considero, et me cum aliis tunc maiore, quam ego praeditis perfectione, comparo, minor illa perfectio non malum; sed secundum gradus minus bonum erit: Si vero me ipsum, postquam perfectiore statu excidi, eoque propria orbatus imprudentia, cum prima mea forma, qua ex manu mei Creatoris prodii, et perfectior eram, comparo, me esse deteriorem, quam antea iudicare debeo: nam non creator; sed ego me ipsum eo redegi; vires enim mihi me ab errore praeservandi, uti et tu fateris, suppetebant.
To the first I seem to have answered in some way above; and although I would grant that to be less perfect than another being can posit no evil in me, because I cannot demand a better state from the creator, and it only makes my state differ by degrees; yet I shall not on that account be able to grant and confess, if I am now more imperfect than I was before and have created that imperfection for myself by my own fault, that I am not to that extent so much the worse; if, I say, I consider myself before I ever fell into imperfection, and compare myself with others then endowed with greater perfection than I, that lesser perfection will not be an evil but a lesser good according to degree; but if I compare myself, after I have fallen from a more perfect state and been bereft of it by my own imprudence, with my first form, in which I came forth from the hand of my Creator and was more perfect, I must judge that I am worse than before: for not the creator but I myself brought myself to this; for I had the powers to preserve myself from error, as you too confess.
  1. a So Gebhardt (G IV 102); the 1677 edition (OP p. 485) and Van Vloten and Land (VVL³ III 73) have eatenus tanto peiorem without non, which makes Blyenbergh deny what he goes on to assert. Gebhardt explains (G IV 397) that the Latin version, having changed the construction of the Dutch, ought to have added non, as Meijer had argued; the Dutch says that he would have to confess that he is so much the worse (G IV 102).
Quod secundum spectat, nempe an malum, quod statuis consistere in melioris status privatione, quem non tantum Adamus; sed et nos omnes subita, et inordinata nimis actione amisimus, an illud, inquam, respectu Dei mera sit negatio. Ut hoc vero sana mente examinemus, videndum nobis est, quomodo hominem constituas et a Deo ante omnem errorem dependentem facias, et quomodo post errorem eundem hominem constituas.
As regards the second: namely whether the evil which you hold to consist in the privation of a better state, which not only Adam but all of us lost by a too sudden and disordered action, whether that, I say, is a mere negation in relation to God. To examine this with a sound mind, we must see how you constitute man and make him dependent on God before all error, and how you constitute the same man after error.
Ante errorem eum describis, quod non plus ipsi essentiae competit, quam quantum ei divinus intellectus, et potentia tribuit, et revera confert, hoc est, (nisi a mente tua aberrem) quod homo nec plus, nec minus perfectionis potest habere, quam quantum essentiae Deus in ipso posuit: hoc vero est hominem tali modo a Deo dependentem facere, quemadmodum elementa, lapides, herbas, etc. Si autem haec tua sit sententia, quid sibi velint verba Princip. P. 1. Prop. 15. non percipio.
Before error you describe him as having no more essence than the divine intellect and power attributes to him and really confers; that is (unless I mistake your mind), that man can have neither more nor less perfection than the essence God has put in him: but this is to make man dependent on God in the same way as the elements, stones, plants, etc. But if this is your opinion, I do not understand what the words of the Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, mean.
Cum autem voluntas, inquis, libera sit ad se determinandum: sequitur nos potestatem habere facultatem assentiendi intra limites intellectus continendi, ac proinde efficiendi, ne in errorem incidamus. Annon videtur contradictio, voluntatem tam liberam, ut se ab errore possit praeservare, et una a Deo dependentem facere, ut nec plus, nec minus perfectionis queat proferre, quam quantum essentiae Deus ipsi dedit?
‘But since the will’, you say, ‘is free to determine itself, it follows that we have the power of containing the faculty of assenting within the limits of the intellect, and so of bringing it about that we do not fall into error.’1 Does it not seem a contradiction to make the will so free that it can preserve itself from error, and at the same time so dependent on God that it can bring forth neither more nor less perfection than the essence God has given it?
  1. 1 Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, Schol. (G I 174).
Ad secundum, nempe quomodo hominem constituis post errorem, dicis, hominem subita nimis actione, nimirum voluntatem non continendo intra intellectus limites, seipsum perfectiore conditione privare: verum mihi videtur, tibi et hic, et in principiis utraque huius privationis extrema propius fuisse explicanda, quid ante privationem possideret quidque post amissionem perfecti istius status (ut eum appellas) sibi servaret. Dicitur quidem quid perdiderimus; sed non quid retinuerimus Princ. Part. 1. Prop. 15. Tota igitur imperfectio erroris in sola optimae libertatis privatione consistet, quae error vocatur.
As to the second, namely how you constitute man after error, you say that man by too sudden an action, namely by not containing the will within the limits of the intellect, deprives himself of a more perfect condition; but it seems to me that both here and in the Principles you should have explained more closely both extremes of this privation: what he possessed before the privation, and what he kept for himself after the loss of that perfect state (as you call it). We are told indeed what we have lost, but not what we have retained (Principles, Part I, Prop. 15): ‘The whole imperfection of error, therefore, will consist solely in the privation of the best freedom, which is called error.’2
  1. 2 Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, Schol. (G I 175).
Examinemus utcumque hoc ea ratione, qua a te statuitur. Statuis non solum tam diversos cogitandi modos in nobis dari, quorum alios volendi, aliosque intelligendi nuncupamus; sed etiam inter eos talem esse ordinem, ut res non debeamus velle, antequam eas clare intelligamus; affirmas etiam, quod, si voluntatem nostram intra limites intellectus continemus, nunquam errabimus, et quod denique in nostra potestate est voluntatem intra limites intellectus continere.
Let us examine this, at any rate, in the way you lay it down. You maintain not only that there are in us such different modes of thinking, of which we call some willing and others understanding, but also that there is such an order between them that we ought not to will things before we understand them clearly; you affirm too that if we contain our will within the limits of the intellect we shall never err, and finally that it is in our power to contain the will within the limits of the intellect.
Cum haec serio mente agito, alterutrum ut verum sit, necessum est; vel omne id, quod ponitur, est fictum, vel Deus eundem ordinem nobis impressit. Si impressit, annon absurdum esset affirmare id sine fine factum esse Deumque, ut ullum ordinem observemus et sequamur, non exigere: nam illud in Deo contradictionem poneret.
When I turn this over seriously in my mind, one of two things must be true: either everything that is laid down is feigned, or God has impressed this same order upon us. If he has impressed it, would it not be absurd to affirm that this was done to no purpose, and that God does not require us to observe and follow any order? For that would posit a contradiction in God.
Et si ordinem in nobis positum debemus observare, quomodo adeo a Deo dependentes possumus et esse, et manere: si enim nemo nec plus, nec minus perfectionis habet, quam quantum essentiae accepit, et si haec vis ex effectibus debeat cognosci, ille, qui voluntatem suam ultra limites intellectus extendit, non tantum virium a Deo accepit, alioquin eas in effectum deduceret; et per consequens is, qui errat, perfectionem non errandi a Deo non accepit, alias nunquam erraret. Nam secundum te tantum essentiae datum est, quantum perfectionis efficitur.
And if we must observe the order placed in us, how can we be and remain so dependent on God? For if no one has more or less perfection than the essence he has received, and if this power must be known from its effects, then he who extends his will beyond the limits of the intellect has not received so much power from God, otherwise he would bring it into effect; and consequently he who errs has not received from God the perfection of not erring, otherwise he would never err. For according to you as much essence has been given as perfection is brought about.
Deinde si Deus nobis tantum essentiae tribuit, ut eum observare queamus ordinem, ut tu nos eum posse servare affirmas, et si tantum perfectionis producimus, quantum essentiae nacti sumus, qui fit, ut eum transgrediamur, qui, ut eum possimus transgredi ordinem, utque voluntatem non semper intra limites intellectus contineamus.
Next, if God has given us so much essence that we can observe that order, as you affirm that we can keep it, and if we produce as much perfection as the essence we have obtained, how does it come about that we transgress it, that we can transgress that order, and that we do not always contain the will within the limits of the intellect.
Tertio, si a Deo tantopere dependeo, ut supra te statuere ostendi, ut nec intra, nec extra limites intellectus voluntatem continere possim, nisi mihi Deus in antecessum tantum dederit essentiae, et sua voluntate prius alterutrum determinaverit: quomodo ergo mihi, si hoc penitius consideremus, voluntatis libertas usu venire potest.
Third, if I depend on God so much, as I have shown above that you hold, that I can contain the will neither within nor beyond the limits of the intellect unless God has given me so much essence beforehand and has first determined the one or the other by his will: how then, if we consider this more deeply, can freedom of the will fall to me.
Nonne in Deo contradictionem ponere videtur, nobis praescribere ordinem nostram voluntatem intra limites nostri intellectus continendi, et non praebere tantum essentiae, vel perfectionum, ut eum observemus; et si, iuxta tuam sententiam, tantum perfectionis nobis concessit, nunquam profecto errare possemus: quantum enim essentiae possidemus, tantum perfectionis producere oportet, semperque concessas vires in nostris operationibus ostendere: nostri autem errores sunt argumentum, nos eiusmodi potentiam a Deo ita dependentem (ut statuis) non possidere; adeo ut alterutrum verum esse debeat, vel nos non adeo Deo dependere, vel nos in nobis potentiam non errandi non habere; at potentiam non errandi habemus, prout statuis. Ergo a Deo non adeo dependemus.
Does it not seem to posit a contradiction in God, to prescribe to us the order of containing our will within the limits of our intellect, and not to provide essence or perfection enough for us to observe it; and if, according to your opinion, he has granted us so much perfection, we could certainly never err: for as much essence as we possess, so much perfection we must produce, and always show in our operations the powers granted; but our errors are proof that we do not possess such a power so dependent on God (as you hold); so that one of two things must be true: either we do not depend on God so much, or we do not have in us the power of not erring; but we do have the power of not erring, as you hold. Therefore we do not depend on God so much.
Ex dictis iam clare patere videtur, impossibile esse, ut malum aut meliori statu privari respectu Dei esset negatio. Quid enim significat privari, aut perfectiorem statum amittere? nonne est a maiori ad minorem perfectionem, et per consequens a maiori ad minorem essentiam transire; et a Deo in certa mensura perfectionis, et essentiae collocari? nonne est velle, nos alium, praeter sui perfectam notitiam, non posse acquirere statum, nisi aliter decrevisset, ac voluisset?
From what has been said it now seems clearly evident that it is impossible that evil, or being deprived of a better state, should be a negation in relation to God. For what does it mean to be deprived, or to lose a more perfect state? Is it not to pass from greater to lesser perfection, and consequently from greater to lesser essence, and to be placed by God in a certain measure of perfection and essence? Is it not his willing that we can acquire no other state outside his perfect knowledge, unless he had decreed and willed otherwise?
Fierine potest, ut illa creatura ab omniscio, et summe perfecto ente producta, quod voluit, ut talem essentiae statum retineret, imo, cum qua Deus, ut eam in eo statu retineret, continuo concurrit; ut, inquam, in essentia declinaret, hoc est, in perfectione praeter Dei notitiam minor fieret? Haec res absurditatem involvere videtur.
Can it be that this creature, produced by an omniscient and supremely perfect being which willed that it should retain such a state of essence, indeed, with which God continually concurs in order to keep it in that state, can it be, I say, that it should decline in essence, that is, become less in perfection, without God’s knowledge? This seems to involve absurdity.
Annon absurdum est dicere, Adamum perfectiorem amisisse conditionem, et consequenter ineptum fuisse ad eum ordinem, quem Deus in anima eius posuerat, Deumque nullam habere notitiam, qualis, quantaeque perfectionis Adamus iacturam fecerat? Potestne comprehendi, Deum ens aliquod constituere ita dependens, ut non nisi eiusmodi opus produceret, et tum propter illud opus perfectiorem perderet statum: (praeterquam quod eius absoluta esset causa) et tamen nullam eius haberet notitiam?
Is it not absurd to say that Adam lost a more perfect condition, and was consequently unfit for the order which God had placed in his soul, and that God has no knowledge of what kind and how great a perfection Adam had lost? Can it be understood that God should constitute some being so dependent that it could produce no work but such a one, and that it should then lose a more perfect state because of that work (besides the fact that he would be its absolute cause), and yet that he should have no knowledge of it?
Concedo inter actum, et malum actui adhaerens dari discrimen; sed vero malum respectu Dei esse negationem superat meum captum. Deum actum scire, eundem determinare, et cum eo concurrere, et tamen malum, quod in illo actu est, eiusque exitum non cognoscere, mihi in Deo impossibile videtur.
I grant that there is a distinction between the act and the evil adhering to the act; but that evil should be a negation in relation to God surpasses my understanding. That God should know the act, determine it and concur with it, and yet not know the evil which is in that act, and its outcome, seems to me impossible in God.
Adverte mecum, Deum in actu meo progignendi cum uxore mea concurrere: est enim positivum quid, et per consequens claram eius habet Deus scientiam; verum quatenus eo actu abutor, et cum alterius uxore contra datam fidem, et iusiurandum meum rem habeo, illum actum malum comitatur. Quid iam hic respectu Dei esset negativum? non quod actum progignendi faciam: quatenus etenim is positivus est, cum eodem Deus concurrit. Debet ergo istud malum, quod illum actum comitatur, tantummodo esse, quod ego contra proprium foedus, vel Dei mandatum cum aliena congredior, cum qua non licet.
Consider with me: God concurs in my act of begetting with my wife; for it is something positive, and consequently God has clear knowledge of it; but insofar as I misuse this act, and have to do with another man’s wife against my given faith and oath, evil accompanies that act. What here would now be negative in relation to God? Not that I perform the act of begetting: for insofar as it is positive, God concurs with it. This evil, then, which accompanies that act, must be only this: that against my own covenant, or God’s command, I go with another’s wife, with whom it is not lawful.
Iam vero, potestne capi, Deum nostras scire actiones, cum iis concurrere, et tamen ignorare, quacum illam actionem producimus, eo magis quod Deus etiam cum illius foeminae actione, cum qua rem habebam, concurrit? Id de Deo sentire durum videtur. Attende occidendi actum; quatenus est actus positivus, cum eo Deus concurrit; sed vero istius actionis effectum, nempe alicuius entis destructionem, et Dei creaturae dissolutionem anne ignoraret? quasi proprium opus Deo foret incognitum.
Now can it be grasped that God knows our actions and concurs with them, and yet does not know with whom we produce that action, the more so since God also concurs with the action of the woman with whom I had to do? It seems hard to think this of God. Consider the act of killing: insofar as it is a positive act, God concurs with it; but would he be ignorant of the effect of this action, namely the destruction of some being and the dissolution of God’s creature? As if his own work were unknown to God.
(Metuo ne mentem tuam bene percipiam:b Conceptus quippe tui, quam ut tam foedum committas errorem, perspicaciores sunt) fortasse instabis, eos actus, prout eos pono, mere esse bonos, et nullum eos comitari malum; sed tum, quid malum nuncupes, capere nequeo, quod perfectioris conditionis privationem sequitur; et tum mundus in aeterna, et perpetua poneretur confusione, nosque bestiis similes redderemur. Vide quaeso, quam utilitatem ea sententia mundo conferret.
(I fear that I do not perceive your mind well: for your concepts are too perspicacious for you to commit so foul an error.) Perhaps you will insist that these acts, as I put them, are purely good, and that no evil accompanies them; but then I cannot grasp what you call the evil which follows the privation of a more perfect condition; and then the world would be placed in eternal and perpetual confusion, and we should be made like the beasts. See, I pray, what benefit this opinion would bring the world.
  1. b Strictly, metuo ne … percipiam means ‘I fear that I do perceive’; the sense requires the negative, which the Dutch has: ‘ick vreese dat ick hier U E meeninge niet wel moet verstaen’ (G IV 109).
[G IV 109] Vulgarem hominis descriptionem reiicis, et unicuique homini tantum tribuis perfectionis, quantum Deus ipsi, ut operetur, largitus est. Sed ea ratione mihi videris statuere impios Deum aeque, ac pios, suis operibus colere: Quare? quia utrique perfectiora nequeunt producere opera, quam quantum utrisque essentiae datum est, et suis demonstrant effectibus.
You reject the common description of man, and attribute to each man as much perfection as God has bestowed on him for acting. But in that way you seem to me to maintain that the impious worship God by their works as much as the pious: Why? Because neither can produce more perfect works than the essence given to each, as they show by their effects.
Nec mihi quaestioni satisfacere in secunda responsione videris, dicendo, quo res aliqua plus perfectionis habet, eo etiam magis de Deitate participat, Deique perfectionem exprimit magis. Quum ergo probi inaestimabiliter plus perfectionis, quam improbi habeant, virtus eorum cum improborum virtute comparari nequit. Improbi, quia Deum non cognoscunt, non sunt nisi instrumentum in manu artificis, quod inscium servit et serviendo consumitur: Probi contra conscii serviunt, et serviendo perfectiores evadunt.
Nor do you seem to me to satisfy the question in your second answer,1 by saying: ‘the more perfection a thing has, the more it also participates in the Deity, and the more it expresses God’s perfection. Since, then, the good have incomparably more perfection than the wicked, their virtue cannot be compared with the virtue of the wicked. The wicked, because they do not know God, are nothing but a tool in the hand of the craftsman, which serves without knowing it and is used up in serving: the good, on the contrary, serve knowingly, and become more perfect by serving.’
  1. 1 Letter 19.
In utrisque tamen hoc verum est, eos amplius non posse operari; quanto etenim plus perfectionis hic prae illo efficit, tanto plus essentiae unus prae alio accepit. Annon igitur exigua sua perfectione impii aeque, ac pii, Deum colunt? Nam, secundum tuam sententiam, nihil amplius ab impiis Deus flagitat, alioquin plus essentiae in eos contulisset; sed vero plus essentiae non dedit, uti ex effectibus liquet. Ergo amplius ab eis non petit.
Yet in both this is true, that they cannot do more; for the more perfection the one brings about compared with the other, the more essence the one has received compared with the other. Do not the impious, then, with their slight perfection, worship God as much as the pious? For according to your opinion God demands nothing more from the impious, otherwise he would have conferred more essence on them; but he has not given more essence, as is clear from the effects. Therefore he does not ask more of them.
Et siquando unusquisque in specie, nec plus, nec minus, quam Deus vult, facit, cur is, qui parum operatur, sed tamen tantum, quantum Deus ab ipso exigit, Deo aeque, ac probus, non esset acceptus?
And if each one in particular does neither more nor less than God wills, why should he who does little, but yet as much as God requires of him, not be as acceptable to God as the good man?
Insuper, quemadmodum per malum, quod actum comitatur, nostra imprudentia, ex tua sententia, perfectiorem perdimus statum, ita et hic videris statuere, quod, continendo voluntatem intra limites intellectus, non tantum adeo perfecti manemus, ac sumus; sed quod praeterea serviendo perfectiores evadimus; quod contradictionem involvere mihi persuadeo, si modo tantopere a Deo dependemus, ut nec plus, nec minus perfectionis queamus efficere, quam quantum essentiae accepimus, hoc est, quam Deus voluit, ut tunc per imprudentiam peiores, aut per prudentiam meliores fiamus. Adeo ut nihil aliud statuere videris, quam, si homo talis sit, ac eum describis, impios suis operibus, aeque, ac pios suis, Deum colere, et hac ratione ita, ut elementa, herbae, lapides etc. a Deo dependentes reddimur.
Moreover, just as through the evil which accompanies the act we lose a more perfect state by our imprudence, according to your opinion, so here too you seem to maintain that by containing the will within the limits of the intellect we not only remain as perfect as we are, but besides become more perfect by serving; which I am persuaded involves a contradiction, if only we depend on God so much that we can bring about neither more nor less perfection than the essence we have received, that is, than God has willed, so that we should then become worse through imprudence or better through prudence. So that you seem to maintain nothing other than that, if man is such as you describe him, the impious worship God by their works as much as the pious by theirs, and in this way we are made dependent on God as the elements, plants, stones, etc. are.
Cui rei igitur inserviet noster intellectus? cui voluntatem intra limites intellectus continendi potestas? qua de causa ordo ille nobis infixus est? Et vide, quaeso ab alia parte, qua re nos privemus, nempe anxia, et seria meditatione ad nos ipsos secundum regulam perfectionis Dei, et ordinem nobis impressum perfectos reddendum: nosmet precatione, et ad Deum suspiriis, quibus tam saepe extraordinariam confortationem accepisse percepimus, orbamus: nosmet tota religione, ac omni illa spe, et acquiescentia, quam ex precibus, et religione speramus, orbamus.
To what purpose, then, will our intellect serve? To what purpose the power of containing the will within the limits of the intellect? For what reason has that order been fixed in us? And see, I pray, on the other side, of what we deprive ourselves: namely, of anxious and serious meditation on making ourselves perfect according to the rule of God’s perfection and the order impressed on us; we bereave ourselves of prayer and of sighs to God, by which we have so often perceived that we received extraordinary strengthening; we bereave ourselves of all religion, and of all the hope and contentment which we hope for from prayers and religion.
Enimvero, si nullam mali habet Deus cognitionem, multo minus credibile est, eum malum puniturum. Quaenam ergo supersunt rationes, quo minus facinora quaevis (si modo iudicem effugio) avide perpetrem? cur non detestandis mediis divitias mihi acquiro? cur absque discrimine, et, quo caro nos trahit, id, quod lubet, non perpetro?
Indeed, if God has no knowledge of evil, it is much less credible that he will punish evil. What reasons, then, remain why I should not eagerly commit any crimes (if only I escape the judge)? Why should I not acquire riches by detestable means? Why should I not do whatever I please, without distinction, wherever the flesh draws us?
Dices, quia virtus propter se est amanda. Qui vero virtutem amare possum? mihi tantum essentiae, ac perfectionis non est tributum; et si tantum acquiescentiae, et ex hoc et ex illo habere licet, cur mihi vim, ut voluntatem intra limites intellectus contineam, infero? cur id non facio, quo me affectus trahunt? cur non hominem, qui mihi alicubi obest, clam occido? etc.
You will say: because virtue is to be loved for its own sake.2 But how can I love virtue? So much essence and perfection has not been given me; and if I may have as much contentment from the one as from the other, why should I do violence to myself to contain the will within the limits of the intellect? Why should I not do what my affects draw me to? Why should I not secretly kill a man who stands in my way somewhere? etc.
  1. 2 Spinoza answers in Letter 21. Compare Part IV, Prop. 18, Schol.: ‘virtue is to be wanted for its own sake’, and Part V, Prop. 41, Schol..
Ecce quam cunctis impiis, et impietati demus occasionem? nos truncis, omnesque nostras actiones motibus horologiorum similes reddimus.
See what occasion we give to all the impious and to impiety? We make ourselves like tree-trunks, and all our actions like the motions of clocks.
Ex dictis valde durum mihi videtur, quod improprie tantum in Deum peccare possimus dici: cui enim rei data nobis voluntatem intra limites intellectus continendi potentia inservit, si, ubi eam transgredimur, contra eum peccamus ordinem? Regeres forte, id non esse peccatum in Deum; sed in nos ipsos: nam si proprie erga Deum peccare diceremur, etiam dicendum esset, aliquid contra Dei voluntatem fieri; quod iuxta te est impossibile: Ergo etiam peccatum.
From what has been said it seems to me very hard that we can be said to sin against God only improperly: for to what purpose does the power given us of containing the will within the limits of the intellect serve, if, when we transgress it, we sin against that order? You will perhaps reply that it is not a sin against God but against ourselves: for if we were said to sin properly against God, it would also have to be said that something happens against God’s will; which according to you is impossible: therefore so is sin.
Interim alterutrum verum sit necesse est, Deum vel velle, vel nolle. Si vult, quomodo nostri respectu malum esse potest? Si non vult, id, ex tua opinione, non fieret. Quamvis autem hoc aliquam, ut tua fert sententia, involveret absurditatem, praedictas tamen absurditates admittere periculosissimum videtur. Quis novit, si anxie inquirerem, annon remedium posset inveniri his aliquo modo conciliandis.
Meanwhile, one of two things must be true: God either wills it or does not will it. If he wills it, how can it be evil in relation to us? If he does not will it, on your opinion it would not happen. But although this, as your opinion has it, would involve some absurdity, it still seems most dangerous to admit the absurdities stated above. Who knows, if I were to inquire anxiously, whether a remedy could not be found for reconciling these in some way.
[G IV 113] Atque sic tuae epistolae examen iuxta regulam meam primam generalem finiam: antequam vero ad examen iuxta secundam regulam accedam, duo adhuc proponam, quae epistolam tuam spectant, quaeque scripsisti Princ. Part. 1. Prop. 15 prius est, quod affirmas, nos potestatem volendi, et iudicandi intra limites intellectus retinere posse: quod absolute concedere nequeo.
And so I shall finish the examination of your letter according to my first general rule; but before I come to the examination according to the second rule, I shall put forward two more points which concern your letter and what you wrote in the Principles, Part I, Prop. 15. The first is that you affirm that we can keep the power of willing and judging within the limits of the intellect: which I cannot grant absolutely.
Nam hoc si foret verum, certe ex innumerabilibus vel unus reperiretur homo, qui se ea praeditum potestate ostenderet: etiam quivis apud se experiri potest, se quantas etiam impendat vires, eum scopum attingere non posse. Et si quis de hoc negotio dubitat, seipsum examinet, quoties invito intellectu passiones rationem suam vincant, etiam tum quum summis obnititur viribus.
For if this were true, surely among countless men at least one would be found who showed himself endowed with that power; and anyone can find by experience in himself that, however much force he applies, he cannot reach that goal. And if anyone doubts this matter, let him examine himself, how often, against the will of his intellect, the passions conquer his reason, even when he resists with all his might.
At dices, quod hoc minus praestamus, non est, quia nobis impossibile est; sed quia sufficientem non adhibemus diligentiam; regero, quod, si hoc possibile esset, saltem ex tot millibus vel unus inveniretur; sed ne unus omnium hominum exstitit, vel existit, qui auderet gloriari, se in errores haud incidisse. Quaenam vero huius rei certiora, quam ipsa exempla, possunt adduci argumenta? Si pauci essent, unus esset; iam vero quum nullus sit, nulla eius quoque datur probatio.
But you will say that our failing to do this is not because it is impossible for us, but because we do not apply sufficient diligence; I reply that if this were possible, at least one out of so many thousands would be found; but not one of all men has existed or exists who would dare to boast that he has not fallen into errors. And what more certain arguments for this can be brought than the examples themselves? If there were a few, there would be one; but now, since there is none, there is no proof of it either.
Instabis et dices; si fieri potest, ut iudicium suspendendo, et voluntatem intra terminos intellectus continendo, semel, ne errem, efficere queo, quare, ubi eandem adhibeo diligentiam, semper id efficere non possem? Respondeo, me videre non posse, nos hodie tantum virium habere, ut in eo semper perseveremus; semel una hora, quando omnes intendo nervos, iter duorum milliarium conficere potis sum, verum semper id praestare non possum; ita me summo studio ab errore semel praeservare possum, sed vires ad id semper praestandum deficiunt.
You will insist and say: if it can happen that by suspending judgment and containing the will within the bounds of the intellect I can once bring it about that I do not err, why could I not always bring it about when I apply the same diligence? I answer that I cannot see that we today have so much strength that we can always persevere in it; once, in one hour, when I strain every nerve, I am able to cover a journey of two miles, but I cannot always do it; so I can once preserve myself from error with the greatest effort, but the strength to do so always is lacking.
Clarum mihi videtur, primum hominem, e manu perfecti illius artificis prodeuntem, iis praeditum fuisse viribus; sed (ut in hoc tecum sentio) eum, illis viribus non satis utendo, vel abutendo, statum suum amisisse perfectum ad praestandum, quod antea sui erat arbitrii. Haecque multis rationibus, ni nimius essem, firmarem.
It seems clear to me that the first man, coming forth from the hand of that perfect craftsman, was endowed with those powers; but (as I agree with you in this) that by not using those powers enough, or by misusing them, he lost his perfect state for doing what had before been within his choice. And this I would confirm by many reasons, if I were not being too long.
Et hoc in negotio omnem Sacrae Scripturae essentiam, quae propterea apud nos in honore esse debet, sitam esse opinor, quoniam nos id, quod tam clare intellectus noster naturalis confirmat, docet: nempe, lapsum ex nostra prima perfectione nostra imprudentia factum esse. Quid igitur istius lapsus emendatione magis necessarium? Atque hominem lapsum ad Deum reducere Sacrae Scripturae unicus quoque est scopus.
And in this matter I think the whole essence of Holy Scripture lies, which is why it ought to be held in honour among us, since it teaches us what our natural intellect so clearly confirms: namely, that the fall from our first perfection came about through our imprudence. What, then, is more necessary than the amendment of this fall? And to bring fallen man back to God is also the sole aim of Holy Scripture.
Posterius est, Princip. Part. 1. Prop. 15. quod affirmas, res clare et distincte intelligere repugnare naturae hominis, unde tandem concludis, longe melius esse rebus, quamvis confusis assentiri, et libertatem exercere, quam semper indifferentem, hoc est, in infimo gradu libertatis manere.
The second is what you affirm in the Principles, Part I, Prop. 15: that to understand things clearly and distinctly is contrary to the nature of man;1 from which you finally conclude that it is far better to assent to things, even if confused, and to exercise freedom, than always to remain indifferent, that is, in the lowest degree of freedom.
  1. 1 The Principles (Part I, Prop. 15, Schol., G I 175) draws this conclusion only on the supposition ‘that it is contrary to man’s nature to perceive things clearly and distinctly’.
Quam ut concedam conclusionem, impedit in me obscuritas: nam suspensum iudicium nos in eo statu, in quo a creatore sumus creati, conservat; verum rebus confuse est rebus non intellectis assentiri,a et ita aeque facile vero ac falso assentiri.
An obscurity in me prevents me from granting this conclusion: for suspended judgment keeps us in the state in which we were created by the creator; but to assent to things confusedly is to assent to things not understood, and so to assent as easily to the false as to the true.
  1. a So the 1677 edition (OP p. 492) and Gebhardt, evidently for rebus confuse assentiri est rebus non intellectis assentiri; the translation gives that sense.
Et si (ut D. Des Cartes alicubi docet) eum ordinem in assentiendo non usurpamus, quem Deus inter nostrum intellectum, nostramque voluntatem constituit, nempe ut non nisi clare perceptis assentiamur: quamvis tunc casu in veritatem incidamus, tamen quia verum non eo, quo Deus voluit ordine, amplectimur, peccamus; et per consequens, ut assensus cohibitio nos in statu, in quo a Deo constituti sumus, conservat, ita confusus assensus nostrum statum peiorem reddit; erroris quippe ponit fundamentum, quo perfectum deinceps amittimus statum.
