And if (as Mr Descartes teaches somewhere2) we do not use in assenting the order which God has established between our intellect and our will, namely that we assent only to things clearly perceived, then, although we may by chance hit upon the truth, yet because we do not embrace the truth in the order God willed, we sin; and consequently, just as the withholding of assent keeps us in the state in which we were placed by God, so confused assent makes our state worse; for it lays the foundation of error, through which we then lose our perfect state.
  1. 2 Descartes, Meditations IV (AT VII 60): if I affirm what I do not clearly perceive and it is true, ‘I shall indeed hit upon the truth by chance, but I shall not on that account be free of blame, since it is manifest by the natural light that the perception of the intellect must always precede the determination of the will’.
Sed te dicentem audio: nonne praestat, ut nos perfectiores reddamus, rebus licet confusis assentiendo, quam ut non assentiendo semper in infimo perfectionis, et libertatis gradu maneamus? At praeterquam quod hoc negaverimus, et aliquo modo ostenderimus, nos nosmet non meliores; sed deteriores reddidisse; nobis etiam impossibile, et quasi contradictio videtur, Deum cognitionem rerum, ab ipso determinatarum, longius extendere, ac eam, quam nobis dedit; imo Deum tunc absolutam nostrorum errorum causam involvere.
But I hear you saying: is it not better to make ourselves more perfect by assenting to things, though confused, than by not assenting always to remain in the lowest degree of perfection and freedom? But besides the fact that we have denied this, and have shown in some way that we have made ourselves not better but worse, it also seems to us impossible, and as it were a contradiction, that God should extend the knowledge of things determined by him further than the knowledge which he has given us; indeed, that God would then be involved as the absolute cause of our errors.
Nec huic contrarium est, quod nos Deum, ut plura in nos conferret, quam contulit, non possumus accusare; quia ad id non tenebatur. Verum quidem est, Deum non teneri, ut plura, quam dedit, daret; sed summa Dei etiam infert perfectio, ut creatura ab ipso procedens nullam contradictionem involveret, prout tum videretur sequi.
Nor is it contrary to this that we cannot accuse God of not conferring more on us than he has conferred, because he was not bound to do so. It is true indeed that God is not bound to give more than he has given; but God’s supreme perfection also implies that a creature proceeding from him should involve no contradiction, as would then seem to follow.
Nullibi namque in creata natura, praeterquam in nostro intellectu, deprehendimus scientiam. In quem alium finem ille nobis concessus esset, nisi ut Dei opera contemplaremur et cognosceremus? Et quidnam evidentius sequi videtur, quam quod harmonia inter res cognoscendas, nostrumque intellectum dari debet?
For nowhere in created nature, except in our intellect, do we find knowledge. To what other end would it have been granted us, unless that we should contemplate and know God’s works? And what seems to follow more evidently than that there must be harmony between the things to be known and our intellect?
Si tuam epistolam circa ea, quae modo diximus, ad meam secundam regulam generalem examinarem, magis, quam in prima, discreparemus. Mihi namque videtur, (aberranti commonstra viam) te Sacrae Scripturae eam infallibilem veritatem et divinitatem non adscribere, quam ego in ea esse mihi persuadeo. Verum quidem est, quod te credere ais, Deum res Sacrae Scripturae prophetis revelasse; sed modo adeo imperfecto, ut, si factum est, prout tu statuis, in Deo contradictionem involveret.
If I were to examine your letter, as regards what we have just said, by my second general rule, we should differ more than under the first. For it seems to me (show the way to one who strays) that you do not ascribe to Holy Scripture the infallible truth and divinity which I am persuaded is in it. It is true indeed that you say you believe that God revealed the things of Holy Scripture to the prophets; but in so imperfect a manner3 that, if it happened as you maintain, it would involve a contradiction in God.
  1. 3 Spinoza had said that the prophets devised parables (Letter 19).
Si enim Deus suum verbum, ac voluntatem hominibus manifestavit, certum in finem, idque clare iis manifestavit. Si iam prophetae ex illo verbo, quod acceperant, Parabolam finxissent, id Deus vel voluisset, vel noluisset. Si voluisset, ut exin Parabolam fingerent, hoc est, ut ab eius mente aberrarent; Deus istius erroris esset causa, aliquidque quod contrariaretur sibi, vellet. Si noluisset, impossibile erat, ut prophetae inde Parabolam effingerent.
For if God has manifested his word and will to men, he has manifested it to a certain end, and clearly. If, then, the prophets had devised a parable from the word they had received, God either willed it or did not will it. If he had willed that they should devise a parable from it, that is, that they should stray from his mind, God would be the cause of that error, and would will something contrary to himself. If he had not willed it, it was impossible for the prophets to devise a parable from it.
Praeterea credibile est, si supponitur, Deum verbum suum prophetis dedisse, quod ita eis dedit, ut illud accipiendo non erraverint: nam Deus in verbo suo revelando certum finem habere debebat; at sibi finem, ut homines in errorem duceret, proponere nequit: Id enim in Deo esset contradictio. Etiam homo contra Dei voluntatem errare non poterat; nam id, secundum te, fieri nequit.
Moreover, it is credible, if it is supposed that God gave his word to the prophets, that he gave it to them in such a way that they did not err in receiving it: for God must have had a certain end in revealing his word; but he cannot set himself the end of leading men into error: for that would be a contradiction in God. Nor could man err against God’s will; for that, according to you, cannot happen.
Praeter haec omnia, de illo summe perfecto Deo credi non potest, eum permittere, ut suo verbo, prophetis dato, ad illud populo explicandum, alius a prophetis tribueretur sensus, quam Deus voluit: Si enim statuimus, Deum prophetis suum concessisse verbum, simul asserimus, Deum modo extraordinario prophetis apparuisse, vel cum iis loquutum fuisse.
Besides all this, it cannot be believed of that supremely perfect God that he would allow a sense other than he willed to be given by the prophets to his word, given to the prophets for explaining it to the people: for if we maintain that God granted his word to the prophets, we assert at the same time that God appeared to the prophets in an extraordinary way, or spoke with them.
Si iam prophetae de hoc tradito verbo Parabolam fingunt, hoc est, alium ei sensum tribuunt, quam, ut tribuerent, voluit, Deus hanc rem eos edocuisset. Est etiam tam respectu prophetarum impossibile, quam respectu Dei contradictio, prophetas alium sensum potuisse habere, quam eos habere voluit Deus.
If, then, the prophets devise a parable from this word handed down to them, that is, give it a sense other than he willed them to give, God would have taught them this. It is also as impossible with respect to the prophets as it is a contradiction with respect to God that the prophets could have had a sense other than God willed them to have.
[G IV 119] Deum suum ita revelasse verbum, uti tu vis, parum probas, nempe eum salutem, et perditionem tantum revelasse, certa eum in finem decrevisse media, atque salutem, ac perditionem non nisi decretorum mediorum effectus esse.
That God revealed his word in the way you would have it, namely that he revealed only salvation and perdition, that he decreed certain means to that end, and that salvation and perdition are nothing but the effects of the decreed means, you prove poorly.1
  1. 1 See Letter 19.
Nam profecto, si prophetae Dei verbum isthoc sensu acceperant, quas rationes, ad alium illi adscribendum sensum, habuissent? Sed et nullam adducis probationem, qua nos, ut hanc tuam supra prophetarum sententiam poneremus, convinceres.
For surely, if the prophets had received God’s word in this sense, what reasons would they have had for ascribing another sense to it? But you also bring no proof by which to convince us to set this opinion of yours above that of the prophets.
Si vero existimas, hanc esse probationem, quod alioquin illud verbum multas imperfectiones, et contrarietates includeret, dico, hoc duntaxat dici, non vero probari. Et quis novit, si uterque sensus produceretur, uter minores includeret imperfectiones? Denique Enti summe perfecto bene perspectum erat, quid populus caperet, quaeque optima esset methodus, iuxta quam populus institui deberet.
And if you think the proof is that otherwise that word would include many imperfections and contrarieties, I say that this is merely said, not proved. And who knows, if both senses were brought forward, which would include fewer imperfections? Finally, the supremely perfect Being well understood what the people could grasp, and what was the best method by which the people ought to be instructed.
[G IV 120] Quantum ad secundum primae tuae quaestionis membrum attinet, quaeris ex te ipso, quare Deus Adamo, ne ex arbore ederet, cum tamen contrarium decrevisset, edixit; respondes vero, edictum Adamo factum in hoc solo consistere, quod nimirum Deus Adamo revelavit τὸ edere ex arbore mortis esse causam, quemadmodum nobis per naturalem rationem venenum mortiferum esse revelat.
As regards the second part of your first question, you ask yourself why God commanded Adam not to eat from the tree when he had nevertheless decreed the contrary; and you answer that ‘the command given to Adam consisted only in this, that God revealed to Adam that eating from the tree was the cause of death, just as he reveals to us by natural reason that poison is deadly’.1
  1. 1 Letter 19.
Si statuitur, Deum aliquid Adamo interdixisse, quaenam rationes sunt, quo magis crederem modum prohibitionis, quem tu adfers, quam quem adducunt prophetae, quibus Deus ipse modum prohibitionis revelavit? Dices, mea prohibendi ratio magis naturalis est, et ideo magis veritati, Deoque conveniens. Sed hoc omne nego.
If it is maintained that God forbade Adam something, what reasons are there why I should believe the manner of prohibition which you bring forward rather than the one which the prophets adduce, to whom God himself revealed the manner of prohibition? You will say: my account of the prohibition is more natural, and therefore more in keeping with truth and with God. But all this I deny.
Nec capio, quod Deus per naturalem intellectum nobis, venenum lethale esse, revelavit; nec rationes video, quibus unquam scirem, aliquid esse venenatum, nisi malos veneni effectus in aliis vidissem, et audivissem. Quod homines, quia venenum non noscunt, inscii edant et moriantur, quotidiana nos docet experientia.
Nor do I grasp that God has revealed to us through the natural intellect that poison is deadly; nor do I see reasons by which I would ever know that something is poisonous, unless I had seen and heard of the bad effects of poison in others. That men, because they do not know poison, eat it unknowingly and die, daily experience teaches us.
Dices, si homines scirent, id venenum esse, etiam malum id esse, eos non lateret; sed respondeo neminem veneni habere notitiam, aut posse habere; nisi qui vidit, aut audivit, aliquem eius usu damnum sibi dedisse; et si ponimus, nos ad hunc usque diem nunquam audivisse, vel vidisse, aliquem eius usu sibi nocuisse, non tantum nunc quoque ignoraremus; sed et sine metu in proprium damnum id usurparemus, quemadmodum tales veritates indies docemur.
You will say that if men knew that it is poison, it would not be hidden from them that it is also evil; but I answer that no one has, or can have, knowledge of poison except one who has seen or heard that someone has done himself harm by using it; and if we suppose that up to this day we had never heard or seen that anyone had harmed himself by using it, not only would we still be ignorant of it now, but we would also use it to our own harm without fear, as we are taught such truths daily.
[G IV 121] Quid magis in hac vita candidum atque erectum animum, quam illius perfectae Deitatis contemplatio, delectat? Nam quemadmodum circa perfectissimum versatur, ita etiam perfectissimum, quod in finitum nostrum intellectum cadere potest, involvere debet. Nec mihi in vita quicquam est, quod cum ea mutarem delectatione.
What delights a candid and upright mind more in this life than the contemplation of that perfect Deity? For as it is concerned with what is most perfect, so it must also involve the most perfect that can fall within our finite intellect. Nor is there anything in my life for which I would exchange that delight.
In hac, caelesti instigatus appetitu, multum temporis insumere possum; sed et simul tristitia affici, quando intueor tam multa finito meo intellectui deesse: verum tristitiam sedo spe ea, quam possideo, quae mihi vita charior est, quod posthac existam, et permanebo et eam Deitatem maiori perfectione, quam nunc, intuebor.
In it, moved by a heavenly appetite, I can spend much time; but I can also at the same time be affected with sadness when I see how much is lacking to my finite intellect: but I calm the sadness with the hope I possess, which is dearer to me than life, that I shall exist hereafter and remain, and shall behold that Deity with greater perfection than now.
Ubi brevem, praetervolantemque considero vitam, in qua singulis momentis mortem exspecto; si deberem credere me finem habiturum, sanctaque illa, et praestantissima cariturum contemplatione, certe omnium creaturarum, quibus cognitio finis sui deest, miserrimus essem. Quippe metus mortis ante obitum miserum me redderet, et post cum plane nihil, et per consequens miser essem, quia divina illa contemplatione privatus essem. Huc vero me tuae videntur ducere opiniones, quod ubi hic esse desino, etiam in aeternum desinam, cum econtra illud verbum, et Dei voluntas consolentur interno suo in anima mea testimonio, quod post hanc vitam in statu perfectiore me aliquando, contemplando summe perfectam Deitatem, oblectabo.
When I consider this short and fleeting life, in which I expect death at every moment, if I had to believe that I would have an end and be deprived of that holy and most excellent contemplation, I would certainly be the most miserable of all creatures, which lack knowledge of their end. For fear of death before my death would make me miserable, and after it I would be altogether nothing, and consequently miserable, because I would be deprived of that divine contemplation. And this is where your opinions seem to lead me: that when I cease to be here, I shall also cease for eternity;1 whereas, on the contrary, that word and the will of God console me with their inner testimony in my soul that after this life I shall one day delight, in a more perfect state, in contemplating the supremely perfect Deity.
  1. 1 Nadler quotes this passage as Blyenbergh’s perceptive recognition that Spinoza’s views make it hard to see how the soul is immortal (Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 252). Spinoza’s own doctrine is in Part V, Props. 21–23 and the scholium to Prop. 34.
Profecto licet spes ista aliquando falsa esse deprehenderetur, me tamen, dum spero, beatum reddit.
Certainly, though this hope should one day be found false, yet while I hope it makes me blessed.
Unicum hoc est, quod precibus, suspiriis, et seriis votis a Deo peto, et petam, (utinam ad id plus conferre liceret!) quamdiu spiritus hos reget artus, ut ipsi placeat, me sua bonitate tam beatum reddere, ut, quando hoc corpus dissolvitur, ens intellectuale ad perfectissimam illam Deitatem contemplandum maneam, et si hoc tantum nanciscor, mihi, quomodo hic credatur, quid invicem persuadeatur, an aliquid naturali intellectu fundetur, et percipi ab eo possit, nec ne, perinde est.
This is the one thing that I ask and shall ask of God with prayers, sighs and earnest vows (would that I could contribute more to it!), as long as breath shall rule these limbs:2 that it may please him, in his goodness, to make me so blessed that when this body is dissolved I may remain an intellectual being to contemplate that most perfect Deity; and if I obtain only this, it is all the same to me how it is believed here, what people persuade one another of, whether something is founded on the natural intellect and can be perceived by it or not.
  1. 2 Virgil, Aeneid IV.336: dum spiritus hos regit artus.
Hoc, hoc solummodo est votum meum, meum desiderium, continuaeque preces, ut Deus hanc certitudinem in anima mea confirmet, et, si eam possideo, (ah me miserrimum, si eo destituar!) prae desiderio anima mea exclamet, quemadmodum cervus anhelat aquarum rivos, ita te, o Deus vivens, anima mea desiderat: ah! quando veniet dies, quod tecum ero, et te adspiciam. Si hoc tantum obtineo, omne animae meae studium, et desiderium possideo.
This, this alone, is my vow, my desire and my continual prayer: that God may confirm this certainty in my soul, and, if I possess it (ah, most wretched me, if I should lack it!), that my soul may cry out in longing: As the hart pants after the brooks of water, so my soul longs for thee, O living God:3 ah! when will the day come that I shall be with thee and behold thee? If I obtain only this, I possess all that my soul strives for and desires.
  1. 3 Psalm 42:1–2 (Vulgate 41:2–3): Quemadmodum desiderat cervus ad fontes aquarum, ita desiderat anima mea ad te, Deus … quando veniam, et apparebo ante faciem Dei?
Quia vero nostrum opus Deo displicet,a spem istam, ex tua sententia, non video: Nec intelligo, Deum, (siquidem de eo humano more loqui liceat) si nullam ex opere nostro, et laudatione capit voluptatem, nos produxisse, et conservasse. Si vero a tua sententia aberro, tuam explicationem expeto.
But since our work displeases God, I do not see this hope on your opinion: nor do I understand that God (if we may speak of him in human fashion) produced and preserved us if he takes no pleasure in our work and praise. But if I stray from your opinion, I ask for your explanation.
  1. a The Dutch has ‘because our service is not pleasing to God’ (G IV 123–124).
Verum me, et forte te solito diutius moratus sum; quia vero mihi chartam deesse video, finiam. Horum solutionem videre aveo. Forte hic illic aliquam ex epistola tua eduxi conclusionem, quae fortassis tua non erit opinio; sed super ea re explicationem tuam audire cupio.
But I have detained myself, and perhaps you, longer than usual; and since I see that I am running out of paper, I shall end. I am eager to see the solution of these things. Perhaps here and there I have drawn from your letter some conclusion which will perhaps not be your opinion; but I wish to hear your explanation of it.
[G IV 124] Nuper occupatus fui in quibusdam Dei attributis perpendendis, in quo me tua non parum iuvit appendix. Mentem tuam solummodo fusius explicui, quae mihi non nisi demonstrationes exhibere videtur, et ideo demiror in praefatione affirmari, te illa non sentire; sed obligatum fuisse, ut ita tuum, uti promiseras, Cartesii philosophiam discipulum doceres; et te longe aliam, tam de Deo, quam de anima, speciatim de animae voluntate fovere opinionem: video quoque in illa praefatione dici, te haec cogitata metaphysica brevi prolixiora editurum, quae duo avide desidero: nam de iis aliquid singulare spero.
I have lately been occupied in weighing certain attributes of God, in which your appendix has helped me not a little. I have merely set out your view more fully, which seems to me to present nothing but demonstrations; and so I am surprised that it is affirmed in the preface1 that you do not hold these views, but were obliged to teach your pupil Descartes’s philosophy as you had promised, and that you hold a very different opinion both of God and of the soul, especially of the soul’s will: I see also that it is said in that preface that you will shortly publish these Metaphysical Thoughts at greater length, both of which I eagerly desire: for I hope for something singular from them.
  1. 1 Lodewijk Meyer’s preface to the Principles (G I 131–132): since Spinoza had promised to teach his pupil Descartes’s philosophy, he made it a matter of conscience not to depart from Descartes’s opinions by a hair’s breadth; many things in it he rejects as false, and holds an opinion far different from them.
Mea autem non fert consuetudo aliquem laudibus extollere. Scripsi haec sincero animo, et amicitia non fucata, prout in epistola tua petiisti, utque veritas detegeretur. Excusa nimiam, praeter intentionem, prolixitatem. Si ad has responderis, me tibi summopere devincies.
But it is not my custom to extol anyone with praises. I have written this with a sincere mind and unfeigned friendship, as you asked in your letter, and so that the truth may be uncovered. Excuse the excessive length, beyond my intention. If you answer this, you will oblige me greatly.
Non recuso, si ea lingua, in qua educatus sum, scribere animus est, vel alia, modo Latina, aut Gallica sit; hoc vero responsum, ut eadem exares lingua, peto, quia mentem tuam in ea bene, quod forte in lingua Latina non fieret, percipiebam.
I do not object if you have a mind to write in the language in which I was brought up,2 or in another, provided it is Latin or French; but this reply I ask you to compose in the same language, because I understood your mind well in it, which perhaps would not happen in Latin.
  1. 2 Dutch, in which Spinoza had written Letter 19 (see the note there).
Ita me tibi devincies, egoque ero, ac permanebo, Mi Domine, tibi addictissimus atque officiosissimus,
So you will oblige me, and I shall be and remain, Sir, your most devoted and most dutiful
G. de Blyenbergh.
W. van Blyenbergh.
Dordraci 16. Ianuarii 1665.
Dordrecht, 16 January 1665.
[G IV 125] In responsione tua prolixius,a quid per negationem in Deo intelligas, erudiri exopto.
In your reply I should like to be taught more fully what you understand by negation in God.1
  1. a Printed after the date, as a postscript (OP p. 497).
  1. 1 Spinoza explains it in Letter 21.

Epistola XXI

Letter 21

Spinoza to Willem van Blyenbergh

Viro Doctissimo, ac Ornatissimo,
To the most learned and most accomplished man
Guilielmo de Blyenbergh.
Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine, et amice,
Sir and friend,
[G IV 126] Quum primam tuam epistolam legebam,a existimabam nostras opiniones fere concordare; sed vero ex secunda, quae 21 huius mensis mihi tradita est, longe aliter se rem habere intelligo, videoque nos non tantum dissentire de iis, quae longe ex primis principiis sunt petenda; sed etiam de eisdem ipsis principiis: adeo ut vix credam fore, ut nos epistolis invicem erudire possimus.
When I read your first letter, I thought our opinions almost agreed; but from the second, which was delivered to me on the 21st of this month,1 I understand that the matter stands far otherwise, and I see that we disagree not only about the things that must be derived at long remove from first principles, but also about the principles themselves: so that I hardly believe that we can instruct each other by letters.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 497–505, Letter 34), headed Versio; the letter is undated, but Blyenbergh’s reply gives its date as 28 January 1665 (Letter 22). Gebhardt prints only this Latin (G IV 126–133), adding twice from the Dutch edition of 1677 the words over Deskartes Beginselen (‘on Descartes’s Principles’) after Appendice; they are left out here.
  1. 1 Letter 20, dated 16 January 1665. Nadler quotes this paragraph as showing how far apart the two men stood (Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., pp. 253–254).
Video namque nullam demonstrationem, licet pro demonstrationis legibus solidissimam, apud te valere, nisi conveniat cum ea explicatione, quam vel ipse, vel alii theologi, tibi haud ignoti, sacrae Scripturae tribuunt. Verum si deprehendas, Deum per sacram Scripturam clarius, et efficacius loqui, quam per lumen naturalis intellectus, quod nobis etiam concessit, ac assiduo sapientia sua divina firmiter, et incorrupte conservat, validas habes rationes, ut intellectum flectas ad opiniones, quas sacrae Scripturae tribuis, quin ipse haud aliter facere possem.
For I see that no demonstration, however solid according to the laws of demonstration, has weight with you unless it agrees with the explanation which either you yourself or other theologians known to you attribute to Holy Scripture. But if you find that God speaks more clearly and effectively through Holy Scripture than through the light of the natural intellect, which he has also granted us and constantly preserves, firm and uncorrupted, by his divine wisdom, you have strong reasons for bending your intellect to the opinions you attribute to Holy Scripture; indeed, I myself could not do otherwise.
Quod vero me spectat, quia plane, et sine ambagibus profiteor, me S. Scripturam non intelligere, licet aliquot annos in ea insumpserim, et quia me haud fugit, quando solidam nactus sum demonstrationem, me non posse in tales cogitationes incidere, ut unquam de ea dubitare queam, omnino in eo, quod mihi intellectus monstrat, acquiesco sine ulla suspicione, me ea in re deceptum esse, nec Sacram Scripturam, quamvis eam non investigem, ei contradicere posse; quia veritas veritati non repugnat, ut iam ante in appendice mea (caput indicare nequeo, nec enim liber hic ruri penes me est) clare indicavi; et si fructum, quem iam ex intellectu naturali cepi, vel semel falsum esse deprehenderem, me fortunatum redderet, quoniam fruor, et vitam non maerore et gemitu, sed tranquillitate, laetitia, et hilaritate transigere studeo et subinde gradum unum adscendo.
But as for me, since I profess plainly and without circumlocution that I do not understand Holy Scripture, though I have spent some years on it, and since it does not escape me that when I have obtained a solid demonstration I cannot fall into such thoughts that I could ever doubt it, I acquiesce entirely in what the intellect shows me, without any suspicion that I have been deceived in it, or that Holy Scripture, even though I do not search it, can contradict it; because truth does not conflict with truth, as I have already clearly indicated in my appendix2 (I cannot give the chapter, for the book is not with me here in the country3); and if I were even once to find that the fruit which I have already gathered from the natural intellect was false, it would still make me fortunate, since I enjoy it, and try to pass my life not in sorrow and sighing but in tranquillity, joy and cheerfulness, and from time to time I climb a step higher.
  1. 2 Metaphysical Thoughts II.8 (G I 265): ‘for truth does not conflict with truth, nor can Scripture teach the trifles commonly feigned’.
  2. 3 Spinoza was still at the farm near Schiedam (see the note to Letter 19).
Agnosco interim (id, quod summam mihi praebet satisfactionem, et mentis tranquillitatem) cuncta potentia entis summe perfecti, ac eius immutabili ita fieri decreto.
Meanwhile I recognize (which gives me the greatest satisfaction and tranquillity of mind) that all things happen thus by the power of a supremely perfect being and by his immutable decree.
[G IV 127] Ad tuam vero ut redeam epistolam, dico me tibi summas ex animo agere gratias, quod mihi tuam in tempore philosophandi rationem aperuisti; sed quod tu talia, qualia vis ex mea epistola educere, mihi affingas, pro eo nullas tibi gratis ago. Quam, quaeso, materiam mea praebuit epistola has mihi affingendi opiniones, homines nempe bestiis esse similes, homines bestiarum more mori, et interire, nostra Deo displicere opera, etc. (licet in postremo hoc summopere dissentiamus, quia aliter te non percipio, quam quod intelligis, Deum nostris operibus delectari, tanquam qui finem suum assecutus est, eo quod res ex voto successit).
But to return to your letter, I thank you most heartily for opening to me in time your manner of philosophizing; but for imputing to me such things as you wish to draw out of my letter, I give you no thanks. What material, I ask, did my letter offer for imputing to me these opinions: namely, that men are like beasts, that men die and perish in the manner of beasts, that our works displease God,1 etc. (though on this last we disagree greatly, since I do not understand you otherwise than as meaning that God delights in our works, as one who has attained his end because the thing has turned out as he wished).
  1. 1 See Letter 20.
Quantum ad me, ego profecto clare, dixi, probos Deum colere, et assidue colendo perfectiores evadere, Deum amare; hoccine est eos bestiis similes reddere; aut eos instar bestiarum perire, vel denique eorum opera Deo non placere?
As for me, I certainly said clearly that the good worship God, and by constantly worshipping become more perfect, and love God; is this to make them like beasts, or to have them perish like beasts, or finally to say that their works do not please God?
Si meas literas maiore cum attentione legisses, clare perspexisses, nostrum dissensum in hoc solo positum esse; scilicet an Deus, ut Deus, hoc est, absolute, nulla ei humana attributa adscribendo, perfectiones, quas probi accipiunt, eis communicet; (ut ego intelligo) anve ut iudex, quod ultimum tu statuis; et ea de causa defendis impios, quia iuxta Dei decretum, quicquid possunt, faciunt, Deo aeque, ac pios servire. Sed vero secundum mea dicta id nullatenus sequitur: quia Deum tanquam iudicem non introduco, et ideo ego opera ex operis qualitate, non vero ex potentia operatoris aestimo, et merces, quae opus consequitur, tam necessario id consequitur, quam ex natura trianguli sequitur, tres eius angulos duobus rectis aequales esse debere.
If you had read my letter with greater attention, you would have seen clearly that our disagreement lies in this alone: namely, whether God, as God, that is, absolutely, without ascribing any human attributes to him, communicates to the good the perfections which they receive (as I understand it), or as a judge, which is what you maintain; and for that reason you maintain that the impious, because according to God’s decree they do whatever they can, serve God as much as the pious. But according to what I said this by no means follows: because I do not introduce God as a judge, and therefore I value works by the quality of the work, not by the power of the worker; and the reward which follows the work follows it as necessarily as it follows from the nature of a triangle that its three angles must be equal to two right angles.
Et hoc unusquisque intelliget, qui tantum ad id, quod summa nostra beatitudo in amore erga Deum consistit, attendit, quodque ille amor necessario ex Dei cognitione, quae nobis tantopere commendatur, fluit. Hoc autem generaliter facillime demonstrari potest, si modo ad naturam Decreti Dei attendatur, quemadmodum in mea appendice explicui: Verum fateor, eos omnes, qui divinam naturam cum humana confundunt, valde ad id intelligendum ineptos esse.
And everyone will understand this who attends only to this, that our highest blessedness consists in love towards God,2 and that this love flows necessarily from the knowledge of God which is so much commended to us. This can be demonstrated very easily in general, if only one attends to the nature of God’s decree, as I have explained in my appendix: but I confess that all who confuse the divine nature with the human are very unfit to understand it.
  1. 2 Compare Part V, Prop. 36, Schol.: ‘our salvation, or blessedness, or freedom consists in a constant and eternal love of God’.
[G IV 128] Hisce literis finem hic imponere animus erat, ne tibi amplius essem molestus iis rebus, quae tantummodo (uti clarum est ex valde devoto additamento fini tuae epistolae affixo) pro ioco, et risu inserviunt, nulli autem usui sunt. Ne vero petitionem tuam omni modo reiiciam, ulterius ad explicationem vocum negationis, et privationis, et breviter ad id, quod necessarium est ad sensum praecedentis meae epistolae dilucidius enucleandum, progrediar.
I had intended to put an end to this letter here, so as not to trouble you further with things which serve only (as is clear from the very devout addition appended to the end of your letter1) for jest and laughter, and are of no use. But so as not to reject your request altogether, I shall go on further to the explanation of the words negation and privation, and briefly to what is necessary to unfold more clearly the sense of my preceding letter.
  1. 1 Blyenbergh’s prayer for the life to come (Letter 20).
Dico igitur primo privationem non esse privandi actum; sed tantum simplicem, et meram carentiam, quae in se nihil est: est quippe ens rationis tantum, vel modus cogitandi, quem formamus, quum res invicem comparamus. Dicimus, exempli gratia, caecum visu privatum esse, quia eum facile, ut videntem imaginamur, sive imaginatio haec hinc oriatur, quod cum aliis, qui vident, sive quod praesentem suum statum cum praeterito, quum videbat, comparamus; et cum hunc virum ea ratione consideramus, nempe comparando suam naturam cum natura aliorum, vel cum praeterita sua, tunc affirmamus, visum ad suam naturam pertinere, et propterea dicimus, eum eo privatum esse.
I say, then, first, that privation is not the act of depriving, but only a simple and mere lack, which in itself is nothing: it is indeed only a being of reason, or mode of thinking, which we form when we compare things with one another. We say, for example, that a blind man is deprived of sight, because we easily imagine him as seeing, whether this imagination arises from our comparing him with others who see, or his present state with his past one, when he saw; and when we consider this man in that way, namely by comparing his nature with the nature of others or with his own past nature, then we affirm that sight belongs to his nature, and therefore say that he is deprived of it.
Verum quum Dei decretum, eiusque natura consideratur, non magis de illo homine, quam de Lapide possumus affirmare, eum visu orbatum esse, quia illo tempore non magis sine contradictione illi homini visus, quam lapidi competit; quoniam ad hominem illum nil amplius pertinet, et suum est, quam id, quod divinus intellectus et voluntas ei tribuit. Et propterea Deus non magis est causa τοῦ illius non videre,a quam τοῦ non videre lapidis: id quod mera est negatio.
But when God’s decree and its nature are considered, we can no more affirm of that man than of a stone that he is bereft of sight, because at that time sight belongs to that man no more without contradiction than to the stone; since nothing more belongs to that man, and is his, than what the divine intellect and will attribute to him. And therefore God is no more the cause of that man’s not seeing than of the stone’s not seeing: which is mere negation.
  1. a τοῦ: the Greek article in the genitive, ‘of the not-seeing’, as printed in 1677 (OP p. 500); see the note to Letter 18.
Ita etiam, cum ad naturam hominis, qui appetitu libidinis ducitur, attendimus, praesentemque appetitum cum eo, qui in probis est, vel cum eo, quem ipse alias habuit, comparamus; affirmamus, eum hominem meliore appetitu privatum esse, quia tunc isti virtutis appetitum competere iudicamus; quod facere non possumus, si ad naturam Divini decreti, et intellectus attendimus: nam eo respectu melior ille appetitus non magis ad naturam istius hominis eo tempore, quam ad naturam Diaboli, vel lapidis pertinet, et idcirco eo respectu melior appetitus non est privatio, sed negatio. Adeo ut privatio nihil aliud sit, quam aliquid de re negare, quod iudicamus ad suam naturam pertinere, et negatio nil aliud, quam aliquid de re negare, quia ad suam naturam non pertinet.
So also, when we attend to the nature of a man who is led by the appetite of lust, and compare his present appetite with that which is in the good, or with the one he himself had at another time, we affirm that this man is deprived of a better appetite, because we then judge that the appetite of virtue belongs to him; which we cannot do if we attend to the nature of the divine decree and intellect: for in that respect that better appetite no more belongs to the nature of this man at that time than to the nature of the Devil or of a stone; and therefore in that respect the better appetite is not a privation but a negation. So that privation is nothing other than to deny of a thing something which we judge to belong to its nature, and negation nothing other than to deny of a thing something because it does not belong to its nature.
Et hinc liquet, quare Adami appetitus rerum terrenarum nostri, non vero intellectus Dei respectu tantum malus esset. Licet enim Deus praeteritum et praesentem Adami statum sciret, non idcirco Adamum praeterito statu privatum esse intelligebat, hoc est, praeteritum ad suam naturam pertinere: Nam tunc Deus aliquid contra suam voluntatem, hoc est, contra proprium suum intellectum intelligeret.
And from this it is clear why Adam’s appetite for earthly things was evil only in relation to our intellect, and not to God’s. For although God knew Adam’s past and present state, he did not on that account understand Adam to be deprived of his past state, that is, the past state to belong to his nature: for then God would understand something against his will, that is, against his own intellect.
Hoc si bene percepisses, simulque me illam libertatem, quam Cartesius menti adscribit, non concedere, quemadmodum L. M. in praefatione meo nomine testatus est, vel minimam in meis verbis non invenires contradictionem.
If you had perceived this well, and at the same time that I do not grant the freedom which Descartes ascribes to the mind, as L. M. testified in my name in the preface,1 you would not find the least contradiction in my words.
  1. 1 Lodewijk Meyer’s preface to the Principles (G I 132), which gives as an example of what Spinoza rejects the account of the will in the scholium to Part I, Prop. 15, of the Principles and in Metaphysical Thoughts II.12: ‘for he does not think it distinct from the intellect, much less endowed with such freedom’.
Sed video, me melius multo facturum fuisse, si in prima mea epistola Cartesii verbis respondissem, dicendo, nos non posse scire, quomodo nostra libertas, et quicquid ab ea dependet cum Dei providentia et libertate congruat, (sicuti in appendice diversis locis feci) adeo ut ex Dei creatione nullam in libertate nostra queamus invenire contradictionem, quia capere haud possumus, quomodo Deus res creavit, et (quod idem est) quomodo eas conservat. Verum putabam, te praefationem legisse, meque, si ex animi sententia non responderem, in amicitiae officium, quam animitus offerebam, peccaturum fuisse. Sed haec susque deque fero.
But I see that I should have done much better if in my first letter I had answered in Descartes’s words, saying that we cannot know how our freedom, and whatever depends on it, agrees with God’s providence and freedom (as I did in several places in the appendix), so that we can find no contradiction to our freedom in God’s creation, because we cannot grasp how God created things and (what is the same) how he preserves them. But I thought that you had read the preface, and that if I did not answer according to my real view I should sin against the duty of the friendship which I was heartily offering. But I care little for all this.
[G IV 129] Quia tamen video, te Cartesii mentem hactenus non bene percepisse, peto, ut haec duo attendas: Primo neque me, neque Cartesium unquam dixisse, quod ad nostram naturam pertinet, ut nostram voluntatem intra limites intellectus contineamus; sed tantum, quod Deus nobis intellectum determinatum, et voluntatem indeterminatam dedit, ita tamen, ut ignoremus, in quem finem nos creavit; porro, quod istiusmodi indeterminata, vel perfecta voluntas nos non solum perfectiores reddit; sed quoque, ut tibi in sequentibus dicam, quod ea nobis sit valde necessaria.
Since, however, I see that you have not so far perceived Descartes’s mind well, I ask you to attend to these two points. First, that neither I nor Descartes ever said that it belongs to our nature to contain our will within the limits of the intellect; but only that God has given us a determinate intellect and an indeterminate will, in such a way, however, that we do not know to what end he created us; further, that such an indeterminate or perfect will not only makes us more perfect, but also, as I shall tell you below, is very necessary to us.
Secundo quod nostra libertas nec in quadam contingentia, nec in quadam indifferentia sita est; sed in modo affirmandi, et negandi; adeo ut, quo rem aliquam minus indifferenter affirmamus, aut negamus, eo liberiores simus: Verbi causa, si Dei natura nobis est cognita, tam necessario ex natura nostra τὸ affirmare sequitur, Deum existere, quam ex natura trianguli eius tres angulos duobus rectis aequari fluit; et tamen nunquam magis liberi sumus, quam quum rem tali modo affirmamus.
Second, that our freedom lies neither in a certain contingency nor in a certain indifference, but in the manner of affirming and denying; so that the less indifferently we affirm or deny something, the freer we are:1 for example, if the nature of God is known to us, the affirming that God exists follows from our nature as necessarily as it flows from the nature of a triangle that its three angles are equal to two right angles; and yet we are never more free than when we affirm a thing in this way.
  1. 1 So the Principles, Part I, Prop. 15, Schol. (G I 175), where indifference is ‘the lowest degree of freedom’.
Quia vero haec necessitas nihil aliud est, quam Dei decretum, veluti in mea appendice clare ostendi; hinc quodammodo intelligere licet, quo pacto rem libere agamus, eiusque causa simus, non obstante, quod eam necessario, et ex Dei decreto agamus.
But because this necessity is nothing other than God’s decree, as I have shown clearly in my appendix, it can in some way be understood from this how we act freely and are the cause of the action, notwithstanding that we do it necessarily and from God’s decree.
Hoc, inquam, aliquo modo possumus intelligere, cum quid affirmamus, quod clare, et distincte percipimus, ubi vero quicquam, quod clare ac distincte non capimus, asserimus, hoc est, cum patimur, ut voluntas extra nostri intellectus limites exspatietur, tum illam necessitatem, Deique decreta non ita percipere possumus; sed nostram quidem libertatem, quam nostra voluntas semper includit, (quo respectu opera nostra solummodo bona, vel mala appellantur) et si tunc conamur libertatem nostram cum Dei decreto, et continua creatione conciliare, confundimus id, quod clare, et distincte intelligimus, cum eo, quod non percipimus, et propterea frustra id conamur.
This, I say, we can understand in some way when we affirm something which we perceive clearly and distinctly; but when we assert anything which we do not grasp clearly and distinctly, that is, when we allow the will to stray beyond the limits of our intellect, then we cannot perceive that necessity and God’s decrees in the same way, but we do indeed perceive our freedom, which our will always includes (in which respect alone our works are called good or evil); and if we then try to reconcile our freedom with God’s decree and continual creation, we confuse what we understand clearly and distinctly with what we do not perceive, and therefore we try in vain.
Sufficit ergo nobis, quod scimus nos liberos esse, et nos tales esse posse non obstante Dei decreto, nosque mali causam esse, eo quod nullus actus, nisi respectu nostrae libertatis, tantum malus nominari potest. Haec sunt, quae Cartesium spectant, ut demonstrarem eius dicta ab ea parte nullam pati contradictionem.
It is enough for us, then, that we know that we are free, and that we can be so notwithstanding God’s decree, and that we are the cause of evil, because no act can be called evil except in relation to our freedom alone. These are the things that concern Descartes, to show that his words suffer no contradiction on that side.
[G IV 130] Iam ad ea, quae me attinent, me convertam, ac primo breviter referam utilitatem, quae ex mea opinione nascitur, quae praecipue in eo est sita, quod scilicet noster intellectus Deo mentem, et corpus extra omnem superstitionem offert, nec preces nobis valde utiles esse nego: nam meus intellectus est nimis parvus ad omnia media determinandum, quae Deus habet, quibus homines ad sui amorem, hoc est, ad salutem perducat; adeo ut tantum absit hanc opinionem noxiam futuram esse, ut econtra iis, qui nullis praeiudiciis nec puerili superstitione praeoccupati sunt, unicum sit medium ad summum beatitudinis gradum perveniendi.
Now I shall turn to what concerns me, and first I shall briefly report the usefulness which arises from my opinion,1 which lies chiefly in this, that our intellect offers mind and body to God free of all superstition; nor do I deny that prayers are very useful to us: for my intellect is too small to determine all the means God has by which to bring men to love of him, that is, to salvation; so that this opinion is so far from being harmful that, on the contrary, for those who are not taken up with prejudices and childish superstition, it is the only means of reaching the highest degree of blessedness.
  1. 1 Compare the uses of the doctrine set out at the end of Part II, Prop. 49, Schol..
[G IV 131] Quod vero ais, me homines, eos a Deo tam dependentes faciendo, ideo elementis, herbis, et lapidibus similes reddere, id sufficienter ostendit te meam opinionem perversissime intelligere, et res, quae intellectum spectant, cum imaginatione confundere.
But when you say that by making men so dependent on God I make them like the elements, plants and stones, this shows sufficiently that you understand my opinion most perversely, and confuse the things that concern the intellect with the imagination.
Si enim puro intellectu percepisses, quid sit a Deo dependere, certe non cogitares, res, quatenus a Deo dependent, mortuas, corporeas, et imperfectas esse, (quis unquam de ente summe perfecto tam viliter ausus fuit loqui) econtra caperes, ea de causa, et quatenus a Deo dependent, perfectas esse. Adeo ut hanc dependentiam, atque necessariam operationem quam optime per Dei decretum intelligamus, quando non ad truncos, et herbas; sed ad maxime intelligibiles, et res creatas perfectissimas attendimus, ut ex illo, quod secundo de Cartesii mente iam ante memoravimus, clare apparet, quod attendere debuisses.
For if you had perceived with pure intellect what it is to depend on God, you would certainly not think that things, insofar as they depend on God, are dead, corporeal and imperfect (who ever dared to speak so meanly of the supremely perfect being?); on the contrary, you would grasp that they are perfect for that reason, and insofar as they depend on God. So that we understand this dependence and necessary operation best through God’s decree when we attend not to tree-trunks and plants but to the most intelligible and most perfect created things, as is clear from what we mentioned second above concerning Descartes’s mind, to which you should have attended.
Nec reticere possum, me id summopere mirari, quod dicis: si Deus delictum non puniret, (hoc est, tanquam iudex tali poena, quam ipsum delictum non inferret: Id enim solum nostra est quaestio.) quaenam ratio impedit, quo minus quaevis scelera avide perpetrem?
Nor can I keep silent that I am greatly surprised at what you say: if God did not punish wrongdoing (that is, as a judge, with such a punishment as the wrongdoing itself does not bring with it: for that alone is our question), what reason prevents me from eagerly committing any crimes?1
  1. 1 Letter 20. Compare Part V, Prop. 41, Schol., on those who would give way to their lusts if they did not believe that the mind outlives the body.
Certe qui illud tantum (quod de te non spero) formidine poenae omittit, is nulla ratione ex amore operatur, et quam minime virtutem amplectitur. Quantum ad me, ea omitto, vel omittere studeo, quia expresse cum mea singulari natura pugnant, meque a Dei amore, et cognitione aberrare facerent.
Certainly he who refrains from them only for fear of punishment (which I do not hope of you) in no way acts from love, and embraces virtue as little as possible. As for me, I refrain from them, or try to refrain, because they are expressly contrary to my singular nature, and would make me stray from the love and knowledge of God.
Porro si ad naturam humanam paulum attendisses, et naturam Dei decreti, prout in appendice explicui, percepisses, tandemque scivisses, quomodo res esset deducenda, antequam ad conclusionem perveniatur, non adeo temere dixisses, hanc opinionem nos truncis, etc. similes reddere; nec tam multas absurditates, quam imaginaris, mihi affinxisses.
Further, if you had attended a little to human nature, and had perceived the nature of God’s decree as I explained it in the appendix, and had known, finally, how a thing must be deduced before one comes to the conclusion, you would not have said so rashly that this opinion makes us like tree-trunks, etc.; nor would you have imputed to me so many absurdities as you imagine.
De duabus illis rebus, quas ais, priusquam ad secundam tuam regulam pergis, te percipere haud valere, respondeo primo Cartesium sufficere ad conclusionem tuam faciendam, quod nempe, si modo attendis ad naturam tuam, experiris, te posse iudicium tuum suspendere; si vero dicas, te in te ipso non experiri, nos tantum virium hodie in rationem obtinere, ut hoc semper continuare possemus, id idem Cartesio esset, ac nos hodie non posse videre, nos, quamdiu existimus, futuros semper res cogitantes, vel naturam rei cogitantis retenturos, quod profecto contradictionem involvit.
Of those two things which you say you cannot perceive1 before you go on to your second rule, I answer, first, that Descartes suffices for drawing your conclusion: namely, that if only you attend to your nature, you find by experience that you can suspend your judgment; but if you say that you do not find in yourself that we today have so much power over reason that we could always continue this, that would be the same for Descartes as that we cannot see today that we shall always, as long as we exist, be thinking things, or retain the nature of a thinking thing; which certainly involves a contradiction.
  1. 1 Letter 20.
Ad secundum cum Cartesio dico, si voluntatem nostram extra limites intellectus nostri valde limitati extendere non possemus, nos miserrimos futuros, neque in nostra potestate fore vel frustum panis comedere, vel passum progredi, vel subsistere: omnia enim incerta, atque periculis sunt plena.
To the second I say with Descartes that if we could not extend our will beyond the limits of our very limited intellect, we should be most miserable, and it would not be in our power either to eat a piece of bread, or to take a step forward, or to stand still: for all things are uncertain and full of dangers.
[G IV 132] Transeo nunc ad secundam tuam regulam, asseroque, me quidem credere, quod ego eam veritatem, quam tu in scriptura esse credis, illi non tribuam, et tamen credo, quod ego illi tantum, si non plus adscribam authoritatis; quodque longe cautius, quam alii, caveam, ne illi pueriles quasdam, et absurdas sententias affingam, quod nemo praestare potest, nisi is, qui philosophiam bene intelligit, vel Divinas habet revelationes; adeo ut me valde parum moveant explicationes, quas vulgares theologi de scriptura adferunt, praesertim si istius sint farinae, ut Scripturam semper iuxta litteram, sensumque externum sumant; et nunquam, praeter Socinianos, crassum adeo vidi theologum, qui non percipit Sacram Scripturam creberrime more humano de Deo loqui, ac suum sensum Parabolis exprimere; et quod ad contradictionem, quam frustra (mea quidem opinione) conaris ostendere, credo te per Parabolam omnino aliud quid, quam vulgo fit, intelligere: nam quis unquam audivit, eum, qui suos conceptus parabolis exprimit, a suo sensu aberrare.
I pass now to your second rule, and assert that I do indeed believe that I do not attribute to Scripture the truth which you believe is in it, and yet I believe that I ascribe to it as much authority, if not more; and that I take far more care than others not to impute to it certain childish and absurd opinions, which no one can achieve unless he understands philosophy well or has divine revelations; so that I am very little moved by the explanations which the common theologians bring to Scripture, especially if they are of the sort that always take Scripture according to the letter and the outward sense; and, apart from the Socinians, I have never seen a theologian so dull as not to perceive that Holy Scripture very often speaks of God in human fashion and expresses its sense in parables; and as for the contradiction which you try in vain (in my opinion at least) to show, I believe that by a parable you understand something altogether different from what is commonly understood: for who ever heard that he who expresses his concepts in parables strays from his own sense.
Quum Micha Regi Aghabo diceret, se Deum in solio suo sedentem vidisse, ac exercitus caelestes a dextra, et sinistra stantes, Deumque ex illis quaerere, quisnam Aghabum deciperet, illud ipsum certe erat Parabola, qua propheta praecipuum, quod ea occasione (quae non erat, ut sublimia theologiae dogmata doceret) Dei nomine debebat manifestare, sufficienter exprimebat; adeo ut nullo modo a sensu suo aberraret.
When Micaiah told King Ahab that he had seen God sitting on his throne, and the heavenly hosts standing on the right and on the left, and that God asked them which of them would deceive Ahab,1 that was certainly a parable, by which the prophet sufficiently expressed the chief thing which he had to make known in God’s name on that occasion (which was not one for teaching the sublime dogmas of theology); so that he in no way strayed from his own sense.
  1. 1 I Kings 22:19–22.
Sic etiam caeteri prophetae Dei verbum iussu Dei ea ratione populo manifestarunt, tanquam optimo medio, non vero tanquam illo, quod Deus petebat, populum ad scopum Scripturae primarium ducendi, qui iuxta ipsius Christi dictum in hoc consistit, nimirum in amore Dei supra omnia, et proximi tanquam sui ipsius. Sublimes speculationes, credo, Scripturam minime tangunt. Me quod spectat, nulla Dei aeterna attributa ex sacra scriptura didici, nec discere potui.
So too the other prophets made God’s word known to the people at God’s command in that way, as the best means, but not as the means which God required, of leading the people to the primary aim of Scripture, which according to the saying of Christ himself consists in this: namely, in the love of God above all things, and of one’s neighbour as oneself.2 Sublime speculations, I believe, do not touch Scripture at all. As for me, I have not learned, nor could I learn, any eternal attributes of God from Holy Scripture.
  1. 2 Matthew 22:37–39.
[G IV 133] Quantum vero quintum attinet argumentum (scilicet prophetas Dei verbum tali ratione manifestum fecisse, quoniam veritas veritati non est contraria) nihil reliqui est, quam ut (quemadmodum quivis, qui demonstrandi methodum percipit, iudicabit) demonstrem, Scripturam, prout est, esse Dei verum verbum revelatum. Cuius mathematicam demonstrationem, nisi divina Revelatione, habere nequeo.
As for the fifth argument (namely, that the prophets made God’s word known in such a way, since truth is not contrary to truth), nothing remains for me (as anyone who perceives the method of demonstrating will judge) but to demonstrate that Scripture, as it is, is the true revealed word of God. Of this I can have no mathematical demonstration except by divine revelation.
Et ideo dixi, credo, sed non more mathematico scio, omnia, quae Deus prophetis etc. quia firmiter credo, non vero mathematice scio, prophetas Dei intimos Consiliarios, et Legatos fuisse fidos; adeo ut in iis, quae ego affirmavi, nulla omnino sit contradictio; cum e contrario ab altera parte haud paucae inveniantur.
And therefore I said, ‘I believe’, but not ‘I know in the mathematical manner’, ‘that all the things which God [revealed] to the prophets’,1 etc.; because I firmly believe, but do not know mathematically, that the prophets were God’s intimate counsellors and faithful ambassadors; so that in what I have affirmed there is no contradiction at all, whereas on the other side not a few are found.
  1. 1 Letter 19.
Reliquum tuae epistolae, nempe ubi dicis: Denique ens summe perfectum noverat, etc. et deinde quod contra exemplum de veneno adfers, ac ultimo, quod appendicem, et id, quod sequitur, spectat, dico hanc praesentem quaestionem non respicere.
The rest of your letter, namely where you say, ‘Finally, the supremely perfect Being knew’,1 etc., and then what you bring against the example of poison, and lastly what concerns the appendix and what follows, does not, I say, bear on the present question.
  1. 1 Letter 20.
Praefationem L. M. quod attinet, in ipsa certe simul ostenditur, quid Cartesio adhuc probandum esset, ut solidam de libero arbitrio demonstrationem formaret, additurque, me contrariam fovere sententiam, et quomodo eam foveam: quam forte suo tempore indicabo, nunc vero animus non est.
As for L. M.’s preface, it certainly shows at the same time what Descartes still had to prove in order to form a solid demonstration of free will, and adds that I hold the contrary opinion, and how I hold it: which I shall perhaps make known in due time, but now I have no mind to.
De opere vero super Cartesium nec cogitavi, nec ulteriorem eius gessi curam, postquam sermone Belgico prodiit: et quidem non sine ratione, quam hic recensere longum foret. Adeo ut nihil dicendum restet, quam me etc.
As for the work on Descartes, I have neither given it a thought nor taken any further care of it since it came out in Dutch:1 and indeed not without reason, which it would take long to recount here. So that nothing remains to be said but that I am, etc.
  1. 1 The translation of the Principles by Pieter Balling (Nadler p. 250).

Epistola XXII

Letter 22

Willem van Blyenbergh to Spinoza

Clarissimo viro
To the most distinguished man
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Guilielmus de Blyenbergh.
from Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine, et chare amice,
Sir, and dear friend,
[G IV 134] Epistolam tuam, 28 Ianuarii scriptam,a suo tempore accepi; aliae occupationes praeter studia impediverunt, quo minus ad tuam citius responderim: et quoniam hic illic acribus tua referta est reprehensionibus, quid de ea iudicarem, vix sciebam: nam in prima, 5 Ianuarii exarata, tam liberaliter amicitiam tuam ex animo mihi obtuleras, hac addita obtestatione, meam non solum illo tempore scriptam; sed et subsequentes valde gratas fore; quin amice flagitabas, ut libere, si quas insuper movere possem difficultates, obiicerem, uti in mea, 16 Ianuarii data, prolixius paulo praestiti.
Your letter written on 28 January1 I received in due time; occupations other than my studies prevented me from answering it sooner; and since it is filled here and there with sharp reproofs, I hardly knew what to think of it: for in the first, written on 5 January,2 you had so generously offered me your friendship from the heart, adding the assurance that not only my letter written at that time but also later ones would be very welcome; indeed you asked me in friendly fashion to put forward freely any further difficulties I could raise, as I did at somewhat greater length in mine of 16 January.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 505–510, Letter 35), headed Versio; Gebhardt prints the Dutch first (G IV 134–144). The 1677 edition prints in italics the words that Blyenbergh takes from Spinoza’s letters; quotation marks mark them here.
  1. 1 Letter 21.
  2. 2 Letter 19.
Ad eam amicam, et erudientem, iuxta postulatum, et promissum tuum, exspectabam responsionem; sed econtra talem, quae nimiam non redolet amicitiam, accepi; nempe quod nullae demonstrationes, licet validissimae apud me valent, quod mentem Cartesii non percipio, quod res spirituales nimium cum terrenis confundo, etc. ita ut invicem epistolis diutius erudire non possemus.
To that I expected a friendly and instructive reply, in accordance with your request and promise; but instead I received one which does not smell of much friendship:3 namely, that no demonstrations, however strong, have weight with me, that I do not perceive Descartes’s mind, that I confuse spiritual things too much with earthly ones, etc., so that we could no longer instruct each other by letters.
  1. 3 Nadler quotes this passage on Blyenbergh’s being taken aback by Spinoza’s tone (Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 254).
Ad quae amice respondeo, me firmiter credere, te supra dicta melius me intelligere, et assuetum magis esse res corporeas a spiritualibus discernere: in metaphysica enim, quam ego inchoo, summum adscendisti gradum, et ideo, ut erudirer, favorem tuum captabam; nunquam vero existimabam, me obiectionibus, libere factis, offensionis causam praebiturum.
To which I reply in friendly fashion that I firmly believe that you understand the things mentioned above better than I, and are more accustomed to distinguish corporeal things from spiritual: for in metaphysics, which I am only beginning, you have climbed to the highest degree, and that is why I sought your favour, to be instructed; but I never thought that by objections freely made I would give cause for offence.
Summas tibi ex animo ago gratias, quod tantum laborem in duabus epistolis exarandis, praecipue in secunda, suscepisti; ex posteriore, quam ex priore, tuam mentem clarius percepi, et nihilominus ei assensum praebere nequeo, nisi difficultates, quas adhuc in ea invenio, tollantur: nec hoc offensionis causam dare potest. Nam illud vitium in intellectu nostro arguit, si veritati sine fundamento necessario assentimur: Licet tui veri essent conceptus, iis assensum praebere non licet, quamdiu in me quaedam obscuritatis, vel dubitationis rationes supersunt, quamvis dubitationes non ex re proposita, sed ex mei intellectus imperfectione orirentur.
I thank you most heartily for undertaking so much labour in writing two letters, especially the second; from the later I perceived your mind more clearly than from the earlier, and nevertheless I cannot give it my assent unless the difficulties which I still find in it are removed: nor can this give cause for offence. For it argues a fault in our intellect if we assent to the truth without a necessary foundation: even if your concepts were true, I may not give them assent so long as some reasons for obscurity or doubt remain in me, even if the doubts arose not from the matter proposed but from the imperfection of my intellect.
Et quia hoc abunde tibi notum est, aegre ferre non debes, si rursus quasdam formem obiectiones: cogor ita facere, quamdiu rem clare percipere nequeo; nam hoc in alium non fit finem, quam ad veritatem inveniendam; non vero ad tuam mentem praeter intentionem tuam detorquendam; et propterea ad haec pauca amicam peto responsionem.
And since this is abundantly known to you, you ought not to take it ill if I again form some objections: I am compelled to do so as long as I cannot perceive the matter clearly; for this is done to no other end than to find the truth, and not to twist your mind against your intention; and therefore I ask a friendly reply to these few points.
Dicis, nihil ad essentiam rei amplius pertinere, quam id, quod divina voluntas, potentiaque ei concedit, et revera tribuit; et cum ad naturam hominis, qui appetitu libidinis ducitur, attendimus, praesentemque appetitum cum eo, qui in probis est, vel cum eo, quem ipse alias habuit, comparamus, affirmamus eum hominem meliore appetitu privatum esse, quia tunc isti virtutis appetitum competere iudicamus; quod facere non possumus, si ad naturam divini decreti et intellectus attendimus: nam eo respectu melior ille appetitus non magis ad naturam istius hominis eo tempore, quam ad naturam Diaboli, aut lapidis pertinet, etc. Licet enim Deus praeteritum, et praesentem Adami statum sciret, non idcirco Adamum praeterito statu privatum esse intelligebat, hoc est, praeteritum ad suam praesentem naturam pertinere, etc.
You say that nothing more belongs to the essence of a thing than what the divine will and power grants and really attributes to it; and ‘when we attend to the nature of a man who is led by the appetite of lust,4 and compare his present appetite with that which is in the good, or with the one he himself had at another time, we affirm that this man is deprived of a better appetite, because we then judge that the appetite of virtue belongs to him; which we cannot do if we attend to the nature of the divine decree and intellect: for in that respect that better appetite no more belongs to the nature of this man at that time than to the nature of the Devil or of a stone’, etc. ‘For although God knew Adam’s past and present state, he did not on that account understand Adam to be deprived of his past state, that is, the past state to belong to his present nature’, etc.
  1. 4 Letter 21, where the last words read ‘to his nature’.
Ex quibus verbis clare sequi videtur, iuxta tuam sententiam, nihil aliud ad essentiam pertinere, quam quod eo momento, quo percipitur, res habet, hoc est, si me voluptatis desiderium tenet, illud desiderium ad meam illo tempore pertinet essentiam, et si me non tenet, illud non-desiderium ad meam eo, quo non desidero, tempore essentiam pertinet, unde infallibiliter consequitur, me tum respectu Dei aeque perfectionem in meis operibus includere, (solummodo gradu differentibus) quando voluptatum cupiditate, quam quando ea non teneor; quando omnis generis scelera perpetro, quam quando virtutem, et iustitiam exerceo.
From these words it seems clearly to follow, according to your opinion, that nothing else belongs to the essence than what the thing has at the moment at which it is perceived; that is, if the longing for pleasure holds me, that longing belongs to my essence at that time, and if it does not hold me, that non-longing belongs to my essence at the time when I do not long for it; from which it follows infallibly that, in relation to God, I include perfection in my works equally (differing only in degree) when I am held by the desire for pleasures as when I am not; when I commit crimes of every kind as when I practise virtue and justice.
Ad meam enim essentiam eo tempore tantum duntaxat, quantum operor, pertinet: nec enim plus, nec minus, secundum tuam positionem, operari possum, quam quantum revera accepi perfectionis, quia voluptatum, scelerumque cupiditas ad meam eo tempore, quo facio, pertinet essentiam, et eo tempore illam, non vero maiorem, a divina potentia accipio: Exigit ergo divina potentia eiusmodi duntaxat opera. Et ita ex tua positione clare videtur sequi, Deum scelera una, eademque ratione velle, qua ea vult, quae tu nomine virtutis insignis.
For only so much belongs to my essence at that time as I do: for according to your position I can do neither more nor less than the perfection I have really received, since the desire for pleasures and crimes belongs to my essence at the time when I act, and at that time I receive that essence, and not a greater, from the divine power: the divine power, then, requires only works of that kind. And so from your position it seems clearly to follow that God wills crimes in one and the same way as he wills the things which you mark with the name of virtue.
Ponamus iam, quod Deus, ut Deus, non vero ut iudex, piis, et impiis talem, et tantum essentiae largitur, quantum vult, ut illi efficiant; quaenam rationes dantur, ut unius opus non eodem modo, quam alterius velit? Nam quoniam unicuique ad opus suum qualitatem concedit, sequitur utique, quod, quibus minus largitus est, eadem ratione tantum ab ipsis petit, quantum ab illis, quibus plus dedit; et per consequens Deus sui ipsius respectu maiorem, minoremve nostrorum operum perfectionem, voluptatum et virtutum cupiditatem eodem modo exigit.
Let us now suppose that God, as God and not as judge, bestows on the pious and the impious such and so much essence as he wills them to bring about; what reasons are there why he should not will the work of the one in the same way as that of the other? For since he grants each one the quality for his work, it surely follows that from those to whom he has given less he asks, in the same proportion, as much as from those to whom he has given more; and consequently God, in relation to himself, requires the greater or lesser perfection of our works, the desire for pleasures and for virtues, in the same way.
Adeo ut, qui scelera patrat, necessario eadem patrare debeat, quia nihil aliud ad suam eo tempore essentiam spectat; uti is, qui virtutem exercet, ideo virtutem exercet, quia Dei potentia voluit, ut ad suam eo tempore essentiam id pertineret: Rursum mihi videtur Deum aeque, et eodem modo scelera, quam virtutem velle, quatenus autem utraque vult, tam huius, quam illius est causa, ipsique eatenus grata esse debent. Quod de Deo concipere durum est.
So that he who commits crimes must necessarily commit them, because nothing else belongs to his essence at that time; just as he who practises virtue practises virtue because God’s power willed that it should belong to his essence at that time: again it seems to me that God wills crimes equally and in the same way as virtue, and insofar as he wills both, he is the cause of the one as much as of the other, and to that extent they must both be pleasing to him. Which is hard to conceive of God.
Dicis, ut video, probos Deum colere; verum ex tuis scriptis nihil aliud percipio, quam quod Deo servire tantum sit talia efficere opera, quae Deus, ut operaremur, voluit; idem adscribis impiis, et libidinosis; quaenam ergo est differentia, respectu Dei, inter proborum, et improborum cultum?
You say, as I see, that the good worship God; but from your writings I perceive nothing else than that to serve God is only to bring about such works as God willed us to bring about; you ascribe the same to the impious and lustful; what difference is there, then, in relation to God, between the worship of the good and of the wicked?
Ais etiam, probos Deo servire, et serviendo continuo perfectiores evadere; sed non percipio, quid per τὸ perfectiores evadere intelligas, nec quid significet τὸ continuo perfectiores evadere. Nam et impii, et pii suam essentiam, et conservationem, aut continuam creationem a Deo, ut Deo, non vero, ut iudice, nanciscuntur, voluntatemque eius iuxta Dei decretum utrique eodem modo exequuntur. Quaenam ergo differentia, respectu Dei, inter utrosque potest esse?
You also say that the good serve God and by serving become continually more perfect;5 but I do not perceive what you understand by ‘becoming more perfect’, nor what ‘becoming continually more perfect’ means. For both the impious and the pious obtain their essence, and their preservation or continual creation, from God as God, not as judge, and both carry out his will in the same way according to God’s decree. What difference, then, can there be between the two in relation to God?
  1. 5 Letter 19.
Nam τὸ perfectiores continuo evadere non ex opere; sed ex Dei voluntate fluit; adeo ut, si impii per opera sua imperfectiores fiunt, illud non fluat ex operibus; sed ex sola Dei voluntate; et utrique voluntatem Dei tantum exequuntur: nequit ergo in his duobus, ratione Dei, dari discrimen. Quaenam sunt ergo rationes, ut hi per opus suum continuo perfectiores, illi vero deteriores evadant?
For becoming continually more perfect flows not from the work but from God’s will; so that if the impious become more imperfect through their works, this flows not from the works but from God’s will alone; and both only carry out God’s will: there can be no distinction between these two, then, in respect of God. What, then, are the reasons why these become continually more perfect through their work, and those worse?
Sed vero discrimen operis horum supra alterorum opus in hoc ponere videris, quod hoc opus plus perfectionis, quam illud includit. In his tuum, vel meum certo confido latere errorem: nam nullam in tuis scriptis reperire licet regulam, secundum quam res magis, minusve perfecta dicitur, quam quando plus, minusve habet essentiae.
But you seem to place the distinction between the work of the one and the work of the others in this, that this work includes more perfection than that. In this, I am confident, your error or mine certainly lies: for in your writings no rule can be found according to which a thing is called more or less perfect, except when it has more or less essence.
Si iam haec sit perfectionis regula, sunt igitur scelera, Dei respectu, ei aeque, ac opera proborum grata: nam Deus ea, ut Deus, hoc est, sui respectu, eodem modo vult, quia utraque ex Dei fluunt decreto. Si haec sit sola perfectionis regula, errores non nisi improprie tales dici possunt; sed revera nulli errores, nulla dantur scelera: et quicquid est, illam tantummodo, et talem, quam Deus dedit, essentiam complectitur, quae semper, utcunque ea se habeat, perfectionem involvit.
If this, then, is the rule of perfection, crimes are as pleasing to God, in relation to him, as the works of the good: for God, as God, that is, in relation to himself, wills them in the same way, because both flow from God’s decree. If this is the only rule of perfection, errors can be called errors only improperly; but in truth there are no errors, there are no crimes: and whatever is includes only that essence, and such as God gave it, which always, however it stands, involves perfection.
Me hoc clare non posse percipere, fateor, et tu quaerenti, an occidere Deo aeque placeat, ac eleemosynas largiri? an furtum committere, Dei respectu, aeque bonum sit, ac iustum esse? condona. Si neges, quaenam sunt rationes? si affirmes, quae apud me existunt rationes, quibus moverer, ut hoc opus, quod tu virtutem appellas, magis, quam aliud operarer? quaenam lex magis hoc, quam illud, prohibet?
I confess that I cannot perceive this clearly; and you must pardon me for asking whether killing is as pleasing to God as giving alms?6 Whether to commit theft is, in relation to God, as good as to be just? If you deny it, what are the reasons? If you affirm it, what reasons do I have by which I should be moved to do this work which you call virtue rather than another? What law forbids this rather than that?
  1. 6 Spinoza answers these questions in Letter 23.
Si ipsam nomines virtutis legem, fatendum mihi certe est, me nullam apud te reperire, iuxta quam virtus moderanda, et ex qua cognoscenda sit. Quicquid enim est, a Dei voluntate inseparabiliter dependet, et per consequens et hoc, et illud ex aequo virtuosum est. Et, quid tibi virtus, vel lex virtutis sit, non capio; ideo etiam, quod asseris, nos ex amore virtutis debere operari, non intelligo.
If you name the law of virtue itself, I must certainly confess that I find none in you according to which virtue is to be regulated and from which it is to be known. For whatever is depends inseparably on God’s will, and consequently this and that are equally virtuous. And I do not grasp what virtue, or the law of virtue, is for you; and so I also do not understand what you assert, that we ought to act from love of virtue.
Dicis quidem, te scelera, et vitia omittere, quia ea cum singulari tua natura pugnant, et te ab divina cognitione, et amore seducunt; verum nullam horum in omnibus tuis scriptis invenio vel regulam, vel probationem. Quin excusa me, si contrarium ex illis sequi dicam.
You do say that you refrain from crimes and vices7 because they conflict with your singular nature and lead you away from the divine knowledge and love; but in all your writings I find neither rule nor proof of these things. Indeed, forgive me if I say that the contrary follows from them.
  1. 7 Letter 21.
Omittis ea, quae vitia appello, quia cum singulari tua natura pugnant, non autem quia vitia complectuntur; omittis ea, quemadmodum cibus, a quo natura nostra abhorret, relinquitur. Certe qui mala omittet, eo quod sua natura ab eis abhorret, parum de virtute gloriari poterit.
You refrain from what I call vices because they conflict with your singular nature, not because they contain vices; you refrain from them as one leaves alone food which our nature abhors. Certainly he who refrains from evils because his nature abhors them can boast little of virtue.
[G IV 141] Hic iam rursum quaestio moveri potest, an, si daretur animus, cum cuius singulari natura non pugnaret; sed conveniret voluptates, sceleraque patrare, an, inquam, ratio virtutis sit, quae ipsum ad virtutem praestandam, malumque omittendum moveret? Sed qui fieri potest, ut quis cupiditatem voluptatis amitteret, quum eius cupiditas eo tempore de sua sit essentia, ipsamque iam modo acceperit, neque dimittere possit?
Here again the question can be raised whether, if there were a mind with whose singular nature it did not conflict, but agreed, to pursue pleasures and commit crimes, whether, I say, there is a reason of virtue which would move it to practise virtue1 and refrain from evil? But how can it happen that someone should lose the desire for pleasure, when that desire belongs at that time to his essence, and he has only just received it, and cannot let it go?
  1. 1 Answered in Letter 23.
[G IV 142] Hanc etiam consequentiam in tuis scriptis non video, quod nimirum eae actiones, quas scelerum nomine insignio, te a Dei cognitione, et eius amore seducerent. Nam voluntatem Dei tantum exequutus es, neque amplius praestare poteras, quia ad tuam isto tempore essentiam constituendam a divina voluntate, ac potentia nihil amplius datum erat.
Nor do I see in your writings this consequence, namely that those actions which I mark with the name of crimes would lead you away from the knowledge of God and from his love. For you have only carried out God’s will, and could do no more, because nothing more had been given by the divine will and power to constitute your essence at that time.
Quomodo sic determinatum, ac dependens opus te a divino amore aberrare facit? aberrare est confusum, et independens esse, et hoc secundum te est impossibile. Nam sive hoc, sive illud, sive plus, sive minus perfectionis edimus, illud ad esse nostrum pro illo tempore immediate a Deo accipimus: quomodo ergo possumus aberrare? vel ego, per errorem quid intelligatur, non capio. Attamen in hoc, in hoc, inquam, solo causa vel mei, vel tui erroris latere debet.
How does a work so determined and dependent make you stray from the divine love? To stray is to be confused and independent, and this according to you is impossible. For whether we bring forth this or that, more or less perfection, we receive it for our being at that time immediately from God: how, then, can we stray? Or else I do not grasp what is understood by error. And yet in this, in this alone, I say, the cause of either my error or yours must lie.
Hic multa alia dicerem, et quaererem, primo, an substantiae intellectuales alio modo, quam vitae expertes a Deo dependeant? licet enim entia intellectualia plus essentiae, quam vita carentia involvant, annon utraque Deo, Deique decreto ad suum motum in genere, et ad talem motum in specie conservandum opus habent, et per consequens, quatenus dependent, annon una, eademque ratione dependeant?
Here I would say and ask many other things: first, whether intellectual substances depend on God in another way than lifeless ones?
