For although intellectual beings involve more essence than lifeless ones, do not both need God, and God’s decree, to preserve their motion in general and such a motion in particular, and consequently, insofar as they depend, do they not depend in one and the same way?
Secundo, quia, quam ei D. des Cartes adsignavit, libertatem animae non concedis, quaenam sit differentia inter dependentiam intellectualium, et anima carentium substantiarum? et si nullam volendi habent libertatem, quomodo tu eas a Deo dependere concipias? et quo pacto anima a Deo dependeat? Tertio, siquidem nostra anima ea non sit praedita libertate, anne nostra actio proprie Dei actio, et nostra voluntas proprie Dei sit voluntas?
Second, since you do not grant the soul the freedom which Mr Descartes assigned to it, what difference is there between the dependence of intellectual substances and of soulless ones? And if they have no freedom of willing, how do you conceive that they depend on God? And in what way does the soul depend on God? Third, if our soul is not endowed with that freedom, is not our action properly God’s action, and our will properly God’s will?
Plura alia quaerere possem, sed tam multa a te petere non ausim: solummodo ad praecedentia responsum tuum brevi exspecto, an forte hoc medio adhibito sententiam tuam melius intelligam, ut postea de hac re coram tecum pluribus agerem. Ubi enim tuum nactus fuero responsum, Leydam profectus, si gratum sit, in transitu te salutabo.
I could ask many other things, but I would not dare to ask so much of you: I only await your reply to the preceding points soon, to see whether by this means I may perhaps understand your opinion better, so that I may afterwards treat this matter at more length with you in person. For when I have obtained your reply, I shall go to Leiden, and, if it is agreeable, I shall greet you on the way.1
  1. 1 The visit took place: see the beginning of Letter 24, and Nadler pp. 254–255.
His nixus, dico ex animo, tibi salute dicta, me manere tibi addictissimum, atque officiosissimum
Relying on this, I say from my heart, with greetings to you, that I remain your most devoted and most dutiful
G. de Blyenbergh.
W. van Blyenbergh.
[G IV 144] Dordraci 19. Febr. 1665.
Dordrecht, 19 February 1665.
P.S. Nimia festinatione hanc inserere quaestionem oblitus sum: an quod alias nobis eveniret, prudentia nostra non impedire queamus.
P.S. In too great haste I forgot to insert this question: whether we cannot by our prudence prevent what would otherwise happen to us.1
  1. 1 Spinoza declines to answer it at the end of Letter 23.

Epistola XXIII

Letter 23

Spinoza to Willem van Blyenbergh

Viro Doctissimo, ac Ornatissimo,
To the most learned and most accomplished man
Guilielmo de Blyenbergh
Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine et amice,
Sir and friend,
[G IV 144] Duas abs te hac septimana accepi epistolas;a posterior, 9 Martii exarata, tantum ei inserviebat, ut me de priore, 19 Februarii scripta, et mihi Schiedamo missa, certiorem redderet.
I received two letters from you this week; the later, written on 9 March, served only to inform me of the earlier, written on 19 February1 and sent on to me from Schiedam.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 511–515, Letter 36), headed Versio. Gebhardt prints Spinoza’s Dutch after it (G IV 144–152); it is dated ‘Voorburgh den 13 Maart 1665’ and ends otherwise than the Latin (see the note at the end of the letter).
  1. 1 Letter 22.
In hac priore te de eo queri video, quod dixerim nullam apud te demonstrationem locum habere posse etc. quasi illud mearum rationum respectu, quia statim tibi non fecerunt satis, locutus fuerim, quod a mente mea longe abest: respiciebam propria tua verba, quae sic habent. Et siquando post diutinam investigationem accideret, ut naturalis mea scientia videretur cum hoc verbo vel pugnare, vel non satis bene etc. illud verbum tantae apud me est authoritatis, ut conceptus, quos existimo clare percipere, mihi potius suspecti sint, etc.
In the earlier I see that you complain of my having said that no demonstration could have a place with you, etc., as if I had said it with regard to my reasons, because they did not satisfy you at once; which is far from my mind: I had in view your own words, which run thus:2 ‘And if it should ever happen after long investigation that my natural knowledge seemed either to conflict with this word, or not well enough, etc., that word has such authority with me that the concepts which I think I perceive clearly are rather suspect to me’, etc.
  1. 2 Letter 20. The Latin wording differs a little from that of the Latin version of Letter 20, since both translate the Dutch.
Adeo ut tua tantum verba breviter repetierim, nec propterea credo, me ulla in re irae causam praebuisse, eo magis, quod ea, tanquam rationem, adducebam, quibus magnum nostrum dissensum ostenderem.
So that I only repeated your words briefly, and I do not therefore believe that I gave you cause for anger in anything, the more so since I adduced them as a reason, to show our great disagreement.
[G IV 145] Porro, quia in calce secundae epistolae scripseras, te tantummodo sperare, et optare, ut in fide, ac spe perseveres, atque reliqua, quae nobis invicem de intellectu naturali persuademus, tibi esse indifferentia; animo volvebam, uti et iam revolvo, meas nulli usui futuras, mihique ea de causa consultum magis esse, ut studia (quae alioqui tamdiu intermittere cogor) non negligerem propter res, quae nullum fructum possunt largiri.
Further, because at the end of your second letter1 you had written that you only hoped and wished to persevere in faith and hope, and that the rest, which we persuade each other of concerning the natural intellect, was indifferent to you, I turned it over in my mind, as I still do, that my letters would be of no use, and that for that reason it would be wiser for me not to neglect my studies (which I am otherwise forced to interrupt for so long) for the sake of things which can yield no fruit.
  1. 1 Letter 20.
Nec hoc primae epistolae meae contrariatur: quia ibi te, ut merum philosophum, qui (sicuti haud pauci, qui se Christianos autumant, concedunt) nullum alium veritatis Lydium habet lapidem, praeter naturalem intellectum, non vero theologiam, considerabam. Sed de hac re me aliter edocuisti, simulque ostendisti, quod fundamentum, cui nostram amicitiam superaedificare animus erat, non, ut arbitrabar, esset iactum.
Nor does this contradict my first letter: for there I considered you as a pure philosopher, who (as not a few who call themselves Christians grant) has no other touchstone of truth than the natural intellect, and not theology. But on this matter you have taught me otherwise, and at the same time shown that the foundation on which I intended to build our friendship had not, as I thought, been laid.
Denique reliqua quod attinet, ea plerumque tali modo inter disputandum contingunt, ut propterea humanitatis limites non transgrediamur, et hac de causa in secunda tua epistola, nec non in hac his similia, tanquam non animadversa, praetermittam. Haec de tua offensione, ut, me ei nullam dedisse causam, ostenderem, multo minus me ferre haud posse, ut mihi quisquam obloquatur. Nunc, quo ad tuas obiectiones rursus respondeam, me converto.
Finally, as for the rest, such things mostly happen in disputing in such a way that we do not on that account transgress the limits of courtesy; and for this reason I shall pass over things of this kind in your second letter, and also in this one, as if I had not noticed them. So much for your offence, to show that I gave no cause for it, much less that I cannot bear anyone to contradict me. Now I turn to answering your objections again.
Statuo ergo primo, Deum absolute, et revera causam esse omnium, quae essentiam habent, quaecunque etiam illa sint. Si iam poteris demonstrare, malum, Errorem, Scelera, etc. quicquam esse, quod essentiam exprimit, tibi penitus concedam, Deum scelerum, mali, erroris, etc. causam esse. Videor mihi sufficienter ostendisse id, quod formam mali, erroris, sceleris ponit, non in aliquo, quod essentiam exprimit, consistere, ideoque dici non posse, Deum eius esse causam.
I maintain, then, first, that God is absolutely and really the cause of all things that have essence, whatever they may be. If you can now demonstrate that evil, error, crimes, etc. are anything that expresses essence, I shall grant you entirely that God is the cause of crimes, of evil, of error, etc. I seem to myself to have shown sufficiently that what posits the form of evil, error or crime does not consist in anything that expresses essence, and that therefore it cannot be said that God is its cause.
Neronis, verbi gratia, matricidium, quatenus aliquid positivum comprehendebat, scelus non erat: nam facinus externum fecit, simulque intentionem ad trucidandam Matrem Orestes habuit, et tamen, saltem uti Nero, ita non accusatur. Quodnam ergo Neronis scelus? Non aliud, quam quod hoc facinore ostenderet se ingratum, immisericordem, ac inobedientem esse. Certum autem est, nihil horum aliquid essentiae exprimere, et idcirco Deum eorum etiam non fuisse causam, licet causa actus, et intentionis Neronis fuerit.
Nero’s matricide, for example, insofar as it comprehended something positive, was not a crime:2 for Orestes did the same outward deed, and at the same time had the intention of killing his mother, and yet he is not accused, at least not as Nero is. What, then, was Nero’s crime? Nothing other than that by this deed he showed himself ungrateful, unmerciful and disobedient. But it is certain that none of these expresses any essence, and therefore God was not their cause either, though he was the cause of Nero’s act and intention.
  1. 2 Compare Part IV, Prop. 59, Schol., on the act of striking, which considered physically is a virtue.
Porro velim hic notari, nos, dum philosophice loquimur, non debere Phrasibus theologiae uti: Nam, quia theologia Deum passim, nec temere, ut hominem perfectum repraesentat, propterea oportunum est, ut in theologia dicatur Deum quicquam cupere, Deum taedio operibus improborum affici, et proborum delectari; in philosophia vero, ubi clare percipimus, quod Deo illa attributa, quae hominem perfectum reddunt, tam aegre possunt tribui, et adsignari, quam si ea, quae elephantum, asinumve perficiunt, homini tribueremus: Ibi haec, et his similia verba nullum obtinent locum, nec ibi sine nostrorum conceptuum summa confusione ea usurpare licet.
Further, I should like it noted here that when we speak philosophically we ought not to use the phrases of theology: for because theology often, and not without reason, represents God as a perfect man, it is fitting that in theology it should be said that God desires something, that God is affected with distaste by the works of the wicked and takes delight in those of the good; but in philosophy, where we perceive clearly that the attributes which make a man perfect can be ascribed and assigned to God as little as if we were to ascribe to a man those which make an elephant or an ass perfect,3 there these and similar words have no place, nor may we use them there without the greatest confusion of our concepts.
  1. 3 Nadler quotes this passage (Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 254).
Quare philosophice loquendo, dici nequit, Deum a quoquam quicquam petere, neque ei taediosum, aut gratum quid esse; haec quippe omnia humana sunt attributa, quae in Deo locum non habent.
Therefore, speaking philosophically, it cannot be said that God asks anything of anyone, nor that anything is distasteful or pleasing to him; for all these are human attributes, which have no place in God.
[G IV 148] Notari tandem voluissem, quod, quamvis opera proborum, (hoc est, eorum, qui claram Dei habent ideam, ad quam cuncta eorum opera, ut et cogitationes determinantur) et improborum, (hoc est, eorum, qui Dei ideam haud possident, sed tantum rerum terrenarum ideas, ad quas eorum opera, cogitationesque determinantur) et denique omnium eorum, quae sunt, ex Dei aeternis legibus, et decretis necessario profluant, continuoque a Deo dependeant, attamen non solum gradibus; sed et essentia ab invicem differunt: licet etenim mus aeque, ac angelus, et aeque tristitia, ac laetitia, a Deo dependeant, nequit tamen mus species angeli, et tristitia species laetitiae esse.
Finally, I should have wished it noted that although the works of the good (that is, of those who have a clear idea of God, to which all their works and thoughts are determined), and of the wicked (that is, of those who do not possess the idea of God, but only ideas of earthly things, to which their works and thoughts are determined), and finally of all things that are, flow necessarily from God’s eternal laws and decrees and depend continually on God, yet they differ from one another not only in degree but also in essence: for although a mouse depends on God as much as an angel, and sadness as much as joy, yet a mouse cannot be a species of angel, nor sadness a species of joy.
Hisce opinor me tuis Obiectionibus (si eas probe intellexi; quippe interdum dubius haereo, num conclusiones, quas inde deducis, non differant ab ipsa propositione, quam demonstrare suscipis) respondisse.
With this I think I have answered your objections (if I have understood them properly; for at times I am in doubt whether the conclusions you draw from them do not differ from the very proposition you undertake to demonstrate).
[G IV 149] Verum illud clarius patebit, si ad quaestiones propositas ex his fundamentis respondeam. Prima est, an occidere Deo adeo acceptum sit, ac eleemosynas elargiri? altera est, an furari Dei respectu aeque bonum sit, ac iustum esse? tertia denique est, an, si daretur animus, cum cuius singulari natura non pugnaret; sed conveniret, libidinibus obtemperare, et scelera perpetrare, an in eo, inquam, ratio virtutis daretur, quae ipsi, ut bonum faceret, et malum omitteret, persuaderet?
But this will be clearer if I answer the questions proposed on these foundations. The first is whether killing is as acceptable to God as giving alms?1 The second is whether stealing is, in relation to God, as good as being just? The third, finally, is whether, if there were a mind with whose singular nature it did not conflict, but agreed, to obey its lusts and commit crimes, whether, I say, there would be in it a reason of virtue which would persuade it to do good and refrain from evil?
  1. 1 Letter 22.
Ad primam hoc do responsum, me (Philosophice loquendo) quid his verbis, Deo acceptum esse, velis, nescire. Si roges, an Deus hunc non odio habeat, illum vero diligat? an Deum alius contumelia affecerit, alius favore prosecutus fuerit? respondeo, quod non.
To the first I give this answer: that (speaking philosophically) I do not know what you mean by these words, ‘to be acceptable to God’. If you ask whether God does not hate the one and love the other? Whether one has affronted God and the other shown him favour? I answer no.
Si vero quaestio est, numne homines, qui occidunt, et eleemosynas distribuunt, aeque probi, et perfecti sint? Rursus negando respondeo.
But if the question is whether men who kill and men who give alms are equally good and perfect? Again I answer in the negative.
[G IV 150] Ad secundam regero: si bonum respectu Dei inferat, iustum aliquid boni Deo praestare, et furem aliquid mali, respondeo neque iustum, neque furem in Deo delectationem, aut taedium posse causari; si vero quaeratur, an utrumque illud opus, quatenus aliquid reale, et a Deo causatum est, aeque sit perfectum? dico, si ad opera tantum animum advertamus, et ad talem modum, posse fieri, ut utrumque aeque fit perfectum.
To the second I reply: if ‘good in relation to God’ implies that the just man does God some good and the thief some evil, I answer that neither the just man nor the thief can cause God delight or distaste; but if it is asked whether both those works, insofar as they are something real and caused by God, are equally perfect, I say that if we attend only to the works, and in such a way, it may be that both are equally perfect.
Si igitur interroges, numne fur, et iustus aeque sint perfecti, beatique? respondeo, non. Per iustum namque eum intelligo, qui constanter cupit, ut unusquisque, quod suum est, possideat; quam cupiditatem ego in mea Ethica (necdum edita) in piis ex clara, quam de seipsis, et Deo habent, cognitione necessario originem ducere, demonstro. Et quoniam eius generis cupiditatem fur non habet, necessario Dei, et sui ipsius cognitione, hoc est, primario, quod nos homines reddit, destituitur.
If, then, you ask whether the thief and the just man are equally perfect and blessed? I answer no. For by a just man I understand one who constantly desires that each should possess what is his; and I demonstrate in my Ethics (not yet published)1 that this desire necessarily arises in the pious from the clear knowledge which they have of themselves and of God. And since the thief does not have a desire of that kind, he necessarily lacks the knowledge of God and of himself, that is, the chief thing that makes us men.
  1. 1 In the Ethics as published, Part IV, Prop. 37: ‘The good which each one who follows virtue wants for himself he will also desire for other men, and the more so, the greater the knowledge of God he has’; justice is explained in its second scholium.
Si tamen ulterius quaeras, quid te queat movere, ut magis hoc opus, quod virtutem nuncupo, quam aliud facias? dico, me non posse scire, qua via, ex infinitis, Deus utatur, ut te ad hoc opus determinet. Posset esse, ut Deus tibi clare sui ideam impresserit, ut mundum oblivioni propter sui amorem traderes, reliquosque homines, sicut te ipsum, amares; et perspicuum est huiusmodi animi constitutionem cum caeteris omnibus, quae malae vocantur, pugnare, et ea de causa in uno subiecto non posse esse.
If, however, you ask further what could move you to do this work which I call virtue rather than another, I say that I cannot know which way, out of infinitely many, God uses to determine you to this work. It could be that God has impressed on you so clear an idea of himself that for love of him you would consign the world to oblivion and love other men as yourself; and it is plain that such a constitution of mind conflicts with all the others that are called evil, and for that reason they cannot be in one subject.
Porro fundamenta ethices explicandi, et etiam cuncta mea dicta demonstrandi non est hic locus, quia tantum in eo sum, ut ad quaestiones tuas responsum dem, et eas a me avertam, arceamque.
Further, this is not the place to explain the foundations of ethics and also to demonstrate all that I have said, since I am only concerned to answer your questions, and to turn them aside and ward them off from me.
Quantum denique tertiam quaestionem attinet, ea contradictionem supponit, mihique aeque videbatur, ac si quis rogaret: Si melius cum alicuius natura conveniret, ut seipsum suspenderet, an rationes darentur, ut se non suspenderet? Verum, eiusmodi dari naturam, sit possibile, tunc affirmo, (etiamsi liberum arbitrium concederem, sive non concederem) si quis videt, se commodius in cruce posse vivere, quam mensae suae accumbentem, eum stultissime agere, si se ipsum non suspenderet; et is, qui clare videret, se scelera patrando revera perfectiore et meliore vita, vel essentia, quam virtutem sectando, posse frui, is etiam stultus foret, si illa non faceret. Nam scelera respectu istiusmodi naturae humanae perversae virtus essent.
As for the third question, finally, it supposes a contradiction, and seemed to me just as if someone were to ask: If it agreed better with someone’s nature to hang himself, would there be reasons why he should not hang himself? But suppose it possible that there is such a nature; then I affirm (whether I granted free will or not) that if someone sees that he can live more comfortably on the gallows than sitting at his table, he would act most foolishly if he did not hang himself; and he who saw clearly that by committing crimes he could really enjoy a more perfect and better life or essence than by pursuing virtue would also be a fool if he did not commit them. For crimes would be virtue in relation to such a perverted human nature.
Ad aliam quaestionem, quam calci epistolae addidisti, quia una hora vel centum eius generis quaerere possemus, nec tamen unquam ad conclusionem unius perveniremus, et ipse responsionem non adeo urges, non respondebo: hoc tempore tantum dicam, etc.a
To the other question which you added at the foot of your letter,2 since we could ask a hundred of that kind in an hour without ever coming to a conclusion about one, and you yourself do not press much for an answer, I shall not reply: at this time I shall only say, etc.
  1. a Spinoza’s Dutch ends (G IV 152): ‘… and for this time only say that I shall expect you at the time which you appoint, and that you will be very welcome to me. But I should like it to be soon, because I am already intending to go to Amsterdam for a week or two. Meanwhile, after hearty greetings, I remain’, followed by the initials U. E. V. E. D. and the signature ‘B. de Spinoza’.
  1. 2 The postscript of Letter 22.

Epistola XXIV

Letter 24

Willem van Blyenbergh to Spinoza

Viro clarissimo,
To the most distinguished man
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Guilielmus de Blyenbergh.
from Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine, et amice.
Sir and friend,
[G IV 153] Ubi tua honorabar praesentia,a ut in ea perseverarem non tempus, multo minus memoria permittebat, ut quae colloquentes tractavimus, ei mandarem; licet ego quam primum a te discesseram, omnes memoriae vires, quo audita retinerem, colligerem.
When I was honoured with your presence,1 time did not allow me to remain in it, much less did memory allow me to commit to it what we discussed together; although as soon as I had left you I gathered all the powers of my memory to retain what I had heard.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 515–517, Letter 37), headed Versio; Gebhardt prints the Dutch first (G IV 153–157). The digital text of Gebhardt reads honorabor; the 1677 edition has honorabar.
  1. 1 Blyenbergh had visited Spinoza, as he proposed at the end of Letter 22; they talked about freedom, sin and the nature of the soul (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., pp. 254–255).
In proximum ergo profectus locum tuas opiniones chartae mandare conabar; sed experiebar tunc, me revera ne quartam quidem tractatorum partem retinuisse; adeo ut tibi sim excusandus, si adhuc semel, tantum quaerendo de iis, in quibus mentem tuam, vel non bene perceperim, vel non bene retinuerim, tibi molestus sim. Optem, ut hunc laborem aliquo beneficio tibi compensare facultas daretur.
Having gone on to the next place, then, I tried to commit your opinions to paper; but I then found that I had in fact retained not even a quarter of what was discussed;2 so that you must excuse me if I trouble you once more, asking only about those things in which I either did not perceive your mind well or did not retain it well. I wish that I had the means of repaying this labour to you with some favour.
  1. 2 Nadler quotes this passage (p. 255).
Primum erat, quomodo, ubi principia, et cogitata tua metaphysica lego, quae tua, quaeque Cartesii sit sententia, internoscere possim?
The first was: how, when I read your Principles and Metaphysical Thoughts, can I distinguish which is your opinion and which Descartes’s?
[G IV 154] Secundum, an proprie detur error, et in quo consistat?
The second: whether there is properly error, and in what it consists?
Tertium, qua ratione statuas, voluntatem non esse liberam.
The third: on what ground you maintain that the will is not free.
Quartum, quid his verbis, quae L. M. in praefatione tuo nomine scripsit, intelligas, nempe. Cum contra author noster admittat quidem in rerum natura esse substantiam cogitantem: attamen neget illam constituere essentiam mentis humanae; sed statuat, eodem modo, quo extensio nullis limitibus determinata est, cogitationem etiam nullis limitibus determinari; adeoque quemadmodum corpus humanum non est absolute, sed tantum certo modo secundum leges naturae extensae per motum et quietem determinata extensio; sic etiam mentem, sive animam humanam non esse absolute, sed tantum secundum leges naturae cogitantis per ideas certo modo determinatam cogitationem; quae necessario dari concluditur, ubi corpus humanum existere incipit.
The fourth: what you understand by these words which L. M. wrote in your name in the preface, namely: ‘Whereas our author admits indeed that there is in nature a thinking substance, yet denies that it constitutes the essence of the human mind; but maintains that, in the same way in which extension is determined by no limits, thought too is determined by no limits; and so, just as the human body is not extension absolutely, but only extension determined in a certain way according to the laws of extended nature by motion and rest, so also the human mind or soul is not thought absolutely, but only thought determined in a certain way according to the laws of thinking nature by ideas; which is concluded to exist necessarily when the human body begins to exist.’1
  1. 1 Meyer’s preface to the Principles (G I 132). Compare, in the Ethics, Part II, Prop. 11 and Prop. 13: the mind is the idea of the body.
Unde sequi videtur, sicut corpus humanum ex millenis compositum est corporibus, ita etiam humanam mentem ex millenis constare cogitationibus;. et quemadmodum humanum corpus in millena resolvitur, unde componebatur, corpora, sic etiam mentem nostram, ubi corpus deserit, in tam multiplices, ex quibus constabat, cogitationes resolvi: et sicuti resolutae humani nostri corporis partes non amplius unitae manent; sed alia corpora inter ea se insinuant; ita sequi quoque videtur, mente nostra dissoluta, illas innumeras cogitationes, quibus constabat, non amplius combinatas, sed divisas esse: et quemadmodum corpora soluta corpora quidem manent; sed non humana, ita a morte quoque nostram substantiam cogitantem quidem resolvi, ut cogitationes, vel substantiae cogitantes maneant, non vero ita, ut essentia erat earum, quum mens humana dicebantur.
From which it seems to follow that, just as the human body is composed of thousands of bodies, so also the human mind consists of thousands of thoughts;2 and as the human body is resolved into the thousands of bodies of which it was composed, so also our mind, when it leaves the body, is resolved into the many thoughts of which it consisted; and as the resolved parts of our human body no longer remain united, but other bodies insinuate themselves between them, so it seems to follow too that when our mind is dissolved, those countless thoughts of which it consisted are no longer combined but divided; and as dissolved bodies remain bodies but not human ones, so too after death our thinking substance is resolved in such a way that thoughts or thinking substances remain, but not such as their essence was when they were called a human mind.
  1. 2 Compare Part II, Prop. 15: the idea which constitutes the formal being of the human mind is not simple, but composed of very many ideas.
Unde mihi videtur, ac si statueres, substantiam hominis cogitantem mutari, et instar corporum resolvi, quin imo quasdam ita, ut de impiis (ni me fallat memoria) affirmabas, omnino perire, nullamque sibi retinere cogitationem reliquam. Et sicut D. des Cartes, ut L. M. habet, mentem esse substantiam absolute cogitantem tantum praesupponit, ita et Tu, et L. M. praesupponitis maximam partem uti mihi videmini: quare tuam hoc in negotio mentem clare non percipio.
From which it seems to me as if you maintained that the thinking substance of man is changed and resolved like bodies, indeed some of them in such a way that, as you affirmed of the impious3 (if my memory does not deceive me), they perish altogether and retain no thought left to them. And just as Mr Descartes, as L. M. has it, only presupposes that the mind is an absolutely thinking substance,4 so you too and L. M. presuppose for the most part, as it seems to me: therefore I do not perceive your mind clearly in this matter.
  1. 3 Compare Part V, Prop. 38, Schol., on the part of the mind which perishes with the body.
  2. 4 Meyer’s preface (G I 132): Descartes ‘only supposes, does not prove, that the human mind is an absolutely thinking substance’.
[G IV 156] Quintum est, quod tam in colloquio, quam in postrema tua epistola, 13. Martii scripta, statuis, ex clara Dei, et nostri cognitione oriri constantiam, qua, ut quilibet suum sibi habeat, cupimus: Verum hic explicandum restat, qua ratione Dei, et nostri cognitio in nobis constantem voluntatem producat, ut unusquisque, quod suum est, possideat, hoc est, qua via id ex Dei cognitione fluat, vel nos obstringat, ut virtutem amemus, et ea negligamus opera, quae vitia dicimus; et unde procedat (quandoquidem occidere, et furari, iuxta te, aeque quid positivum, ac eleemosynas dare, comprehendant) cur caedem committere non tantum perfectionis, beatitudinis, et acquiescentiae, quantum eleemosynas distribuere, involvat.
The fifth is that, both in conversation and in your last letter, written on 13 March,1 you maintain that from the clear knowledge of God and of ourselves arises the constancy by which we desire that each should have his own: but here it remains to be explained in what way the knowledge of God and of ourselves produces in us the constant will that each should possess what is his; that is, by what way this flows from the knowledge of God, or binds us to love virtue and to neglect the works which we call vices; and whence it comes (since killing and stealing, according to you, comprehend something positive as much as giving alms) that committing murder does not involve as much perfection, blessedness and contentment as distributing alms.
  1. 1 Letter 23.
Dices forte, ut in postrema, quae 13. Martii consignata est, hanc quaestionem ad Ethicam spectare, eamque inibi a te moveri; verum quum sine huius quaestionis, ut et praecedentium elucidatione, ego tuam nequeam percipere mentem, quin semper absurditates supersint, quas conciliare non possum, amice rogo ut latius mihi ad eas respondeas; praesertim ut praecipuas quasdam definitiones, postulata, et axiomata, quibus tua Ethica, et haec imprimis quaestio innititur, proponas, et explanes. Forte, quia labor deterret, te excusabis, verum ut meae satisfacias petitioni saltem hac vice flagito, eo quod absque ultimae quaestionis solutione mentem tuam nunquam recte sim percepturus.
You will perhaps say, as in your last letter, dated 13 March, that this question belongs to the Ethics and is treated there by you; but since without the elucidation of this question, as of the preceding ones, I cannot perceive your mind without absurdities always remaining which I cannot reconcile, I ask you in friendly fashion to answer me more fully on them; especially to set out and explain certain chief definitions, postulates and axioms on which your Ethics, and this question above all, rests. Perhaps you will excuse yourself because the labour deters you; but I beg you to satisfy my request at least this once, because without the solution of the last question I shall never perceive your mind rightly.
Optem, ut laborem tuum aliquo beneficio compensare liceret. Tempus duarum, triumve septimanarum praescribere non audeo; tantum ut ante iter tuum Amstelaedamense ad has responsum des, requiro.
I wish that I could repay your labour with some favour. I do not dare prescribe a time of two or three weeks; I only ask that you answer this before your journey to Amsterdam.2
  1. 2 Announced at the end of Spinoza’s Dutch letter of 13 March (see the note at the end of Letter 23).
Hoc ubi praestiteris, me tibi summopere devincies, egoque ostendam me esse et permanere, Mi Domine, tuum ad quaevis officia paratissimum,
When you have done this, you will oblige me greatly, and I shall show that I am and remain, Sir, most ready for any service to you,
G. de Blyenberg.
W. van Blyenbergh.
[G IV 157] Dordraci 27. Martii 1665.
Dordrecht, 27 March 1665.

Epistola XXVII

Letter 27

Spinoza to Willem van Blyenbergh

Humanissimo, Ornatissimoque viro,
To the most courteous and most accomplished man
Guilielmo de Blyenbergh
Willem van Blyenbergh
Mi Domine, et amice.
Sir and friend,
[G IV 160] Ubi tua, 27. Martii ad me data,a mihi tradebatur, ut Amstelaedamum proficiscerer, in procinctu stabam. Quare, ubi dimidium eius perlegeram, domi relinquebam, donec rediissem, ut tum responderem; quoniam putabam, eam nihil, nisi quaestiones, ad primam spectantes controversiam, comprehendere.
When your letter of 27 March1 was delivered to me, I was on the point of setting out for Amsterdam. So when I had read half of it, I left it at home until I returned, so as to answer it then; because I thought that it contained nothing but questions concerning our first controversy.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 518–519, Letter 38), headed Versio. Gebhardt prints Spinoza’s Dutch after it (G IV 160–162), dated ‘Voorburg den 3. Juni’ (1665); it says that the second letter came under cover to Spinoza’s landlord.
  1. 1 Letter 24.
Ego vero postea eam perlegens longe aliud eius argumentum esse deprehendebam, in eaque non modo probationem eorum, quae in praefatione mearum demonstrationum Geometricarum in Cartesii principia curavi scribi, eo tantum fine, ut meam sententiam cuilibet indicarem, non vero probarem, et hominibus persuaderem; sed etiam magnam ethices partem, quae, ut cuivis notum, metaphysica, et Physica fundari debet, desiderari.
But reading it through afterwards, I found that its argument was quite different, and that in it you ask not only for the proof of what I had had written in the preface to my Geometrical Demonstrations of Descartes’s Principles,2 only in order to indicate my opinion to anyone, not to prove it and persuade men of it, but also for a great part of ethics, which, as everyone knows, must be founded on metaphysics and physics.
  1. 2 Meyer’s preface, from which Blyenbergh had quoted in Letter 24.
Qua de causa, ut tuis facerem satis quaestionibus, a me impetrare non potui; sed occasionem exspectare volui, quo amicissime coram, ut postulato desistas, a te peterem, meae negationis rationem darem, et denique ostenderem eas ad primae nostrae controversiae solutionem non facere; sed econtra maximam partem a solutione istius litis pendere. Tantum ergo abest, ut mea opinio, rerum necessitatem spectans, sine iis non possit percipi; quia hae revera percipi nequeunt, antequam ea in antecessum intelligatur.
For this reason I could not bring myself to satisfy your questions; but I wished to wait for an opportunity to ask you in person, in the friendliest way, to desist from your request,3 to give the reason for my refusal, and finally to show that they do not contribute to the solution of our first controversy, but on the contrary depend for the most part on the solution of that dispute. So far is it, then, from being the case that my opinion concerning the necessity of things cannot be perceived without them, that they in fact cannot be perceived before it is understood.
  1. 3 Nadler quotes this as Spinoza’s polite but unmistakable request to be left alone (p. 255).
Antequam vero offerebatur occasio, hac hebdomada alterum adhuc mihi tradebatur epistolium, quod aliquam displicentiam, ex nimia mora, prae se ferre videtur. Et ideo haec pauca ad te scribere necessitas me coegit, ut de proposito, et decreto meo certiorem, ut iam reddidi, te redderem.
But before the opportunity offered, another short letter was delivered to me this week, which seems to show some displeasure at the long delay. And so necessity compelled me to write these few lines to you, to inform you of my intention and decision, as I have now done.
Spero te, negotio perpenso, ultro a tua petitione destiturum, et tamen animum in me propensum servaturum. Ego a mea parte pro viribus meis in omnibus demonstrabo, me esse, etc.
I hope that, having weighed the matter, you will of your own accord desist from your request,4 and yet keep your kindly disposition towards me. For my part I shall show in all things, as far as I can, that I am, etc.
  1. 4 The correspondence ends here. Nine years later Blyenbergh published a refutation of the Theological-Political Treatise (Leiden, 1674) (Nadler p. 255).

Epistola XXXII

Letter 32

Spinoza to Henry Oldenburg

Nobilissimo, ac Doctissimo viro,
To the most noble and most learned man
Henrico Oldenburgio
Henry Oldenburg
Vir Nobilissime,
Most noble Sir,
[G IV 169] Quod me ad philosophandum tu,a et Nobilissimus D. Boylius benigne hortamini, maximas habeo gratias; ego quidem pro tenuitate mei ingenii, quantum queo, pergo, non dubitans interim de vestro auxilio, et benevolentia.
I give you the greatest thanks that you and the most noble Mr Boyle1 kindly urge me to philosophize; for my part, I go on as far as I can, according to the slenderness of my talents, not doubting meanwhile your help and goodwill.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 440–442, Letter 15), as Gebhardt prints it first (G IV 169–175). The original, which belongs to the Royal Society of London (VVL³ III 119), is dated ‘Voorburg, 20 November 1665’ and ends with a postscript asking whether all the astronomers judge from the comets’ motion that there were two comets, or only so as to save Kepler’s hypothesis; Gebhardt prints it after the printed text.
  1. 1 Robert Boyle, the chemist, some of whose essays Oldenburg, the secretary of the Royal Society, had sent Spinoza at Rijnsburg in 1661 (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 225).
Ubi quaeris, quid sentiam circa quaestionem, quae in eo versatur, ut cognoscamus, quomodo unaquaeque pars naturae cum suo toto conveniat, et qua ratione cum reliquis cohaereat, puto te rogare rationes, quibus persuademur unamquamque naturae partem cum suo toto convenire, et cum reliquis cohaerere. Nam cognoscere, quomodo revera cohaereant, et unaquaeque pars cum suo toto conveniat, id me ignorare dixi in antecedenti mea epistola; quia ad hoc cognoscendum requireretur totam naturam, omnesque eius partes cognoscere.
When you ask what I think about the question which turns on our knowing how each part of nature agrees with its whole and in what way it coheres with the rest, I think you are asking for the reasons by which we are persuaded that each part of nature agrees with its whole and coheres with the rest. For to know how they really cohere, and how each part agrees with its whole, I said in my preceding letter that I do not know, because to know this it would be required to know the whole of nature and all its parts.
Conabor igitur rationem ostendere, quae me id affirmare cogit; attamen prius monere velim, me naturae non tribuere pulchritudinem, deformitatem, ordinem, neque confusionem. Nam res non, nisi respective ad nostram imaginationem, possunt dici pulchrae, aut deformes, ordinatae, aut confusae.
I shall try, therefore, to show the reason which compels me to affirm it; but first I should like to point out that I do not attribute to nature beauty, ugliness, order or confusion. For things cannot be called beautiful or ugly, ordered or confused, except in relation to our imagination.2
  1. 2 As the Appendix to Part I argues of order and confusion, beauty and ugliness, which are ‘only modes of imagining’ (Part I, Appendix).
[G IV 170] Per partium igitur cohaerentiam nihil aliud intelligo, quam quod leges, sive natura unius partis ita sese accommodat legibus, sive naturae alterius, ut quam minime sibi contrarientur. Circa totum, et partes considero res eatenus, ut partes alicuius totius, quatenus earum natura invicem se accommodat, ut, quoad fieri potest, inter se consentiant, quatenus vero inter se discrepant, eatenus unaquaeque ideam ab aliis distinctam in nostra mente format, ac proinde, ut totum, non ut pars, consideratur.
By the coherence of parts, then, I understand nothing else than that the laws, or nature, of one part so adapts itself to the laws, or nature, of another that they are as little contrary to one another as possible. Concerning whole and parts, I consider things as parts of some whole insofar as their natures so adapt themselves to one another that they agree among themselves as far as possible; but insofar as they differ among themselves, to that extent each forms in our mind an idea distinct from the others, and so is considered as a whole, not as a part.
Ex. gr. cum motus particularum lymphae, chyli, etc. invicem pro ratione magnitudinis, et figurae ita se accommodant, ut plane inter se consentiant, unumque fluidum simul omnes constituant, eatenus tantum chylus, lympha, etc. ut partes sanguinis considerantur: quatenus vero concipimus particulas lymphaticas ratione figurae, et motus, a particulis chyli discrepare, eatenus eas, ut totum, non ut partem, consideramus.
For example, when the motions of the particles of lymph, chyle, etc. so adapt themselves to one another, according to their size and shape, that they agree entirely among themselves and all together constitute one fluid, to that extent only are chyle, lymph, etc. considered as parts of the blood; but insofar as we conceive the lymphatic particles as differing from the particles of chyle by reason of their shape and motion, to that extent we consider them as a whole, not as a part.
[G IV 171] Fingamus iam, si placet vermiculum in sanguine vivere, qui visu ad discernendas particulas sanguinis, lymphae, etc. valeret, et ratione ad observandum, quomodo unaquaeque particula ex alterius occursu, vel resilit, vel partem sui motus communicat, etc. Ille quidem in hoc sanguine, ut nos in hac parte universi, viveret, et unamquamque sanguinis particulam, ut totum, non vero ut partem, consideraret, nec scire posset, quomodo partes omnes ab universali natura sanguinis moderantur, et invicem, prout universalis natura sanguinis exigit, se accommodare coguntur, ut certa ratione inter se consentiant.
Let us now feign, if you please, that a little worm lives in the blood, which had sight sharp enough to distinguish the particles of blood, lymph, etc., and reason to observe how each particle, on meeting another, either rebounds or communicates part of its motion, etc. It would live in this blood as we live in this part of the universe, and would consider each particle of blood as a whole, not as a part, and could not know how all the parts are regulated by the universal nature of the blood and compelled to adapt themselves to one another, as the universal nature of the blood requires, so as to agree among themselves in a certain ratio.
Nam si fingamus, nullas dari causas extra sanguinem, quae novos motus sanguini communicarent, nec ullum dari spatium extra sanguinem, nec alia corpora, in quae particulae sanguinis suum motum transferre possent, certum est, sanguinem in suo statu semper mansurum, et eius particulas nullas alias variationes passuras, quam eas, quae possunt concipi ex data ratione motus sanguinis ad lympham, chylum, etc. et sic sanguis semper, ut totum, non vero ut pars, considerari deberet.
For if we feign that there are no causes outside the blood which would communicate new motions to the blood, nor any space outside the blood, nor other bodies to which the particles of blood could transfer their motion, it is certain that the blood would always remain in its state and its particles would undergo no variations other than those which can be conceived from the given ratio of the motion of the blood to the lymph, chyle, etc.; and so the blood would always have to be considered as a whole, not as a part.
Verum quia plurimae aliae causae dantur, quae leges naturae sanguinis certo modo moderantur, et vicissim illae a sanguine, hinc fit, ut alii motus, aliaeque variationes in sanguine oriantur, quae consequuntur non a sola ratione motus eius partium ad invicem, sed a ratione motus, sanguinis, et causarum externarum simul ad invicem: hoc modo sanguis rationem partis, non vero totius habet. De toto, et parte modo dixi.
But since there are very many other causes which regulate the laws of the nature of the blood in a certain way, and are in turn regulated by the blood, it comes about that other motions and other variations arise in the blood, which follow not only from the ratio of the motion of its parts to one another, but from the ratio of the motion of the blood and of the external causes together to one another; in this way the blood has the character of a part, not of a whole. I have just spoken of whole and part.
[G IV 172] Iam cum omnia naturae corpora eodem modo possint, et debeant concipi, ac nos hic sanguinem concepimus: omnia enim corpora ab aliis circumcinguntur, et ab invicem determinantur ad existendum, et operandum certa, ac determinata ratione, servata semper in omnibus simul, hoc est, in toto universo eadem ratione motus ad quietem; hinc sequitur omne corpus, quatenus certo modo modificatum existit, ut partem totius universi, considerari debere, cum suo toto convenire, et cum reliquis cohaerere; et quoniam natura universi non est, ut natura sanguinis, limitata; sed absolute infinita, ideo ab hac infinitae potentiae natura eius partes infinitis modis moderantur, et infinitas variationes pati coguntur.
Now since all the bodies of nature can and must be conceived in the same way as we have here conceived the blood (for all bodies are surrounded by others, and are determined by one another to exist and to operate in a certain and determinate manner, the same ratio of motion to rest being always preserved in all of them together, that is, in the whole universe),1 it follows that every body, insofar as it exists modified in a certain way, must be considered as a part of the whole universe, and that it agrees with its whole and coheres with the rest. And since the nature of the universe is not limited, as the nature of the blood is, but absolutely infinite, its parts are regulated in infinite ways by this nature of infinite power, and are compelled to undergo infinite variations.
  1. 1 The physics of Part II says the same: a composite individual keeps its nature while its parts change, provided they keep the same ratio of motion and rest, and so the whole of nature is one individual whose parts vary in infinite ways without any change of the whole (Part II, Lemma 7, Schol.; the definition of an individual is given after Lemma 3).
Verum ratione substantiae unamquamque partem arctiorem unionem cum suo toto habere concipio. Nam ut antehac in prima mea epistola, quam Rhenoburgi adhuc habitans tibi scripsi, conatus sum demonstrare, cum de natura substantiae sit esse infinitam, sequi ad naturam substantiae corporeae unamquamque partem pertinere, nec sine ea esse, aut concipi posse.
But with respect to substance I conceive that each part has a closer union with its whole. For, as I tried to demonstrate earlier in my first letter,2 which I wrote to you while I was still living at Rijnsburg, since it is of the nature of substance to be infinite, it follows that each part pertains to the nature of corporeal substance and can neither be nor be conceived without it.
  1. 2 Letter 2, in which Spinoza undertook to show, among other things, that every substance must be infinite, or supremely perfect in its kind; in the Ethics, Part I, Prop. 8, with the indivisibility of corporeal substance in Props. 12–13 and the scholium to Prop. 15.
[G IV 173] Vides igitur, qua ratione, et rationem, cur sentiam corpus humanum partem esse naturae: quod autem ad mentem humanam attinet, eam etiam partem naturae esse censeo; nempe quia statuo, dari etiam in natura potentiam infinitam cogitandi, quae, quatenus infinita, in se continet totam naturam obiective, et cuius cogitationes procedunt eodem modo, ac natura, eius nimirum ideatum.
You see, therefore, in what way, and for what reason, I think that the human body is a part of nature. As for the human mind, I think that it too is a part of nature; namely, because I maintain that there is also in nature an infinite power of thinking which, insofar as it is infinite, contains in itself the whole of nature objectively, and whose thoughts proceed in the same way as nature, namely its object.1
  1. 1 ideatum: the object of the idea. Compare Part II, Prop. 7 with its corollary: whatever follows formally from God’s infinite nature follows objectively in God from his idea in the same order and connection.
[G IV 174] Deinde mentem humanam hanc eandem potentiam statuo, non quatenus infinitam, et totam naturam percipientem; sed finitam, nempe quatenus tantum humanum corpus percipit, et hac ratione mentem humanam partem cuiusdam infiniti intellectus statuo.
Next, I maintain that the human mind is this same power, not insofar as it is infinite and perceives the whole of nature, but insofar as it is finite, namely insofar as it perceives only the human body; and in this way I maintain that the human mind is a part of a certain infinite intellect.1
  1. 1 Part II, Prop. 11, Cor.: ‘the human mind is a part of the infinite intellect of God’.
Verum haec omnia, et quae huic rei annexa sunt, hic accurate explicare, et demonstrare, res esset nimis prolixa, nec puto te id impraesentiarum a me exspectare. Imo dubito, an mentem tuam satis perceperim, atque aliud responderim, ac rogaveris, quod ex te scire desidero.
But to explain and demonstrate all these things, and what is connected with them, accurately here would be too long a business, and I do not think you expect it of me at present. Indeed I doubt whether I have grasped your meaning well enough, and whether I have not answered something other than what you asked; which I should like to know from you.
Quod deinde scribis, me innuisse Cartesii regulas motus falsas fere omnes esse, si recte memini, D. Hugenium id sentire dixi, nec ullam aliam falsam esse affirmavi, quam regulam sextam Cartesii, circa quam D. Hugenium etiam errare me putare dixi; qua occasione petii, ut mihi communicares experimentum, quod secundum eam hypothesin experti estis in vestra Regia societate; sed tibi id non licere iudico, quia de hoc nihil respondes.
As for your writing next that I hinted that almost all of Descartes’s rules of motion are false: if I remember rightly, I said that Mr Huygens thinks so, and I affirmed no other to be false than Descartes’s sixth rule,1 about which I said that I thought Mr Huygens too was in error; on which occasion I asked you to communicate to me the experiment which you made in your Royal Society according to that hypothesis; but I judge that you are not permitted to do so, since you reply nothing about it.
  1. 1 The rules of collision in Descartes’s Principles of Philosophy, II.46–52; the sixth (II.51) concerns a body striking another at rest that is exactly equal to it (AT VIII-1 69).
Dictus Hugenius totus occupatus fuit, et adhuc est in expoliendis vitris dioptricis; in quem finem fabricam adornavit, in qua et patinas tornare potest, satis quidem nitidam: quid autem ea promoverit, adhuc nescio, nec, ut verum fateor, valde scire desidero. Nam me experientia satis docuit in patinis sphaericis libera manu tutius, et melius expoliri, quam quavis machina. De pendulorum successu, et tempore transmigrationis in Galliam nondum aliquid certi possum scribere. etc.
The said Huygens has been, and still is, wholly occupied in polishing dioptrical glasses; for this purpose he has fitted up a workshop, quite a neat one indeed, in which he can also turn dishes;1 but what he has achieved with it I do not yet know, nor, to tell the truth, do I much wish to know. For experience has taught me well enough that with spherical dishes it is safer and better to polish by free hand than by any machine. Of the success of the pendulums and the time of his moving to France I can write nothing certain yet, etc.
  1. 1 patinas: the curved tools, ground to the required sphere, on which lenses are polished; Spinoza himself ground lenses.

Epistola L

Letter 50

Spinoza to Jarig Jelles

Viro Humanissimo, atque Prudentissimo,
To the most courteous and most prudent man
Jarigh Jelles
Jarig Jelles
Humanissime vir,
Most courteous Sir,
[G IV 238] Quantum ad Politicam spectat,a discrimen inter me, et Hobbesium, de quo interrogas, in hoc consistit, quod ego naturale ius semper sartum tectum conservo, quodque Supremo Magistratui in qualibet Urbe non plus in subditos iuris, quam iuxta mensuram potestatis, qua subditum superat, competere statuo, quod in statu naturali semper locum habet.
With regard to politics, the difference between me and Hobbes, which you ask about, consists in this: that I always keep natural right intact, and that I hold that the supreme magistrate in any city has no more right over its subjects than in proportion to the power by which it exceeds the subject;1 which is always the case in the natural state.
  1. a The text is the Latin version printed in the 1677 edition (OP pp. 557–558, Letter 50), headed Versio, which gives the addressee’s name as asterisks; Van Vloten and Land supply it (VVL³ III 172), and took the Latin to be Spinoza’s own translation, except, it seems, the last sentences. The Dutch text, which Gebhardt prints after the Latin (G IV 239–241), is dated ‘’s Gravenhage, 2 May 1671’; the Latin, The Hague, 2 June 1674. The book by Mansvelt mentioned at the end appeared in 1674.
  1. 1 The principle of the Theological-Political Treatise, ch. 16 (G III 189): ‘the right of each extends as far as its determinate power extends’. On the natural and the civil state in the Ethics, see Part IV, Prop. 37, Schol. 2.
[G IV 239] Porro, quod demonstrationem attinet, quam ego in appendice Geometricarum in Cartesii principia demonstrationum stabilio, nempe Deum non, nisi valde improprie, unum, vel unicum dici posse; respondeo, rem solummodo existentiae, non vero essentiae respectu unam, vel unicam dici: res enim sub numeris, nisi postquam ad commune genus redactae fuerunt, non concipimus.
Further, as regards the demonstration which I establish in the appendix to the Geometrical Demonstrations of Descartes’s Principles, namely that God cannot be called one or unique except very improperly:1 I reply that a thing is called one or unique only in respect of its existence, not of its essence; for we do not conceive things under numbers until they have been brought under a common genus.
  1. 1 The appendix is the Metaphysical Thoughts, Part I, ch. 6 (G I 246), which says only that God may be called one insofar as we separate him from other beings, and unique insofar as we conceive that there cannot be more than one of the same nature, and adds: ‘if we wished to examine the matter more accurately, we could perhaps show that God is called one and unique only improperly; but the matter is not of such importance, indeed of none at all, to those who are concerned with things, not with names’. Compare Part I, Prop. 14, Cor. 1, ‘God is unique’, where the word is explained as meaning that there is only one substance.
Qui, verbi gratia, sestertium, et imperialem manu tenet, de numero binario non cogitabit, nisi hunc sestertium, et imperialem uno, eodemque, nempe nummorum, vel monetarum, nomine vocare queat: nam tunc, se duos nummos, vel monetas habere, potest affirmare; quoniam non modo sestertium; sed etiam imperialem nummi, vel monetae nomine insignit.
Whoever, for example, holds a sesterce and an imperial2 in his hand will not think of the number two unless he can call this sesterce and this imperial by one and the same name, namely that of coins or pieces of money: for then he can affirm that he has two coins or two pieces of money, since he designates not only the sesterce but also the imperial by the name of coin or piece of money.
  1. 2 Roman names for the Dutch coins of the original: ‘een stuiver en een rijksdaalder’, a stiver and a rix-dollar (G IV 239; VVL³ III 173).
Hinc ergo clare patet, nullam rem unam, aut unicam nominari, nisi postquam alia res concepta fuit, quae (ut dictum est) cum ea convenit.
From this, then, it is clear that no thing is called one or unique until another thing has been conceived which (as has been said) agrees with it.
Quoniam vero Dei existentia ipsius sit essentia, deque eius essentia universalem non possimus formare ideam, certum est, eum, qui Deum unum, vel unicum nuncupat, nullam de Deo veram habere ideam, vel improprie de eo loqui.
But since God’s existence is his essence itself,3 and we cannot form a universal idea of his essence, it is certain that whoever calls God one or unique has no true idea of God, or speaks improperly of him.
  1. 3 Part I, Prop. 20: ‘God’s existence and his essence are one and the same’.
[G IV 240] Quantum ad hoc, quod figura negatio, non vero aliquid positivum est; manifestum est, integram materiam, indefinite consideratam, nullam posse habere figuram; figuramque in finitis, ac determinatis corporibus locum tantum obtinere. Qui enim se figuram percipere ait, nil aliud eo indicat, quam se rem determinatam, et quo pacto ea sit determinata, concipere.
As to this, that figure is a negation and not something positive: it is evident that matter as a whole, considered indefinitely, can have no figure, and that figure has a place only in finite and determinate bodies. For whoever says that he perceives a figure indicates by this nothing other than that he conceives a determinate thing, and in what way it is determined.
Haec ergo determinatio ad rem iuxta suum esse non pertinet: sed econtra est eius non esse. Quia ergo figura non aliud, quam determinatio, et determinatio negatio est; non poterit, ut dictum, aliud quid, quam negatio, esse.
This determination, therefore, does not pertain to the thing according to its being, but on the contrary is its non-being. Since, then, figure is nothing other than determination, and determination is negation,1 it cannot, as has been said, be anything other than negation.
  1. 1 Compare Part I, Prop. 8, Schol. 1: ‘to be finite is in truth in part a negation, and to be infinite the absolute affirmation of the existence of some nature’.
Librum, quem Ultraiectinus professor in meum scripsit, quique post obitum eius luci expositus est, e fenestra Bibliopolae pendentem vidi: et ex paucis, quae tum temporis in eo legeram, eum lectu, multo minus responsione indignum iudicabam. Relinquebam ergo librum, eiusque authorem.
The book which the Utrecht professor wrote against mine,2 and which was published after his death, I saw hanging in a bookseller’s window; and from the little I read in it at that time I judged it unworthy of being read, much less of being answered. So I left the book, and its author.
  1. 2 Regnier van Mansvelt (1639–1671), professor of philosophy at Utrecht and a Cartesian: Adversus anonymum theologico-politicum liber singularis (Amsterdam, 1674), against the Theological-Political Treatise (VVL³ III 173; Freudenthal, Die Lebensgeschichte Spinoza’s, 1899, p. 279; Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 342).
Mente, subridens volvebam ignarissimos quosque passim audacissimos, et ad scribendum paratissimos esse. Mihi **** merces suas eodem modo, ac propolae, venum exponere videntur, qui semper, quod vilius est, primo loco ostendunt: Ajunt, Diabolum esse vaferrimum; mihi vero, eorum genius longe hunc vafritie superare videtur. Vale.
Smiling, I turned it over in my mind that the most ignorant are everywhere the boldest and the readiest to write. The ****3 seem to me to set out their wares for sale in the same way as hucksters, who always show what is cheapest first: they say the Devil is most cunning; but to me their genius seems far to surpass him in cunning. Farewell.
  1. 3 The word is suppressed in the Latin and the Dutch alike (OP p. 558; G IV 241).
[G IV 241] Hagae Comitis 2. Iunii 1674.
The Hague, 2 June 1674.

Epistola LVII

Letter 57

Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus to Spinoza

Praestantissimo, Acutissimoque philosopho,
To the most excellent and most acute philosopher
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus.
from Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Praestantissime vir,
Most excellent Sir,
[G IV 262] Miror saltem,a quod eo ipso, quo demonstrent philosophi, aliquid falsum esse, eadem ratione ostendant eius veritatem: certitudinem enim intellectus in suae methodi initio Cartesius omnibus aequalem esse putat; in meditationibus autem demonstrat. Idem illi probant, qui putant se aliquid certi demonstrare posse hac ratione, ut pro indubitato a singulis hominibus accipiatur.
I am surprised, at least,1 that philosophers show the truth of something by the very same reasoning by which they demonstrate that it is false. For Descartes, at the beginning of his Method, holds that the certainty of the intellect is equal in all; in the Meditations, however, he demonstrates it. Those prove the same who think they can demonstrate something certain in such a way that it is accepted by every single man as beyond doubt.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 580–582, Letter 61), as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 262–264).
  1. 1 The letter answers a letter of Spinoza’s on free will that has not survived, and was sent through G. H. Schuller, to whom Spinoza replied (Letter 58). The 1677 edition prints it without the writer’s name, which Gebhardt supplies; the Dutch version signs it ‘N.N.’.
Sed his missis, experientiam provoco, et, ut ad haec accurate attendas, submisse rogo, sic enim deprehendetur, si ex duobus unus aliquid affirmet, alter vero neget, et ita, ut sibi eius sint conscii, loquantur, quod licet verbis contrarii videantur; attamen perpensis eorum conceptibus, ambo (unusquisque pro suo conceptu) vera dicant.
But leaving these things aside, I appeal to experience, and humbly ask you to attend to this carefully. For so it will be found that if, of two people, one affirms something and the other denies it, and they speak in such a way that they are conscious of what they say, then although they seem to contradict each other in words, yet when their concepts are weighed, both speak the truth (each according to his own concept).
Quod quidem refero, cum immensae sit in vita communi utilitatis, et possent hoc unico observato innumerae controversiae, et inde sequentes contentiones praecaveri; quamvis veritas haec in conceptu non semper absolute vera sit, sed positis tantum iis, quae in intellectu supponuntur, ut vera. Quae regula etiam adeo est universalis, ut apud omnes homines, ne dementibus quidem, ac dormientibus exceptis, reperiatur: quicquid enim hi dicunt, se videre (licet nobis non ita appareat) aut vidisse, certissimum est, haec revera ita se habere.
I mention this because it is of immense use in common life, and by observing this one thing countless controversies, and the quarrels that follow from them, could be prevented; although this truth in the concept is not always absolutely true, but true only given what is supposed in the intellect as true. This rule is also so universal that it is found among all men, not even the mad and the sleeping excepted; for whatever they say they see (though it does not appear so to us), or have seen, it is most certain that these things really are so.
Quod etiam in casu proposito, de libero nempe arbitrio, clarissime conspicitur. Uterque enim tam qui pro, quam qui contra disputat, verum mihi dicere videtur, prout nimirum quilibet libertatem concipit; liberum enim dicit Cartesius, quod a nulla causa cogitur. Et tu econtra, quod a nulla causa determinatur ad aliquid.
This is also seen most clearly in the case proposed, namely that of free will. For both he who argues for it and he who argues against it seem to me to speak the truth, according, namely, to how each conceives freedom. For Descartes calls free that which is compelled by no cause; and you, on the contrary, that which is determined to something by no cause.1
  1. 1 Spinoza rejects this account of his definition at the start of his reply (Letter 58); his own is Part I, Def. 7.
Fateor itaque tecum, nos in omnibus rebus a certa causa ad aliquid determinari, et sic nullum nos habere liberum arbitrium: sed contra quoque puto cum Cartesio in certis rebus (quod statim aperiam) nos nullatenus cogi, atque ita habere liberum arbitrium. Exemplum formabo a praesenti.
I agree with you, therefore, that in all things we are determined to something by a certain cause, and so have no free will; but on the other hand I also think, with Descartes, that in certain things (as I shall show at once) we are in no way compelled, and so have free will. I shall take an example from the present case.
[G IV 263] Triplex autem est status quaestionis: primo, an in res, quae extra nos sunt, absolute aliquam habeamus potestatem? Quod negatur. Exempli gratia, quod hanc nunc exaro epistolam non est absolute in mea potestate, quandoquidem certe prius scripsissem, ni vel absentia, vel amicorum praesentia impeditus fuissem.
The question, then, has three parts. First, whether we have any absolute power over the things which are outside us? This is denied. For example, that I am now writing this letter is not absolutely in my power, since I would certainly have written earlier if I had not been prevented either by absence or by the presence of friends.
Secundo, an nos in motus corporis nostri, qui sequuntur, voluntate eos ad id determinante, absolute habeamus potestatem? Respondeo limitando, si nimirum sano corpore vivamus. Si enim valeo, semper me ad scribendum applicare, vel non applicare possum.
Second, whether we have absolute power over the motions of our body which follow when the will determines them to it? I answer with a limitation: namely, if we live with a healthy body. For if I am well, I can always apply myself to writing or not apply myself.
Tertio, an, quando mihi meum rationis exercitium usurpare licet, eo liberrime, hoc est, absolute uti possim? Ad quod respondeo affirmative. Quis enim mihi negaret, nisi contradicendo propriae conscientiae, quod non possum in meis cogitationibus cogitare, me velle scribere, aut non scribere.
Third, whether, when I am permitted the use of my reason, I can use it most freely, that is, absolutely? To this I answer affirmatively. For who would deny me, unless by contradicting his own consciousness, that I can think in my thoughts that I want to write, or not to write?
Et quoad operationem quoque, quia hoc externae causae permittunt, (quod secundum concernit casum) quod equidem tam scribendi, quam non scribendi facultatem habeam: fateor quidem tecum, dari causas, quae me ad id determinant, quod iam scribo, quia scilicet primo mihi scripsisti, eademque opera postulavisti, ut prima occasione rescriberem, et, quia impraesentiarum datur occasio, eam non libenter amitterem. Pro certo etiam affirmo, teste conscientia, cum Cartesio, istiusmodi res me propterea non cogere, meque revera (quod negatu videtur impossibile) id nihilominus omittere posse, non obstantibus his rationibus.
And as regards the act too, since external causes permit this (which concerns the second case), namely that I do indeed have the faculty both of writing and of not writing: I admit indeed with you that there are causes which determine me to this, that I am now writing, namely that you wrote to me first, and at the same time asked me to reply at the first opportunity, and since the opportunity now offers, I would not willingly lose it. I also affirm for certain, with Descartes, on the testimony of consciousness, that things of this kind do not on that account compel me, and that I really could (which seems impossible to deny) nevertheless refrain from writing, notwithstanding these reasons.
Si quoque cogeremur a rebus externis, cui possibile est habitum virtutis acquirere? imo hoc posito omnis malitia excusabilis esset. Sed quot modis non fit, ut, si a rebus externis ad aliquid determinemur, obfirmato tamen, ac constanti ei resistamus animo?
Again, if we were compelled by external things, who could acquire the habit of virtue? Indeed, on this supposition all wickedness would be excusable. But in how many ways does it not happen that, when we are determined to something by external things, we nevertheless resist it with a firm and constant mind?
[G IV 264] Ut itaque superioris regulae clariorem dem explicationem. Ambo equidem verum dicitis proprium iuxta conceptum: si autem absolutam veritatem spectemus, haec tantum competit sententiae Cartesii.
So, to give a clearer explanation of the rule stated above. You both speak the truth according to your own concepts; but if we look to absolute truth, it belongs only to Descartes’s opinion.
Supponis enim in conceptu, ut certum, libertatis essentiam in eo consistere, quod a nulla re determinamur. Hoc sic posito, utrumque verum erit; veruntamen cum essentia cuiuslibet rei in eo consistat, sine quo ne quidem concipi possit, et clare sane concipiatur libertas, licet ab externis causis in nostris actionibus ad aliquid determinemur; sive licet semper causae sint, quae incitamento nobis sunt, ut actiones nostras tali modo dirigamus, cum tamen id plane non efficiant; verum nullatenus, posito quod cogamur.a
For you suppose in your concept, as certain, that the essence of freedom consists in our being determined by nothing. On this supposition both will be true; but since the essence of any thing consists in that without which it cannot even be conceived, and freedom is certainly conceived clearly even though in our actions we are determined to something by external causes, or even though there are always causes which serve as incitements to us to direct our actions in such a way, without, however, bringing this about at all; but by no means, on the supposition that we are compelled.
  1. a The sentence lacks the main clause that should answer cum essentia … consistat, as Van Vloten and Land observe (VVL³ III 194).
Vide praeterea Cartesii I. Tom. Epist. 8. et 9. item II. Tom. pag. 4. Sed haec sufficiant. Rogo, ut ad has difficultates respondeas,b
See also Descartes, vol. I, Letters 8 and 9, and vol. II, p. 4. But let this suffice. I ask you to reply to these difficulties,
  1. b The Latin breaks off here; the Dutch version ends the sentence with a promise of gratitude and the signature ‘N.N.’ (G IV 264).
8. Octob. 1674.
8 October 1674.

Epistola LVIII

Letter 58

Spinoza to Georg Hermann Schuller

Viro Doctissimo, atque Expertissimo
To the most learned and expert man
G. H. Schuller
G. H. Schuller
Expertissime Domine,
Most expert Sir,
[G IV 265] Misit mihi amicus noster I. R.a literas, quas ad me dignatus es scribere, una cum iudicio tui amici de mea, et Cartesii de libero arbitrio sententia, quae mihi gratissima fuerunt. Et, quamvis in praesentia, praeterquam quod valetudinem non satis firmam habeam, aliis rebus admodum distrahar, tamen vel singularis tua humanitas, vel quod ego praecipuum puto, veritatis studium, quo teneris, me cogit, ut tuo desiderio pro mei ingenii tenuitate, satisfaciam.
Our friend J. R.1 has sent me the letter which you were good enough to write to me, together with your friend’s judgment2 of my opinion and of Descartes’s on free will, which were most welcome to me. And although at present, besides not having very firm health, I am much distracted by other matters, yet either your singular kindness or, what I consider the chief thing, the zeal for truth by which you are possessed, compels me to satisfy your wish, as far as my slender talents allow.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP, Letter 62), as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 265–268). It bears no date; it answers Letter 57, of 8 October 1674.
  1. 1 The Amsterdam bookseller Jan Rieuwertsz (VVL³ III 195), who published Spinoza’s Principles of Descartes’s Philosophy and the Theological-Political Treatise (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 124).
  2. 2 Tschirnhaus’s letter (Letter 57), which Schuller had forwarded.
Etenim quid tuus amicus velit, antequam experientiam provocat, et accuratam attentionem petit, nescio. Quod deinde addit, si quando inter duos alter de re quapiam quid affirmat, alter autem neget etc. Verum est, si intelligit illos duos, quamvis iisdem utantur vocabulis, de rebus tamen diversis cogitare, cuius rei exempla aliquot amico I. R. olim misi, cui iam scribo, ut tibi eadem communicet.
For what your friend means before he appeals to experience and asks for careful attention, I do not know. As for what he adds next, if ever, between two people, one affirms something about some matter and the other denies it, etc.: it is true if he means that the two, although they use the same words, are nevertheless thinking of different things; of which I once sent some examples to our friend J. R., to whom I am now writing that he should communicate them to you.
Transeo igitur ad illam libertatis definitionem, quam meam esse ait; sed nescio, unde illam sumpserit. Ego eam rem liberam esse dico, quae ex sola suae naturae necessitate existit, et agit; coactam autem, quae ab alio determinatur, ad existendum, et operandum certa, ac determinata ratione.
I pass, therefore, to that definition of freedom which he says is mine; but I do not know where he took it from. I say that that thing is free which exists and acts from the necessity of its nature alone; and compelled, that which is determined by another to exist and to operate in a certain and determinate manner.3
  1. 3 Part I, Def. 7, nearly word for word: ‘That thing is called free which exists from the necessity of its nature alone, and is determined to act by itself alone. But that thing is called necessary, or rather compelled, which is determined by another to exist and to operate in a certain and determinate manner.’
Ex. gr. Deus, tametsi necessario, libere tamen existit, quia ex sola suae naturae necessitate existit. Sic etiam Deus se, et absolute omnia libere intelligit, quia ex sola ipsius naturae necessitate sequitur, ut omnia intelligat. Vides igitur me libertatem non in libero decreto; sed in libera necessitate ponere.
For example, God, though necessarily, nevertheless exists freely, because he exists from the necessity of his nature alone. So too God understands himself and absolutely all things freely, because it follows from the necessity of his nature alone that he understands all things. You see, therefore, that I place freedom not in a free decree, but in a free necessity.4
  1. 4 Compare Part I, Prop. 17, Cor. 2, that God alone is a free cause, and Prop. 32, that the will cannot be called a free cause.
Sed ad res creatas descendamus, quae omnes a causis externis determinantur ad existendum, et operandum certa, ac determinata ratione. Quod ut clare intelligatur, rem simplicissimam concipiamus. Ex. gr. Lapis a causa externa, ipsum impellente, certam motus quantitatem accipit, qua postea, cessante causae externae impulsu, moveri necessario perget. Haec igitur lapidis in motu permanentia coacta est, non quia necessaria; sed quia impulsu causae externae definiri debet; et quod hic de lapide, id de quacunque re singulari, quantumvis illa composita, et ad plurima apta esse concipiatur, intelligendum est, quod scilicet unaquaeque res necessario a causa externa aliqua determinatur ad existendum, et operandum certa, ac determinata ratione.
But let us descend to created things, which are all determined by external causes to exist and to operate in a certain and determinate manner.6 To understand this clearly, let us conceive a very simple thing. For example, a stone receives from an external cause which impels it a certain quantity of motion, by which it will afterwards necessarily continue to move when the impulse of the external cause has ceased.5 This continuance of the stone in motion is therefore compelled, not because it is necessary, but because it must be defined by the impulse of the external cause; and what is to be understood here of the stone must be understood of any singular thing, however composite and capable of many things it is conceived to be: namely, that each thing is necessarily determined by some external cause to exist and to operate in a certain and determinate manner.
  1. 5 Compare Part II, Lemma 3, Cor.: a body in motion moves until it is determined to rest by another body.
  2. 6 Part I, Prop. 28.
[G IV 266] Porro, concipe iam, si placet, lapidem, dum moveri pergit, cogitare, et scire, se, quantum potest, conari, ut moveri pergat. Hic sane lapis, quandoquidem sui tantummodo conatus est conscius, et minime indifferens, se liberrimum esse, et nulla alia de causa in motu perseverare credet, quam quia vult. Atque haec humana illa libertas est, quam omnes habere iactant, et quae in hoc solo consistit, quod homines sui appetitus sint conscii, et causarum, a quibus determinantur, ignari.
Now conceive, if you please, that the stone, while it continues to move, thinks, and knows that it is striving, as far as it can, to continue to move. This stone, surely, since it is conscious only of its striving, and is by no means indifferent, will believe that it is most free and that it perseveres in motion for no other cause than because it wills to. And this is that human freedom which all boast of having, and which consists only in this, that men are conscious of their appetite and ignorant of the causes by which they are determined.1
  1. 1 As in the Appendix to Part I (Part I, Appendix), Part II, Prop. 35, Schol. and Part III, Prop. 2, Schol.; the stone’s striving answers to the conatus of Part III, Prop. 6.
Sic infans se lac libere appetere credit; puer autem iratus vindictam velle, et timidus fugam. Ebrius deinde credit, se ex libero mentis decreto ea loqui, quae postea sobrius tacuisse vellet. Sic delirans, garrulus, et huius farinae plurimi se ex libero mentis decreto agere, non autem impetu ferri credunt.
So the infant believes that it freely wants milk; the angry boy that he wants vengeance, and the timid one flight. The drunk, again, believes that he says from a free decree of the mind what afterwards, when sober, he would wish he had kept silent about. So the delirious man, the chatterer, and very many of this stamp believe that they act from a free decree of the mind, and are not carried away by impulse.2
  1. 2 Almost word for word in Part III, Prop. 2, Schol..
Et quia hoc praeiudicium omnibus hominibus innatum est, non ita facile eodem liberantur. Nam quamvis experientia satis superque doceat, homines nihil minus posse, quam appetitus moderari suos, et quod saepe, dum contrariis affectibus conflictantur, meliora videant, et deteriora sequantur, se tamen liberos esse credunt, idque propterea, quod res quasdam leviter appetant, et quarum appetitus facile potest contrahi memoria alterius rei, cuius frequenter recordamur.
And since this prejudice is innate in all men, they are not so easily freed from it. For although experience teaches more than enough that men can do nothing less than moderate their appetites, and that often, when they are torn by contrary affects, they see the better and follow the worse, they nevertheless believe themselves free; and that because they want some things lightly, and their appetite for these can easily be reduced by the memory of another thing which we frequently recollect.
His, quaenam mea de libera, et coacta necessitate, deque ficta humana libertate sit sententia, satis, ni fallor, explicui; ex quibus facile ad tui amici obiectiones respondetur. Nam, quod cum Cartesio ait, illum liberum esse, qui a nulla causa externa cogitur, si per hominem coactum intelligit eum, qui invitus agit, concedo nos quibusdam in rebus nullatenus cogi, hocque respectu habere liberum arbitrium; sed si per coactum intelligit, qui quamvis non invitus, necessario tamen agit, (ut supra explicui) nego nos aliqua in re liberos esse.
