With these things I have, unless I am mistaken, explained sufficiently what my opinion is of free and compelled necessity, and of feigned human freedom; from which your friend’s objections are easily answered. For when he says, with Descartes, that he is free who is compelled by no external cause: if by a man compelled he understands one who acts against his will, I grant that in certain things we are in no way compelled, and in this respect have free will; but if by compelled he understands one who, though not against his will, nevertheless acts necessarily (as I explained above), I deny that we are free in anything.
[G IV 267] At amicus tuus contra affirmat; nos rationis exercitio liberrime, hoc est, absolute uti posse, qua in re satis, ne dicam nimis confidenter perstat. Quis enim, ait, nisi propriae contradicendo conscientiae, negaret, me cogitationibus meis cogitare posse, quod vellem, et quod non vellem scribere.
But your friend, on the contrary, affirms that we can use the exercise of reason most freely, that is, absolutely; in which he persists confidently enough, not to say too confidently. For who, he says, would deny, unless by contradicting his own consciousness, that I can think in my thoughts that I want to write, and that I do not want to write?
Pervelim scire, quam ille conscientiam, praeter illam, quam supra exemplo lapidis explicui, narrat: Ego sane, ne meae conscientiae, hoc est, ne rationi, et experientiae contradicam, et ne praeiudicia, et ignorantiam foveam, nego, me ulla absoluta cogitandi potentia cogitare posse, quod vellem, et quod non vellem scribere.
I should very much like to know what consciousness he speaks of, other than the one I explained above with the example of the stone. For my part, so as not to contradict my consciousness, that is, reason and experience, and so as not to foster prejudices and ignorance, I deny that I can, by any absolute power of thinking, think that I want to write and that I do not want to write.
Sed ipsius conscientiam appello, qui sine dubio expertus est, se in somnis non habere potestatem cogitandi, quod vellet et quod non vellet scribere; nec cum somniat se velle scribere, potestatem habet, non somniandi se velle scribere; nec minus expertum illum esse credo, quod mens non semper aeque apta sit ad cogitandum de eodem obiecto; sed prout corpus aptius est, ut in eo huius, vel illius obiecti imago excitetur, ita mens aptior est ad hoc, vel illud obiectum contemplandum.
But I appeal to his own consciousness. He has no doubt experienced that in sleep he does not have the power of thinking that he wants to write and that he does not want to; nor, when he dreams that he wants to write, does he have the power not to dream that he wants to write. Nor do I believe that he has any less experienced that the mind is not always equally capable of thinking of the same object, but that, as the body is more capable of having the image of this or that object aroused in it, so the mind is more capable of contemplating this or that object.1
  1. 1 Compare Part II, Prop. 14 and, on dreams, Part III, Prop. 2, Schol..
Cum praeterea addit, quod causae, cur animum ad scribendum applicuerit, ipsum quidem ad scribendum impulerint; sed non coegerint, nihil aliud significat, (si rem aequo pondere examinare velis) quam quod ipsius animus ita tum erat constitutus, ut causae, quae ipsum alias, cum scilicet magno aliquo affectu conflictatur, non potuissent, nunc facile potuerunt flectere, hoc est, causae, quae ipsum alias non potuissent cogere, coegerunt iam, non ut invitus scriberet; sed ut necessario scribendi esset cupidus.
When he adds, further, that the causes for which he applied his mind to writing impelled him to write but did not compel him, he means nothing else (if you are willing to weigh the matter fairly) than that his mind was then so constituted that causes which at other times, namely when he was torn by some great affect, could not have bent him, now could easily do so; that is, causes which at other times could not have compelled him have now compelled him, not to write against his will, but to be necessarily desirous of writing.
Quod porro statuit: quod si a causis externis cogeremur, virtutis habitum acquirere possit nemo; nescio, quis ipsi dixerit, non posse ex fatali necessitate; sed tantummodo ex libero mentis decreto, fieri, ut firmato, et constanti simus animo.
As for his further statement that if we were compelled by external causes, no one could acquire the habit of virtue: I do not know who told him that it cannot happen from fatal necessity, but only from a free decree of the mind, that we are of a firm and constant mind.
Et quod denique addit: quod hoc posito omnis malitia excusabilis esset. Quid inde? Nam homines mali non minus timendi sunt, nec minus perniciosi, quando necessario mali sunt.
And what he finally adds: that on this supposition all wickedness would be excusable. What then? For wicked men are no less to be feared, and no less pernicious, when they are necessarily wicked.
Sed de his si placet, vide meae appendicis ad Cartesii principiorum lib. I. et II. ordine geometrico demonstrator. Partis II. Caput VIII.
But on these matters see, if you please, Part II, Chapter 8, of my Appendix1 to the first and second books of Descartes’s Principles demonstrated in geometrical order.
  1. 1 The Metaphysical Thoughts (Cogitata Metaphysica) appended to Spinoza’s Principles of Descartes’s Philosophy (1663); Part II, chapter 8, is ‘On the will of God’ (G I 264).
[G IV 268] Denique tuus amicus, qui haec mihi obiicit, vellem, ut mihi responderet, qua ratione ille humanam virtutem, quae ex libero mentis decreto oritur, simul concipiat cum Dei praeordinatione. Quod si cum Cartesio fatetur, se haec nescire conciliare, ergo telum, quo ipse transfixus iam est, in me vibrare conatur. Sed frustra. Nam si meam sententiam attento animo examinare velis, omnia congruere videbis, etc.
Finally, I should like your friend, who makes these objections to me, to answer me how he conceives human virtue, which arises from a free decree of the mind, together with God’s preordination. If he admits, with Descartes, that he does not know how to reconcile these,1 then he is trying to hurl at me the weapon by which he himself has already been pierced. But in vain. For if you are willing to examine my opinion attentively, you will see that everything agrees, etc.
  1. 1 Descartes, Principles of Philosophy I.41: the freedom of our will and God’s preordination are both certain, though we cannot comprehend how they agree (AT VIII-1 20).

Epistola LXIII

Letter 63

Georg Hermann Schuller to Spinoza

Praestantissimo, acutissimoque philosopho,
To the most excellent and most acute philosopher
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
G. H. Schuller.
from G. H. Schuller
Praestantissime vir,
Most excellent Sir,
[G IV 274] Serio abs te peto,a ut dubitationes hic motas solvere, tuamque ad eas responsionem mittere placeat. Sit, rogo, demonstratio ea ostensiva; non vero ad impossibile deducens, num scilicet primo nos plura de Deo attributa, quam cogitationem, extensionemque possimus cognoscere? Porro num inde sequatur creaturas, aliis constantes attributis, nullam posse concipere extensionem? Unde sequeretur tot constituendos esse mundos, quot dantur Dei attributa.
I earnestly ask you1 to be good enough to resolve the doubts raised here, and to send me your reply to them. Let the demonstration, I ask, be ostensive, not one leading to the impossible:2 namely, first, whether we can know more attributes of God than thought and extension? Further, whether it follows from this that creatures consisting of other attributes can conceive no extension? From which it would follow that as many worlds must be constituted as there are attributes of God.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 590–591, Letter 65), which prints the letter without the writer’s name. Gebhardt prints first the original, which survives (G IV 274–276), and then this text. In the original Schuller apologizes for his long silence; writes that their friend Tschirnhaus, still in England, sends his respects and has asked him to put these questions; and adds that Tschirnhaus has removed the strange opinion of Spinoza that Boyle and Oldenburg had formed, so that they now think most highly of him and greatly esteem the Theological-Political Treatise. It closes with greetings from ‘D. a. Gent’ and Jan Rieuwertsz.
  1. 1 Georg Hermann Schuller, an Amsterdam physician (Nadler, Spinoza: A Life, 2nd ed., p. 260), writes on behalf of their friend Tschirnhaus, then in England; Spinoza’s answer is Letter 64.
  2. 2 A direct proof, not a reductio ad absurdum; Spinoza remarks in his reply that he prefers the latter for negative propositions (Letter 64).
Quantae ex. gr. extensionis noster existeret mundus, tantae quoque extensionis mundi, aliis affecti attributis, essent: quemadmodum vero, praeter cogitationem, non nisi extensionem percipimus, ita etiam istius mundi creaturae, non nisi sui mundi attributa, atque cogitationem perciperent.
For example, of however great an extension our world exists, of so great an extension also would the worlds be that are affected with other attributes; and just as we perceive, besides thought, nothing but extension, so too the creatures of such a world would perceive nothing but the attributes of their world, and thought.
[G IV 275] Secundo, quoniam Dei intellectus tam essentia, quam existentia a nostro differt intellectu; nihil ergo cum nostro commune habebit; ac proinde (per 3 Ethic. Prop. part. 1.) Dei intellectus nostri causa esse nequit.
Second, since God’s intellect differs from our intellect both in essence and in existence,1 it will therefore have nothing in common with ours; and so (by Prop. 3 of Part I of the Ethics) God’s intellect cannot be the cause of ours.
  1. 1 As argued in the scholium to Part I, Prop. 17: God’s intellect is the cause of our intellect both as to essence and as to existence, and therefore differs from it in both, as the Dog, the heavenly constellation, differs from the dog that barks.
Tertio, in schol. 10. Prop. part. 1. Ethic. ais nihil in natura clarius esse, quam quod unumquodque ens sub aliquo attributo debeat concipi; (quod optime capio) et quo plus realitatis, aut esse habeat, eo plura ei competant attributa. Hinc sequi videtur, dari entia, quae tria, quatuor, aliaque plura habent attributa, licet ex demonstratis colligere licuerit, unumquodque ens duobus tantum constare attributis, nimirum certo quodam Dei attributo, eiusdemque attributi idea.
Third, in the scholium to Prop. 10 of Part I of the Ethics you say that nothing in nature is clearer than that each being must be conceived under some attribute (which I understand very well), and that the more reality, or being, it has, the more attributes belong to it. From this it seems to follow that there are beings which have three, four, or still more attributes, although from what has been demonstrated it could be gathered that each being consists of only two attributes, namely a certain attribute of God and the idea of that attribute.
Quarto, eorum, quae a Deo immediate producta sunt, quaeque mediante infinita quadam modificatione producuntur, exempla desiderarem. Prioris generis cogitatio, ac extensio; posterioris vero intellectus in cogitatione, motus in extensione esse videntur.
Fourth, I should like examples of those things which are produced immediately by God, and of those which are produced by the mediation of some infinite modification.1 Of the first kind seem to be thought and extension; of the second, intellect in thought, motion in extension.
  1. 1 The infinite modes of Part I, Props. 21–23, which the Ethics describes without giving examples; Spinoza supplies them in Letter 64.
Haec sunt, quae abs te, siquidem tempus aliquod tibi sit reliquum, peto. Vale etc.
These are the things I ask of you, if you have any time left over. Farewell, etc.
25 Iulii 1675.
25 July 1675.

Epistola LXIV

Letter 64

Spinoza to Georg Hermann Schuller

Doctissimo, Expertissimoque viro.
To the most learned and expert man
G. H. Schuller
G. H. Schuller
Expertissime vir,
Most expert Sir,
[G IV 277] Gaudeo, quod tandem occasioa tibi oblata fuerit, ut me tuis literis, mihi semper gratissimis, recreares, quod ut frequenter facias, enixe rogo, etc.
I am glad that an opportunity has at last been offered you to refresh me with a letter of yours, always most welcome to me; and I earnestly ask you to do so often, etc.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 591–593, Letter 66), as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 277–278).
Ad dubia pergo, et quidem ad primum dico, mentem humanam illa tantummodo posse cognitione assequi, quae idea corporis actu existentis involvit, vel quod ex hac ipsa idea potest concludi. Nam cuiuscunque rei potentia sola eius essentia definitur, (per prop. 7. p. 3. Ethices) mentis autem essentia (per prop. 13. p. 2.) in hoc solo consistit, quod sit idea corporis actu existentis, ac proinde mentis intelligendi potentia ad ea tantum se extendit, quae haec idea corporis in se continet, vel quae ex eadem sequuntur.
I proceed to the doubts, and to the first I say that the human mind can attain to knowledge only of those things which the idea of a body actually existing involves, or which can be inferred from this idea itself. For the power of any thing is defined by its essence alone (by Prop. 7, Part III of the Ethics), and the essence of the mind (by Prop. 13, Part II) consists only in this, that it is the idea of a body actually existing; and so the mind’s power of understanding extends only to those things which this idea of the body contains in itself, or which follow from it.
At haec corporis idea nulla alia Dei attributa involvit, neque exprimit, quam extensionem, et cogitationem. Nam eius ideatum, nempe corpus (per prop. 6. p. 2.) Deum pro causa habet, quatenus sub attributo extensionis, et non quatenus sub ullo alio consideratur, atque adeo (per axiom. 6. p. 1.) haec corporis idea Dei cognitionem involvit, quatenus tantummodo sub extensionis attributo consideratur. Deinde haec idea, quatenus cogitandi modus est, Deum etiam (per eandem prop.) pro causa habet, quatenus res est cogitans, et non quatenus sub alio attributo consideratur; adeoque (per idem axiom.) huius ideae idea Dei cognitionem involvit, quatenus sub cogitationis, et non quatenus sub alio attributo consideratur.
But this idea of the body involves and expresses no attributes of God other than extension and thought. For its object, namely the body (by Prop. 6, Part II), has God for its cause insofar as he is considered under the attribute of extension, and not insofar as he is considered under any other; and so (by Ax. 6, Part I)1 this idea of the body involves the knowledge of God only insofar as he is considered under the attribute of extension. Next, this idea, insofar as it is a mode of thinking, also has God for its cause (by the same Prop.) insofar as he is a thinking thing, and not insofar as he is considered under another attribute; and so (by the same axiom) the idea of this idea involves the knowledge of God insofar as he is considered under the attribute of thought, and not insofar as he is considered under another attribute.
  1. 1 So the Latin. Axiom 6 says that a true idea must agree with its object; the step seems rather to rest on Axiom 4, that the knowledge of an effect depends on, and involves, the knowledge of its cause.
Apparet itaque mentem humanam, sive corporis humani ideam praeter haec duo nulla alia Dei attributa involvere, neque exprimere. Caeterum ex his duobus attributis, vel eorundem affectionibus nullum aliud Dei attributum (per prop. 10. p. 1.) concludi, neque concipi potest. Atque adeo concludo mentem humanam nullum Dei attributum praeter haec posse cognitione assequi, ut fuit propositum.
It is apparent, therefore, that the human mind, or the idea of the human body, involves and expresses no attributes of God besides these two. Moreover, from these two attributes, or from their affections, no other attribute of God can be inferred or conceived (by Prop. 10, Part I). And so I conclude that the human mind can attain to knowledge of no attribute of God besides these, as was proposed.
Quod autem addis, an ergo tot mundi constituendi sunt, quot dantur attributa? vide schol. Prop. 7. p. 2. Ethices. Posset praeterea haec propositio facilius demonstrari, deducendo rem ad absurdum; quod quidem demonstrandi genus, quando propositio negativa est, prae altero eligere soleo, quia cum natura similium magis convenit.
As for your further question, whether therefore as many worlds must be constituted as there are attributes, see the scholium to Prop. 7, Part II of the Ethics.2 This proposition could, moreover, be demonstrated more easily by reducing the matter to absurdity; which kind of demonstration, when the proposition is negative, I usually choose in preference to the other, because it agrees more with the nature of such things.
  1. 2 That a mode of extension and the idea of that mode are one and the same thing, expressed in two ways, and that whether nature is conceived under one attribute or another, one and the same order, or connection of causes, is found.
Sed quia positivum tantummodo petis, ad alterum transeo, quod est, an id possit ab alio produci, in quo tam essentia, quam existentia discrepat: nam quae ab invicem ita differunt nihil commune habere videntur. Sed cum omnia singularia, praeter illa, quae a suis similibus producuntur, differant a suis causis, tam essentia, quam existentia, nullam hic dubitandi rationem video.
But since you ask only for a positive one, I pass to the other question, which is whether a thing can be produced by another from which it differs both in essence and in existence: for things which differ from one another in this way seem to have nothing in common. But since all singular things, except those produced by their like, differ from their causes both in essence and in existence, I see no reason for doubt here.
[G IV 278] Quo autem ego sensu intelligam, quod Deus sit causa efficiens rerum, tam essentiae, quam existentiae, credo me satis explicuisse in schol. et coroll. Prop. 25. p. 1. Ethic.
In what sense I understand that God is the efficient cause of things, both of their essence and of their existence, I think I have explained sufficiently in the scholium and corollary of Prop. 25, Part I of the Ethics.
Axioma scholii prop. 10. p. 1. ut in fine eiusd. Scholii innui, formamus ex idea, quam habemus entis absolute infiniti, et non ex eo, quod dentur, aut possint dari entia, quae tria, quatuor, etc. attributa habeant.
The axiom of the scholium to Prop. 10, Part I we form, as I indicated at the end of that scholium, from the idea which we have of a being absolutely infinite, and not from there being, or being able to be, beings which have three, four, etc. attributes.
Denique exempla, quae petis, primi generis sunt in cogitatione, intellectus absolute infinitus; in extensione autem motus et quies; secundi autem, facies totius universi, quae quamvis infinitis modis variet, manet tamen semper eadem, de quo vide schol. 7. Lemmatis ante prop. 14. p. 2.
Finally, the examples you ask for: of the first kind are,1 in thought, absolutely infinite intellect; in extension, motion and rest; of the second, the face of the whole universe, which, although it varies in infinite ways, nevertheless remains always the same;2 on which see the scholium to Lemma 7, before Prop. 14, Part II.
  1. 1 The Ethics describes the infinite modes of Part I, Props. 21–23 without naming any: those that follow from the absolute nature of an attribute (the first kind) and those that follow from an attribute modified by such a mode (the second). This letter supplies the examples.
  2. 2 Compare Part II, Lemma 7, Schol.: the whole of nature is one individual, whose parts, that is, all bodies, vary in infinite ways without any change of the whole individual.
His, vir praestantissime, ad tuas, nostrique amici obiectiones respondisse me credo, si tamen scrupulum adhuc remanere existimas, rogo, ut mihi significare non graveris, ut eum etiam, si possim, evellam. Vale, etc.
With these I think, most excellent Sir, I have answered the objections of yourself and our friend; if, however, you think some difficulty still remains, I beg you not to hesitate to tell me of it, so that I may remove it too, if I can. Farewell, etc.
Hagae Com. 29 Iulii, 1675.
The Hague, 29 July 1675.

Epistola LXV

Letter 65

Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus to Spinoza

Acutissimo, ac Doctissimo philosopho,
To the most acute and most learned philosopher
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus.
from Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Vir clarissime,
Most distinguished Sir,
[G IV 279] Abs te peto demonstrationema eius, quod dicis; nempe quod anima non possit plura attributa Dei, quam extensionem, et cogitationem percipere. Quod quidem licet evidenter videam, contrarium tamen ex schol prop. 7. part. 2. Ethices posse deduci, mihi videtur, forte non aliam ob causam, quam quia sensum huius scholii non satis recte percipio. Constitui ergo, haec qua ratione deducam, exponere, te, vir Cl. obnixe rogans, ut mihi velis, ubicunque sensum tuum non recte assequor, solita tua humanitate sucurrere. Ea autem sic sese habent.
I ask you for a demonstration of what you say, namely that the soul cannot perceive more attributes of God than extension and thought. Although I see this evidently, it nevertheless seems to me that the contrary can be deduced from the scholium to Prop. 7, Part II of the Ethics, perhaps for no other reason than that I do not grasp the sense of this scholium rightly enough. I have decided, therefore, to set out how I deduce it, earnestly asking you, distinguished Sir, to be good enough to help me with your usual kindness wherever I do not follow your meaning rightly. The matter stands as follows.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 593–594, Letter 67), which prints the letter without the writer’s name, as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 279).
Quod, licet inde colligam, mundum utique unicum esse, id tamen exinde non minus quoque clarum est, eum ipsum infinitis modis expressum; ac proinde unamquamque rem singularem infinitis modis expressam esse. Unde videtur sequi, quod modificatio illa, quae mentem meam constituit; ac modificatio illa, quae corpus meum exprimit, licet una, et eadem sit modificatio, ea tamen infinitis modis sit expressa, uno modo per cogitationem, altero per extensionem, tertio per attributum Dei mihi incognitum, atque sic porro in infinitum, quia infinita dantur attributa Dei, et ordo, et connexio modificationum videtur esse eadem in omnibus.
Although I gather from it that the world is certainly one, it is nevertheless no less clear from it that it is expressed in infinite ways, and so that each singular thing is expressed in infinite ways. From this it seems to follow that the modification which constitutes my mind and the modification which expresses my body, although it is one and the same modification, is nevertheless expressed in infinite ways: in one way through thought, in another through extension, in a third through an attribute of God unknown to me, and so on to infinity, since there are infinite attributes of God, and the order and connection of the modifications seems to be the same in all.
Hinc iam quaestio oritur, quare mens, quae certam modificationem repraesentat, et quae eadem modificatio non solum extensione, sed infinitis aliis modis est expressa; quare, inquam, tantum modificationem illam per extensionem expressam, hoc est, corpus humanum, et nullam aliam expressionem per alia attributa percipiat. Sed tempus mihi non permittit, ut ea prolixius prosequar, forte haec dubia omnia crebrioribus meditationibus eximentur.
Hence the question now arises why the mind, which represents a certain modification, and which same modification is expressed not only in extension but in infinite other ways, why, I say, it perceives only that modification expressed through extension, that is, the human body, and no other expression through the other attributes.1 But time does not allow me to pursue these things at greater length; perhaps all these doubts will be removed by more frequent meditation.
  1. 1 Spinoza’s answer is Letter 66; Appendix C discusses the question and the answer.
Londini, 12. August. 1675.
London, 12 August 1675.

Epistola LXVI

Letter 66

Spinoza to Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus

Nobilissimo, ac Doctissimo viro,
To the most noble and most learned man
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus
Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Nobilissime vir,
Most noble Sir,
[G IV 280] Caeterum, ad tuam obiectionema ut respondeam, dico, quod quamvis unaquaeque res infinitis modis expressa sit in infinito Dei intellectu, illae tamen infinitae ideae, quibus exprimitur, unam eandemque rei singularis mentem constituere nequeunt; sed infinitas: quandoquidem unaquaeque harum infinitarum idearum nullam connexionem cum invicem habent, ut in eodem scholio propositionis 7. Part. 2. Ethic. explicui, et ex prop. 10. Part. 1. patet. Ad haec si aliquantulum attendas, nihil difficultatis superesse videbis, etc.
But, to reply to your objection, I say that although each thing is expressed in infinite ways in the infinite intellect of God, those infinite ideas by which it is expressed cannot constitute one and the same mind of a singular thing, but infinite minds; since each of these infinite ideas has no connection with the others,1 as I explained in the same scholium to Prop. 7, Part II of the Ethics, and as is evident from Prop. 10, Part I. If you attend to these things a little, you will see that no difficulty remains, etc.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP p. 594, Letter 68), which prints the letter without the addressee’s name, as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 280). Its opening Caeterum (‘But’, ‘For the rest’) suggests that only the end of the letter was printed.
  1. 1 Each attribute must be conceived through itself (Part I, Prop. 10), and the modes of each attribute have God for their cause only insofar as he is considered under the attribute of which they are modes (Part II, Prop. 6); so the idea of a mode of one attribute can neither involve nor be connected with the idea of a mode of another.
Hagae 18 August. 1675.
The Hague, 18 August 1675.

Epistola LXXIII

Letter 73

Spinoza to Henry Oldenburg

Viro Nobilissimo, ac Doctissimo,
To the most noble and most learned man
Henrico Oldenburgio
Henry Oldenburg
Nobilissime Domine,
Most noble Sir,
[G IV 306] Perbreves tuas literas,a 15. Nov. ad me datas, die Saturni elapsa accepi: in iis ea tantummodo indicas, quae in tractatu theologico-Politico crucem lectoribus fixere. Cum tamen ex iis etiam cognoscere speraverim, quaenam eae opiniones essent, quae religiosae virtutis praxin labefactare viderentur, de quibus antea monueras.
Your very short letter, sent to me on 15 November,1 I received last Saturday: in it you indicate only those things in the Theological-Political Treatise which have been a torment to its readers. Though I had hoped also to learn from it what those opinions were which seemed to undermine the practice of religious virtue, of which you had warned me before.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 449–451, Letter 21), where the letter carries no date. Gebhardt prints after it a copy made for Leibniz (G IV 306–309), whose readings include materiam incorpoream for materiam corpoream, praecipue agnoscere for praecipuam agnoscere, and in mente Christi Jesu (‘in the mind of Christ Jesus’) for in Christo Iesu.
  1. 1 Letter 71 (G IV 304), not included here. Spinoza had asked in Letter 68 (G IV 299) which doctrines Oldenburg thought would undermine the practice of religious virtue, and which passages of the Treatise had troubled learned men, since he wished to explain the Treatise with notes; he had gone to Amsterdam to have a book printed and had put the printing off because of a rumour that in it he tried to show that there is no God. Oldenburg answered in a few lines that the troubling passages were those that seemed to treat God and nature ambiguously, so that many thought he confused the two; those that seemed to take away the authority of miracles, by which alone almost all Christians are persuaded that the certainty of revelation can be established; and his silence about Jesus Christ, his incarnation and his satisfaction. These are the ‘three heads’ answered here.
Sed, ut de tribus illis capitibus, quae notas, mentem meam tibi aperiam, dico, et quidem ad primum, me de Deo, et natura sententiam fovere longe diversam ab ea, quam Neoterici Christiani defendere solent. Deum enim rerum omnium causam immanentem, ut aiunt, non vero transeuntem statuo.
But to open my mind to you on those three heads which you note, I say, and indeed as to the first, that I hold an opinion of God and nature far different from the one which the modern Christians usually defend. For I hold that God is the immanent cause, as they say, of all things, and not the transitive cause.2
  1. 2 Part I, Prop. 18: ‘God is the immanent, not the transitive, cause of all things’.
Omnia, inquam, in Deo esse, et in Deo moveri cum Paulo affirmo, et forte etiam cum omnibus antiquis philosophis, licet alio modo; et auderem etiam dicere, cum antiquis omnibus Hebraeis, quantum ex quibusdam traditionibus, tametsi multis modis adulteratis, coniicere licet. Attamen quod quidam putant, tractatum theologico-Politicum eo niti, quod Deus, et natura (per quam massam quandam, sive materiam corpoream intelligunt) unum, et idem sint, tota errant via.
All things, I say, are in God and move in God: this I affirm with Paul,3 and perhaps also with all the ancient philosophers, though in another way; and I would even dare to say, with all the ancient Hebrews, so far as may be conjectured from certain traditions, even though these have been corrupted in many ways. Yet those who think that the Theological-Political Treatise rests on this, that God and nature (by which they understand a certain mass, or corporeal matter)4 are one and the same, are entirely mistaken.
  1. 3 Paul at Athens, Acts 17:28: In ipso enim vivimus, et movemur, et sumus (‘For in him we live, and move, and are’). Compare Part I, Prop. 15: ‘Whatever is, is in God’.
  2. 4 In the Ethics extension is an attribute of God (Part II, Prop. 2), but corporeal substance ‘can be conceived only as infinite, only as unique and only as indivisible’ (Part I, Prop. 15, Schol.), not as a mass made up of parts.
Ad miracula deinde quod attinet, mihi contra persuasum est, divinae revelationis certitudinem sola doctrinae sapientia, non autem miraculis, hoc est, ignorantia adstrui posse, quod satis prolixe cap. VI. de miraculis ostendi.
Next, as for miracles, I am on the contrary persuaded that the certainty of divine revelation can be built up only on the wisdom of its teaching, and not on miracles, that is, on ignorance; which I have shown at some length in chapter VI, on miracles.5
  1. 5 Theological-Political Treatise, ch. 6, ‘De Miraculis’ (G III 81 ff.).
Hoc tantum hic addo, me inter religionem, et superstitionem hanc praecipuam agnoscere differentiam, quod haec ignorantiam, illa autem sapientiam pro fundamento habeat, et hanc causam esse credo, cur Christiani non fide, neque charitate, neque reliquis spiritus Sancti fructibus; sed sola opinione inter reliquos dignoscuntur; nempe, quia, ut omnes, solis miraculis, hoc est ignorantia, quae omnis malitiae fons est, se defendunt; atque adeo fidem, licet veram, in superstitionem vertunt. Verum an huic malo remedium adhibere reges unquam concedent, valde dubito.
I add only this here, that I recognize this chief difference between religion and superstition: that the latter has ignorance for its foundation, the former wisdom; and I believe that this is the reason why Christians are distinguished from others not by faith, nor by charity, nor by the other fruits of the Holy Spirit,6 but by opinion alone; namely, because, like everyone, they defend themselves by miracles alone, that is, by ignorance,7 which is the source of all wickedness; and so they turn faith, though true, into superstition. But whether kings will ever allow a remedy to be applied to this evil, I very much doubt.
  1. 6 Galatians 5:22: Fructus autem Spiritus est caritas, gaudium, pax … (‘But the fruit of the Spirit is charity, joy, peace …’).
  2. 7 Compare the Appendix to Part I, on those who flee at last to the will of God, ‘that is, the asylum of ignorance’ (Part I, Appendix).
Denique, ut de tertio etiam capite mentem meam clarius aperiam, dico ad salutem non esse omnino necesse, Christum secundum carnem noscere; sed de aeterno illo filio Dei, hoc est, Dei aeterna sapientia, quae sese in omnibus rebus, et maxime in mente humana, et omnium maxime in Christo Iesu manifestavit, longe aliter sentiendum.
Finally, to open my mind more clearly on the third head too, I say that for salvation it is not altogether necessary to know Christ according to the flesh;8 but of that eternal son of God, that is, the eternal wisdom of God, which has manifested itself in all things, and chiefly in the human mind, and most of all in Christ Jesus,9 one must think quite otherwise.
  1. 8 II Corinthians 5:16: Et si cognovimus secundum carnem Christum, sed nunc jam non novimus (‘And if we have known Christ according to the flesh, yet now we know him so no longer’).
  2. 9 Compare the Theological-Political Treatise, ch. 1 (G III 21): God revealed himself to the Apostles through the mind of Christ, and ‘in this sense we can also say that the Wisdom of God, that is, a wisdom which is above the human, took on human nature in Christ’; and Part IV, Prop. 68, Schol.: ‘the spirit of Christ, that is, the idea of God’.
Nam nemo absque hac ad statum beatitudinis potest pervenire, utpote quae sola docet, quid verum et falsum, bonum et malum sit. Et quia, uti dixi, haec sapientia per Iesum Christum maxime manifestata fuit, ideo ipsius discipuli eandem, quatenus ab ipso ipsis fuit revelata, praedicaverunt, seseque spiritu illo Christi supra reliquos gloriari posse ostenderunt.
For no one can attain to the state of blessedness without this, since it alone teaches what is true and false, good and evil. And because, as I said, this wisdom was manifested most of all through Jesus Christ, his disciples preached it, insofar as it was revealed to them by him, and showed that they could glory in that spirit of Christ above others.
Caeterum quod quaedam Ecclesiae his addunt, quod Deus naturam humanam assumpserit, monui expresse, me, quid dicant, nescire; imo, ut verum fatear, non minus absurde mihi loqui videntur, quam si quis mihi diceret, quod circulus naturam quadrati induerit.
As for what certain Churches add to these things, that God took on human nature, I have expressly pointed out that I do not know what they say;10 indeed, to confess the truth, they seem to me to speak no less absurdly than if someone were to tell me that a circle had put on the nature of a square.11
  1. 10 Theological-Political Treatise, ch. 1 (G III 21): ‘I must point out here that I am not speaking at all of the things which certain Churches maintain about Christ, nor denying them; for I gladly confess that I do not grasp them’.
  2. 11 The same image of an absurdity in Part I, Prop. 15, Schol.: ‘as if someone, from having feigned that a circle has the properties of a square, concluded that a circle does not have a centre’.
Atque haec sufficere arbitror ad explicandum, quid de tribus illis capitibus sentiam. An eadem Christianis, quos nosti, placitura sint, id tu melius scire poteris. Vale.
And this, I think, is enough to explain what I think about those three heads. Whether the same will please the Christians you know, you will be able to know better. Farewell.

Epistola LXXIV

Letter 74

Henry Oldenburg to Spinoza

Clarissimo, Doctissimoque viro,
To the most distinguished and most learned man
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Henr. Oldenburgius
from Henry Oldenburg
S. P.
Greetings.
[G IV 309] Quandoquidem accusare me viderisa nimiae brevitatis, culpam illam hac vice nimia prolixitate eluam. Exspectaveras, ut video, earum in scriptis tuis opinionum enarrationem, quae religiosae virtutis praxin convellere Lectoribus tuis videantur. Dicam quid sit rei, quod potissimum eos excruciet.
Since you seem to accuse me of excessive brevity, I shall wash that fault away this time with excessive prolixity. You had expected, as I see, an account of those opinions in your writings which seem to your readers to overturn the practice of religious virtue. I shall tell you what the matter is that chiefly torments them.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 451–452, Letter 22), as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 309–311). The edition prints the two Greek words in Greek; the digital text of Gebhardt used here transliterates them, and they are restored from the 1677 page.
Fatalem videris rerum, et actionum omnium necessitatem adstruere: atqui illa concessa, assertaque, legum omnium, omnis virtutis, et religionis incidi nervos, omnesque remunerationes, et poenas inanes esse, autumant. Quicquid cogit, vel necessitatem infert, excusare iidem arbitrantur; proindeque neminem inexcusabilem in Dei conspectu fore censent. Si fatis agamur, duraque revoluta manu omnia certo, et inevitabili tramite vadunt, quis culpae, poenarumque sit locus, illi equidem non assequuntur.
You seem to establish a fatal necessity of all things and actions;1 but if this is granted and asserted, they declare, the sinews of all laws, of all virtue and religion are cut, and all rewards and punishments are empty. Whatever compels, or brings necessity with it, they think excuses; and so they consider that no one will be inexcusable in the sight of God. If we are driven by the fates, and all things, unwound by a hard hand, go along a certain and inevitable path, they for their part cannot see what place there is for blame and punishment.
  1. 1 Spinoza answers in Letter 75. In the Ethics, see Part I, Prop. 29 and Prop. 33, with its scholia, and the uses of the doctrine at the end of Part II, Prop. 49, Schol..
Quis huic nodo adhiberi possit cuneus, perquam ardua res dictu est. Tu quid opis hanc in rem suppeditare posses, scire et discere pervelim.
What wedge can be applied to this knot is a very hard thing to say. What help you could supply in this matter I should very much like to know and to learn.
[G IV 310] Ad sententiam illam tuam, quam de tribus capitibus, a me notatis, aperire mihi dignaris, haec inquirenda subeunt. Primo, quonam sensu miracula, et ignorantiam pro Synonymis, et aequipollentibus habeas, ut in Novissimis tuis sentire videris; cum Lazari a mortuis resuscitatio, et Iesu Christi a morte resurrectio omnem naturae creatae vim superare, et soli potentiae divinae competere videatur; neque id ignorantiam culpabilem arguat, quod intelligentiae finitae, certisque repagulis constrictae limites excedat necessum est.
On that opinion of yours which you deign to open to me on the three heads I noted, these questions occur to me. First, in what sense you hold miracles and ignorance to be synonyms and equivalents, as you seem to think in your last letter;1 since the raising of Lazarus from the dead2 and the resurrection of Jesus Christ from death seem to surpass all the power of created nature and to belong to the divine power alone; nor does that which must exceed the limits of a finite intelligence, confined within fixed bounds, argue a culpable ignorance.
  1. 1 Letter 73.
  2. 2 John 11:43–44.
An non convenire censes creatae menti et scientiae, increatae mentis, ac supremi Numinis talem scientiam, potentiamque agnoscere, quae penetrare, ac praestare ea possit, quorum ratio, ac modus a nobis homuncionibus reddi, et explicari nequeat? Homines sumus, humani nihil a nobis alienum ducendum videtur.
Do you not think it fitting for a created mind and knowledge to acknowledge in the uncreated mind and supreme Deity such knowledge and power as can penetrate and accomplish things whose reason and manner cannot be rendered and explained by us little men? We are men; nothing human, it seems, should be thought foreign to us.3
  1. 3 Terence, Heauton Timorumenos 77: Homo sum: humani nihil a me alienum puto (‘I am a man; I count nothing human foreign to me’).
Deinde, cum capere te nequire fatearis, Deum revera naturam humanam assumpsisse, quaerere ex te fas sit, quomodo illa Euangelii nostri, et epistolae ad Hebraeos scriptae locos intelligas, quorum prior affirmat, verbum carnem factum esse; posterior, Filium Dei non Angelos, sed semen Abrahae assumpsisse.
Next, since you confess that you cannot grasp that God really took on human nature, may I ask you how you understand those passages of our Gospel and of the Epistle written to the Hebrews, of which the former affirms that the Word was made flesh,4 the latter that the Son of God took on not angels but the seed of Abraham?5
  1. 4 John 1:14: Καὶ ὁ λόγος σὰρξ ἐγένετο (‘And the Word became flesh’); Vulgate Et Verbum caro factum est.
  2. 5 Hebrews 2:16: οὐ γὰρ δήπου ἀγγέλων ἐπιλαμβάνεται, ἀλλὰ σπέρματος Ἀβραὰμ ἐπιλαμβάνεται (‘For surely he does not take hold of angels, but he takes hold of the seed of Abraham’); Vulgate Nusquam enim angelos apprehendit, sed semen Abrahae apprehendit.
Et totius Euangelii tenorem id inferre putem Filium Dei unigenitum λόγον, (qui et Deus, et apud Deum erat) in natura humana se ostendisse, et pro nobis peccatoribus ἀντίλυτρον, redemptionis precium, passione, et morte sua exsolvisse. Quid de his, et similibus dicendum, ut sua constet Euangelio, et Christianae religioni, cui te favere opinor, veritas, lubens edoceri vellem.
And I should think that the tenor of the whole Gospel implies that the only-begotten Son of God, the λόγον (‘Word’), who was both God and with God,6 showed himself in human nature, and by his passion and death paid for us sinners the ἀντίλυτρον (‘ransom’),7 the price of redemption. What should be said about these and similar things, so that the Gospel and the Christian religion, which I believe you favour, may keep their truth, I should gladly be taught.
  1. 6 John 1:1: καὶ ὁ λόγος ἦν πρὸς τὸν θεόν, καὶ θεὸς ἦν ὁ λόγος (‘and the Word was with God, and the Word was God’). Oldenburg writes λόγον in the accusative, as the grammar of his Latin sentence requires.
  2. 7 The word of I Timothy 2:6: ὁ δοὺς ἑαυτὸν ἀντίλυτρον ὑπὲρ πάντων (‘who gave himself a ransom for all’); Vulgate qui dedit redemptionem semetipsum pro omnibus.
Plura scribere statueram, sed interpellant amici invisentes, quibus negare humanitatis officia nefas duco. Sed et haec, quae congessi hac epistola, suffecerint, et fortasse taedium tibi philosophanti creaverint. Vale igitur, et me iugem Eruditionis et scientiae tuae cultorem crede.
I had decided to write more, but friends calling on me interrupt, to whom I hold it wrong to refuse the duties of courtesy. But these things too which I have gathered in this letter will suffice, and will perhaps have wearied you as you philosophize. Farewell, then, and believe me a constant admirer of your learning and knowledge.
[G IV 311] Dab. Londini die 16. Decemb. 1675.
Given at London, 16 December 1675.

Epistola LXXV

Letter 75

Spinoza to Henry Oldenburg

Viro Nobilissimo, ac Doctissimo,
To the most noble and most learned man
Henr. Oldenburgio
Henry Oldenburg
Nobilissime Domine,
Most noble Sir,
[G IV 311] Video tandem,a quid id fuerit, quod a me postulabas ne evulgarem; sed quia id ipsum praecipuum est fundamentum eorum omnium, quae in tractatu, quem edere destinaveram, habentur, volo hic paucis explicare, qua ratione ego fatalem omnium rerum, et actionum necessitatem statuam.
I see at last what it was that you asked me not to make public; but since this very thing is the chief foundation of everything in the treatise which I had intended to publish,1 I wish to explain here briefly in what way I hold the fatal necessity of all things and actions.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 452–455, Letter 23), as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 311–313); the letter carries no date.
  1. 1 The Ethics, whose printing Spinoza had put off in the summer of 1675 (Letter 68, G IV 299; see the note to Letter 73).
Nam Deum nullo modo fato subiicio, sed omnia inevitabili necessitate ex Dei natura sequi concipio eodem modo, ac omnes concipiunt, ex ipsius Dei natura sequi, ut Deus se ipsum intelligat; quod sane nemo negat ex divina natura necessario sequi, et tamen nemo concipit, Deum fato aliquo coactum, sed omnino libere, tametsi necessario se ipsum intelligere.
For I in no way subject God to fate, but I conceive that all things follow with inevitable necessity from the nature of God in the same way that everyone conceives that it follows from the very nature of God that God understands himself; which surely no one denies follows necessarily from the divine nature, and yet no one conceives that God understands himself compelled by some fate, but that he understands himself altogether freely, although necessarily.2
  1. 2 In the Ethics, Part I, Def. 7 (that thing is free which exists from the necessity of its nature alone), Prop. 16 and Prop. 17, Cor. 2, ‘God alone is a free cause’.
[G IV 312] Deinde haec inevitabilis rerum necessitas nec iura divina, nec humana tollit. Nam ipsa moralia documenta, sive formam legis, seu iuris ab ipso Deo accipiant, sive non, divina tamen sunt, et salutaria, et si bonum, quod ex virtute, et amore divino sequitur, a Deo tanquam Iudice accipiamus, vel ex necessitate divinae naturae emanet, non erit propterea magis, aut minus optabile, ut nec contra mala, quae ex pravis actionibus, et affectibus sequuntur, ideo, quia necessario ex iisdem sequuntur, minus timenda sunt, et denique sive ea, quae agimus, necessario, vel contingenter agamus, spe tamen, et metu ducimur.
Next, this inevitable necessity of things takes away neither divine nor human laws. For the moral teachings themselves, whether or not they receive the form of law or right from God himself, are nevertheless divine and salutary; and whether we receive the good which follows from virtue and the divine love from God as a judge, or it emanates from the necessity of the divine nature, it will not on that account be more or less desirable;1 just as, on the other hand, the evils which follow from wicked actions and affects are not less to be feared because they follow from them necessarily; and finally, whether we do what we do necessarily or contingently, we are still led by hope and fear.
  1. 1 Compare Part V, Prop. 41, Schol. and Prop. 42: blessedness is not the reward of virtue but virtue itself.
Porro homines coram Deo nulla alia de causa sunt inexcusabiles, quam quia in ipsius Dei potestate sunt, ut lutum in potestate figuli, qui ex eadem massa vasa facit, alia ad decus, alia ad dedecus.
Further, men are inexcusable before God for no other reason than that they are in the power of God himself, as clay is in the power of the potter, who from the same lump makes vessels, some for honour, others for dishonour.1
  1. 1 Romans 9:21: an non habet potestatem figulus luti ex eadem massa facere aliud quidem vas in honorem, aliud vero in contumeliam? (‘Has not the potter power over the clay, from the same lump to make one vessel for honour and another for dishonour?’).
Ad haec pauca si attendere velis aliquantulum, non dubito, quin facili negotio ad omnia argumenta, quae in hanc sententiam obiici solent, respondere possis, ut multi iam mecum experti sunt.
If you will attend a little to these few points, I do not doubt that you will easily be able to answer all the arguments that are usually raised against this opinion, as many have already found with me.
[G IV 313] Miracula, et ignorantiam pro aequipollentibus sumpsi, quia ii, qui Dei existentiam, et religionem miraculis adstruere conantur, rem obscuram per aliam magis obscuram, et quam maxime ignorant, ostendere volunt, atque ita novum argumentandi genus adferunt, redigendo scilicet non ad impossibile, ut aiunt, sed ignorantiam. Caeterum meam de miraculis sententiam satis, ni fallor, explicui in tractatu theologico-Politico.
I took miracles and ignorance as equivalents because those who try to establish God’s existence and religion by miracles want to show something obscure through something else more obscure, of which they are most ignorant, and so bring in a new kind of argument, reducing, namely, not to the impossible, as they say, but to ignorance.1 For the rest, I have explained my view of miracles sufficiently, if I am not mistaken, in the Theological-Political Treatise.
  1. 1 The phrase of the Appendix to Part I: ‘a new way of arguing … namely, reduction not to the impossible, but to ignorance’.
Hoc tantum hic addo, quod si ad haec attendas, quod scilicet Christus non Senatui, nec Pilato, nec cuiquam infidelium; sed sanctis tantummodo apparuerit, et quod Deus neque dextram, neque sinistram habeat, nec in loco; sed ubique secundum essentiam sit, et quod materia ubique sit eadem, et quod Deus extra Mundum in spatio, quod fingunt, imaginario, sese non manifestet, et quod denique corporis humani compages intra debitos limites solo aeris pondere coerceatur, facile videbis, hanc Christi apparitionem non absimilem esse illi, qua Deus Abrahamo apparuit, quando tres vidit homines, quos ad secum prandendum invitavit.
I add only this here: that if you attend to these things, namely that Christ appeared not to the Senate, nor to Pilate, nor to any of the unbelievers, but only to the saints;2 and that God has neither right hand nor left,3 and is not in a place, but is everywhere according to his essence; and that matter is everywhere the same; and that God does not manifest himself outside the world, in the imaginary space which they feign; and finally that the frame of the human body is held within its due limits by the weight of the air alone: you will easily see that this appearance of Christ is not unlike the one in which God appeared to Abraham, when he saw three men whom he invited to dine with him.4
  1. 2 Compare Acts 10:40–41: God raised him on the third day and made him manifest, non omni populo, sed testibus praeordinatis a Deo (‘not to all the people, but to witnesses chosen beforehand by God’).
  2. 3 Mark 16:19: assumptus est in caelum, et sedet a dextris Dei (‘he was taken up into heaven, and sits at the right hand of God’).
  3. 4 Genesis 18:1–8.
At dices, Apostolos omnes omnino credidisse, quod Christus a morte resurrexerit, et ad coelum revera ascenderit: quod ego non nego. Nam ipse etiam Abrahamus credidit, quod Deus apud ipsum pransus fuerit, et omnes Israelitae, quod Deus e coelo igne circumdatus ad montem Sinai descenderit, et cum iis immediate locutus fuerit, cum tamen haec, et plura alia huiusmodi apparitiones, seu revelationes fuerint, captui, et opinionibus eorum hominum accommodatae, quibus Deus mentem suam iisdem revelare voluit.
But you will say that the Apostles all believed absolutely that Christ rose from death and really ascended to heaven: which I do not deny. For Abraham himself also believed that God had dined with him, and all the Israelites that God had descended from heaven surrounded by fire onto Mount Sinai and had spoken with them directly;5 whereas these, and many other things of this kind, were appearances or revelations accommodated to the understanding and opinions of those men to whom God willed to reveal his mind through them.
  1. 5 Exodus 19:18, descendisset Dominus super eum in igne (‘the Lord had descended upon it in fire’); Deuteronomy 5:4, Facie ad faciem locutus est nobis in monte de medio ignis (‘He spoke to us face to face on the mountain from the midst of the fire’).
Concludo itaque Christi a mortuis resurrectionem revera spiritualem, et solis fidelibus ad eorum captum revelatam fuisse, nempe quod Christus aeternitate donatus fuit, et a mortuis, (mortuos hic intelligo eo sensu, quo Christus dixit: sinite mortuos mortuos suos sepelire) surrexit, simulatque vita et morte singularis sanctitatis exemplum dedit, et eatenus discipulos suos a mortuis suscitat, quatenus ipsi hoc vitae eius, et mortis exemplum sequuntur.
I conclude, therefore, that Christ’s resurrection from the dead was in truth spiritual, and revealed only to the faithful according to their understanding; namely, that Christ was endowed with eternity and rose from the dead (I understand ‘the dead’ here in the sense in which Christ said: let the dead bury their dead6) as soon as he gave, by his life and death, an example of singular holiness; and that he raises his disciples from the dead insofar as they themselves follow this example of his life and death.
  1. 6 Matthew 8:22, dimitte mortuos sepelire mortuos suos; Luke 9:60.
Nec difficile esset totam Euangelii doctrinam secundum hanc hypothesin explicare. Imo caput 15. Ep. 1. ad Corinthios ex sola hac hypothesi explicari potest, et Pauli argumenta intelligi, cum alias communem hypothesin sequendo infirma appareant, et facili negotio refelli possint, ut iam taceam, quod Christiani omnia, quae Iudaei carnaliter, Spiritualiter interpretati sunt.
Nor would it be difficult to explain the whole teaching of the Gospel according to this hypothesis. Indeed, chapter 15 of the First Epistle to the Corinthians7 can be explained by this hypothesis alone, and Paul’s arguments understood, whereas if the common hypothesis is followed they appear weak and can easily be refuted; to say nothing of the fact that Christians have interpreted spiritually everything that the Jews interpreted carnally.
  1. 7 Paul’s chapter on the resurrection; for example I Corinthians 15:44, seminatur corpus animale, surget corpus spiritale (‘it is sown a natural body, it shall rise a spiritual body’), and 15:50, ‘flesh and blood cannot possess the kingdom of God’.
Humanam imbecillitatem tecum agnosco. Sed te contra rogare mihi liceat, an nos homunciones tantam naturae cognitionem habeamus, ut determinare possimus, quousque eius vis, et potentia se extendit, et quid eius vim superat? quod quia nemo sine arrogantia praesumere potest, licet ergo absque iactantia miracula per causas naturales, quantum fieri potest, explicare, et quae explicare non possumus, nec etiam demonstrare, quod absurda sint, satius erit iudicium de iis suspendere, et religionem, uti dixi, sola doctrinae sapientia adstruere.
I acknowledge human weakness with you.8 But may I in turn ask you whether we little men have such knowledge of nature that we can determine how far its force and power extend, and what surpasses its force? Since no one can presume this without arrogance, it is permissible, then, without boasting, to explain miracles by natural causes as far as possible; and as for those which we cannot explain, nor yet demonstrate to be absurd, it will be better to suspend judgment about them, and to establish religion, as I said, on the wisdom of its teaching alone.9
  1. 8 Answering Oldenburg’s ‘We are men’ in Letter 74.
  2. 9 Letter 73.
Loca denique Euangelii Iohannis et epistolae ad Hebraeos iis, quae dixi, repugnare credis, quia linguarum orientalium phrases Europaeis loquendi modis metiris, et quamvis Iohannes suum Euangelium Graece scripserit, hebraizat tamen.
Finally, you believe that the passages of the Gospel of John and of the Epistle to the Hebrews conflict with what I have said, because you measure the phrases of Oriental languages by European ways of speaking; and although John wrote his Gospel in Greek, he nevertheless Hebraizes.
Quicquid sit, an credis, quando scriptura ait, quod Deus in Nube sese manifestaverit, aut quod in Tabernaculo, et in Templo habitaverit, quod ipse Deus naturam Nubis, Tabernaculi, et templi assumpserit? atqui hoc summum est, quod Christus de se ipso dixit, se scilicet templum Dei esse, nimirum quia, ut in meis praecedentibus dixi, Deus sese maxime in Christo manifestavit, quod Iohannes ut efficacius exprimeret, dixit verbum factum esse carnem. Sed de his satis.
However that may be, do you believe, when Scripture says that God manifested himself in a cloud,10 or that he dwelt in the Tabernacle and in the Temple, that God himself took on the nature of the cloud, the Tabernacle and the Temple? Yet this is the most that Christ said of himself, namely that he was the temple of God;11 namely because, as I said in my previous letter,12 God manifested himself most of all in Christ; which John, to express it more forcefully, put by saying that the Word was made flesh.13 But enough of these things.
  1. 10 For example Exodus 16:10, et ecce gloria Domini apparuit in nube (‘and behold, the glory of the Lord appeared in a cloud’).
  2. 11 John 2:19 and 2:21: ‘Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up … But he spoke of the temple of his body’.
  3. 12 Letter 73.
  4. 13 John 1:14.

Epistola LXXX

Letter 80

Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus to Spinoza

Acutissimo, atque Doctissimo philosopho,
To the most acute and most learned philosopher
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus.
from Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Vir clarissime,
Most distinguished Sir,
[G IV 331] Primo difficulter admodum concipere queo,a qui a priori corporum existentia demonstretur, quae motus, et figuras habent; cum in extensione, absolute rem considerando, nil tale occurrat.
First, I can only with great difficulty conceive how the existence of bodies which have motion and figure is demonstrated a priori, since in extension, considering the thing absolutely, nothing of the kind is found.1
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP p. 595, Letter 69), which prints the letter without the writer’s name, as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 331).
  1. 1 Spinoza’s answer to this question is in Letter 81, and more fully in Letter 83.
Secundo erudiri abs te vellem, quomodo intelligendum sit, cuius meministi epistola de infinito his verbis. Nec tamen concludunt talia omnem numerum superare ex partium multitudine.
Second, I should like to be taught by you how what you mention in the letter on the infinite is to be understood, in these words: ‘Yet they do not conclude that such things surpass every number from the multitude of their parts.’
Nam revera omnes mathematici mihi videntur circa talia infinita semper demonstrare, quod partium numerus sit tam magnus, ut omnem assignabilem numerum superent, et in exemplo, de duobus circulis ibidem allato, non hoc ipsum declarare videris, quod tamen susceperas. Ibi enim tantum ostendis, quod non hoc ipsum concludunt ex nimia spatii interpositi magnitudine, et quod eius maximum et minimum non habeamus; sed non demonstras prout volebas, quod id non concludant ex partium multitudine.b
For in truth all mathematicians seem to me always to demonstrate, concerning such infinites, that the number of parts is so great that it surpasses every assignable number; and in the example of the two circles given there you do not seem to show this very thing, which you had undertaken to do. For there you show only that they do not conclude this from the excessive size of the interposed space, and from our not having its maximum and minimum; but you do not demonstrate, as you wished, that they do not conclude it from the multitude of the parts.
  1. b The Dutch version adds (G IV 331): ‘Further, I have heard from Mr Leibniz that the tutor of the Dauphin of France, called Huet, a man of outstanding learning, is going to write on the truth of human religion, and to refute your Theological-Political Treatise. Farewell.’ This is the book Spinoza asks about in Letter 83.
2. Maii, 1676.
2 May 1676.

Epistola LXXXI

Letter 81

Spinoza to Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus

Nobilissimo, atque Doctissimo viro,
To the most noble and most learned man
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus
Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Nobilissime vir,
Most noble Sir,
[G IV 332] Quod in epistola de infinito dixi,a quod partium infinitatem ex earum multitudine non concludant, hinc patet, quod, si ea ex earum multitudine concluderetur, non possemus maiorem partium multitudinem concipere; sed earum multitudo, quavis data, deberet esse maior, quod falsum est: nam in toto spatio, inter duos circulos, diversa centra habentes, duplo maiorem partium multitudinem concipimus, quam in eiusdem dimidio, et tamen partium numerus, tam dimidii, quam totius spatii omni assignabili numero maior est.
What I said in the letter on the infinite, that they do not conclude the infinity of the parts from their multitude, is evident from this: that if it were concluded from their multitude, we could not conceive a greater multitude of parts, but their multitude would have to be greater than any given one; which is false. For in the whole space between two circles having different centres we conceive a multitude of parts twice as great as in half of it, and yet the number of the parts, both of the half and of the whole space, is greater than any assignable number.1
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP p. 596, Letter 70), which prints the letter without the addressee’s name, as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 332).
  1. 1 The third of the distinctions of Letter 12: things which cannot be equalled by any number, yet can be conceived as greater and less.
Porro ex extensione, ut eam Cartesius concipit, molem scilicet quiescentem, corporum existentiam demonstrare non tantum difficile; ut ais; sed omnino impossibile est. Materia enim quiescens, quantum in se est, in sua quiete perseverabit, nec ad motum concitabitur, nisi a causa potentiori externa; et hac de causa non dubitavi olim affirmare; rerum naturalium principia Cartesiana inutilia esse, ne dicam absurda.
Further, from extension as Descartes conceives it, namely as a mass at rest, it is not only difficult, as you say, but altogether impossible to demonstrate the existence of bodies. For matter at rest, as far as it is in itself,2 will persevere in its rest, and will not be set in motion except by a more powerful external cause; and for this reason I did not hesitate to affirm once that Descartes’s principles of natural things are useless, not to say absurd.
  1. 2 quantum in se est, the phrase of Part III, Prop. 6; compare Part II, Lemma 3, Cor.: a body at rest remains at rest until it is determined to motion by another.
Hagae 5 Maii, 1676
The Hague, 5 May 1676.

Epistola LXXXII

Letter 82

Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus to Spinoza

Acutissimo, Doctissimoque philosopho,
To the most acute and most learned philosopher
B. D. S.
B. D. S.
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus.
from Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Doctissime vir,
Most learned Sir,
[G IV 333] Velim, ut hac in re mihi gratificeris,a indicando, qui ex conceptu extensionis secundum tuas meditationes varietas rerum a priori possit ostendi, quandoquidem meministi opinionis Cartesii, in qua Cartesius statuit, se eam ex extensione nullo alio modo deducere posse, quam supponendo motu a Deo excitato hoc effectum fuisse in extensione: deducit ergo iuxta meam opinionem corporum existentiam non ex quiescente materia, nisi forte suppositionem motoris Dei pro nihilo haberes; quandoquidem, qui illud ex essentia Dei a priori necessario sequi debeat, abs te non sit ostensum; id quod Cartesius ostensurus captum humanum superare credebat.
I should like you to do me the favour, in this matter, of indicating how, according to your meditations, the variety of things can be shown a priori from the concept of extension; since you mentioned the opinion of Descartes, in which Descartes maintains that he can deduce it from extension in no other way than by supposing that this was brought about in extension by motion excited by God. So in my opinion he deduces the existence of bodies not from matter at rest, unless perhaps you count the supposition of God as mover for nothing; since you have not shown how it must follow necessarily, a priori, from the essence of God, something which Descartes believed it beyond human grasp to show.
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP pp. 597–598, Letter 71), which prints the letter without the writer’s name, as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 333–334).
Quare a te hanc rem requiro, sciens bene, te alias cogitationes habere, nisi alia sontica subsit forte causa, quare illud hactenus manifestum facere nolueris; et si hoc, de quo non dubito, non opus fuisset, non tale quid obscure indicares. Sed certo tibi persuasum habeas, quod sive candide mihi aliquid indices, sive celes, meus tamen erga te affectus immutatus maneat.
So I ask this of you, knowing well that you have other thoughts, unless perhaps there is some other serious cause why you have not wished to make it known until now; and if this, which I do not doubt, had not been necessary, you would not hint at any such thing obscurely. But be assured that whether you tell me something candidly or conceal it, my affection towards you will remain unchanged.
Rationes tamen, cur illud specialiter desiderem, hae sunt, quod in mathematicis semper observarim, quod nos ex quavis re in se considerata, hoc est, ex definitione cuiusque rei, unicam saltem proprietatem deducere valeamus; quod si autem plures proprietates desideremus, necesse esse, ut rem definitam ad alia referamus: tunc siquidem ex coniunctione definitionum harum rerum novae proprietates resultant.
The reasons, however, why I particularly desire this are these: that in mathematics I have always observed that from any thing considered in itself, that is, from the definition of any thing, we are able to deduce only a single property; but if we desire more properties, it is necessary to relate the thing defined to other things; for then new properties result from the conjunction of the definitions of these things.
Ex. gr. Si circuli peripheriam considerem solam, nihil aliud concludere potero, quam quod ubique sibi similis, sive uniformis existat, qua quidem proprietate ab omnibus aliis curvis essentialiter differt, nec ullas alias unquam potero deducere.
For example, if I consider the circumference of a circle alone, I shall be able to conclude nothing else than that it is everywhere like itself, or uniform, by which property it differs essentially from all other curves; and I shall never be able to deduce any others.
Verum si ad alia referam, nimirum ad radios ex centro deductos, ad duas lineas sese intersecantes, aut plures quoque, plures utique hinc proprietates deducere valebo; quae quidem aliquo modo videntur adversari prop. 16. Ethices, quae praecipua fere est 1 lib. tui tractatus: In qua tanquam notum assumitur, posse ex data cuiuscunque rei definitione plures proprietates deduci, quod mihi videtur impossibile, si non ad alia referamus rem definitam; et quod porro effecit, ut non possim videre, qua ratione ex attributo aliquo solo considerato, ex. gr. extensione infinita corporum varietas exsurgere possit; vel si existimas hoc quoque non posse concludi ex unico solo considerato; sed omnibus simul sumptis, vellem hoc edoceri abs te, et qua ratione hoc concipiendum foret. Vale etc.
But if I relate it to other things, namely to the radii drawn from the centre, to two lines intersecting each other, or to more, I shall certainly be able to deduce more properties from this; which indeed seems in some way to conflict with Prop. 16 of the Ethics, which is almost the chief proposition of the first book of your treatise. In it it is taken as known that a number of properties can be deduced from the given definition of any thing,1 which seems to me impossible if we do not relate the thing defined to others; and this has further brought it about that I cannot see in what way, from some attribute considered alone, for example infinite extension, the variety of bodies can arise; or, if you think that this too cannot be inferred from one attribute considered alone, but from all taken together, I should like to be taught this by you, and in what way it is to be conceived. Farewell, etc.
  1. 1 The demonstration of Part I, Prop. 16: ‘from the given definition of any thing the intellect infers a number of properties’.
[G IV 334] Parisiis, 23 Iun. 1676.
Paris, 23 June 1676.

Epistola LXXXIII

Letter 83

Spinoza to Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus

Nobilissimo, atque Doctissimo viro,
To the most noble and most learned man
Ehrenfr. Walth. de Tschirnhaus
Ehrenfried Walther von Tschirnhaus
Nobilissime vir,
Most noble Sir,
[G IV 334] Quod petis, an ex solo extensionis conceptua rerum varietas a priori possit demonstrari, credo me iam satis clare ostendisse, id impossibile esse; ideoque materiam a Cartesio male definiri per extensionem; sed eam necessario debere explicari per attributum, quod aeternam, et infinitam essentiam exprimat.
As for your question whether the variety of things can be demonstrated a priori from the concept of extension alone, I believe I have already shown clearly enough that it is impossible; and that therefore matter is badly defined by Descartes through extension, but must necessarily be explained through an attribute which expresses eternal and infinite essence.1
  1. a The text is that of the 1677 edition (OP p. 598, Letter 72), which prints the letter without the addressee’s name, as Gebhardt prints it (G IV 334–335). The Dutch version adds a signature.
  1. 1 The language of Part I, Def. 6; that extension is such an attribute is Part II, Prop. 2.
Sed de his forsan aliquando, si vita suppetit, clarius tecum agam. Nam huc usque nihil de his ordine disponere mihi licuit.
But perhaps some day, if life lasts,2 I shall discuss these things with you more clearly. For up to now it has not been possible for me to set anything out in order on these matters.
  1. 2 Spinoza died at The Hague seven months later, on 21 February 1677 (Israel, Radical Enlightenment, p. 287).
Quod autem addis, nos ex definitione cuiusque rei, in se consideratae, unicam tantum proprietatem deducere valere, locum forsan habet in rebus simplicissimis, vel entibus rationis, (ad quae figuras etiam refero) at non in realibus. Nam ex hoc solo, quod Deum definio esse ens ad cuius essentiam pertinet existentia, plures eius proprietates concludo: nempe quod necessario existit, quod sit unicus, immutabilis, infinitus, etc. et ad hunc modum plura alia exempla adferre possem, quae impraesentiarum omitto.
As for your addition that from the definition of each thing, considered in itself, we can deduce only one property: this perhaps holds in the simplest things, or beings of reason (to which I also refer figures), but not in real things. For from this alone, that I define God to be a being to whose essence existence pertains, I infer a number of his properties: namely that he exists necessarily, that he is unique, immutable, infinite, etc.;3 and in this way I could bring many other examples, which I omit for the present.
  1. 3 In the Ethics: Part I, Prop. 11, Prop. 14, Cor. 1, Prop. 20, Cor. 2 and Prop. 8.
Denique rogo, ut inquiras, an tractatus D. Huet, (nempe contra tractatum theologico-Politicum) de quo antea scripsisti, lucem iam viderit, et an mihi exemplar transmittere poteris, deinde an iam noveris, quaenam ea sint, quae de refractione noviter inventa sint.
Finally, I ask you to find out whether the treatise of Mr Huet4 (namely, against the Theological-Political Treatise), of which you wrote earlier, has yet seen the light, and whether you could send me a copy; and next, whether you already know what the things are that have recently been discovered about refraction.
  1. 4 Pierre-Daniel Huet’s Demonstratio evangelica, which appeared in Paris in 1679 (Israel, pp. 453–455, 731), ‘a work intended to combat incredulity, Naturalism, and Spinozism’ (Israel, p. 647). Tschirnhaus had passed on the news in Letter 80, in a passage found only in the Dutch version.
Hisce vale, Nobilissime vir, et amare perge, etc.
Farewell with these, most noble Sir, and continue to love me, etc.
Hagae Com. 15 Iulii. 1676.
The Hague, 15 July 1676.
